## CLOSED — Settings persistence (iter-930)

- [x] **SP-1 — settings.json was replaced with defaults after a failed load.** `Settings`
  had no `serde(default)`, so a file missing any field failed to load; the next
  read-then-save at twelve call sites wrote defaults over it.
- [x] **SP-2 — an unparseable settings.json was overwritten without a copy.** `save_to`
  now keeps it as `settings.json.bak` first.
- [x] **SP-3 — settings writes were not atomic.** A crash mid-write could truncate the
  file. Saves now write a temp file and rename it.
- [x] **SP-4 — the theme picker reported success after a failed save.** The status line
  now says "not saved".

## CLOSED — Context management: bust permission (plan 06 P1, iter-929)

- [x] **CM-1 — micro-compaction folded at 40% with no bust permission (plan 06 defect 3).**
  Rewrites now run only under a `BustPermission` (`context_management::bust_permission`):
  natural, force at ≥85%, or explicit after `/compact`.
- [x] **CM-2 — micro-compaction stalled after its first fold.** The marker is a user
  message; the next fold landed on it and refused the lone user. `micro_compact` now
  skips markers when it chooses the next exchange.
- [x] **CM-3 — the ladder's decay truncated the current user request.** The newest
  message kept the highest importance, which capped it near 2.2k characters at the
  default H50. The current turn is no longer decayed.
- [x] **CM-4 — no typed failure when the protected head and tail do not fit.**
  `Error::ContextExhausted` at ≥95%; the gateway compacts and retries once.
- [x] **CM-5 — overflow limits were not remembered.** Smallest overflowing size per
  provider and model is stored in the state table and used as the window.

Open under this section: the context engine's `assemble` runs every turn, not gated by
the bust permission; `/ctx-flush` (named in the plan) does not exist.
## CLOSED — Pending tool name printed three times (iter-933)

- [x] **A pending call showed its name three times.** `ToolStart` set the status notice to
  `Queued <tool>…` / `Running <tool>…` (origin: iter-347, when the notice was the only
  indicator). The spinner footer (`running <tool>`) and the transcript row (`○ <tool>`)
  later named the tool too, so the notice repeated it on its own line above the prompt.
  Live capture in tmux before the fix (installed iter-929 binary, mock provider, five
  `aft_read` calls): a frame held 7 `aft_read` lines, including `Queued aft_read…` in the
  footer and above the prompt. The `ToolStart` handler no longer sets the notice. Same
  capture after the fix (debug build): the pending state is the row plus the footer, and no
  frame exceeds 6 `aft_read` lines. Regression:
  `tui::app::tests::tool_start_leaves_the_status_line_to_the_tool_row`.

## CLOSED — Guardrail-skipped tool calls render as success (iter-934)

- [x] **A skipped duplicate call showed ✓.** The identical-call guardrail's skip result reached
  the TUI as a `Done` block, so the row read `✓ aft_read · 57 tok` and said the read ran.
  Fixed on the render side: the adapter recognises a `Done` block whose output starts with
  `tool_guardrails::SKIP_MESSAGE_PREFIX` and shows `⊘ skipped — repeated call not run`; the
  row header draws `⊘` for any `⊘`-prefixed content (this also fixes the cancelled row, which
  drew `✓`). The agent keeps `success: true` for skips on purpose (stream.rs, the degenerate-loop
  circuit counts failures), so the agent side was not changed. Known ceiling: a real output that
  begins with `Guardrail: ` matches too. Live capture (debug build, mock repeating an identical
  call): repeats 1–2 `✓`, repeats 3–5 `⊘`. Regressions:
  `operant_model::adapter::tests::guardrail_skip_is_not_shown_as_a_completed_call` and
  `operant_ui::messages::tests::render_tool_message_marks_a_skipped_call_as_neither_success_nor_failure`.

## CLOSED — Test runs touched the operator's real emergency stop (iter-935)

- [x] **`tests/pause_gate.rs` engaged and cleared the real `~/.operant/data/ESTOP`.** The test
  engaged the stop, and its `disengage()` (called at the start of the clear test, and by a drop
  guard) removed any stop a person had set. Running `cargo test` could therefore resume an agent
  that had been paused. The two tests also shared that one file while running in parallel, so the
  suite failed intermittently (`the model must not be reached while the estop is engaged`, seen on
  the iter-935 workspace run). Fixed with `estop::scoped_root_for_tests`, a test-only seam that points
  the gate, `engage`, `disengage`, and `state` at a temp root; the two tests take a tokio mutex and
  run one at a time. Verified: three consecutive runs of the file pass, and `~/.operant/data/ESTOP` is
  never created.

## OPEN — CLI command-surface residue (2026-10-21)
- [x] **RT-136 (M, SURFACE) — 26 config settings are accepted, defaulted and printed but nothing reads them.** Found by a config-surface audit (every `pub` field of every settings struct in `operant-core/src/config.rs`, counted against all non-test code in `crates/`; `#[cfg(test)]` items are brace-stripped, not truncated — v1 of the script truncated at the first test module and produced two false hits, `feed_seat_map` and `grant_ttl_days`'s sibling fields, which ARE read in gateway_runner.rs). The 24 users of `operant.example.toml` among them are the reason this matters: a key the template advertises and the binary ignores is a promise the operator cannot see broken (`[tui] landing_title` changes nothing; `[tools.tool_search] search_default_limit` is hardcoded as 10/25 at the tool). Full list and disposition below; the settings structs use `deny_unknown_fields`, so DELETING a key is a breaking change for existing configs — the sanctioned pattern is accept + warn (see the `agentmemory_*` shims), not removal.

  | group | fields | disposition |
  |---|---|---|
  | `[tools.stt]` | `groq_model`, `openai_model` | **WIRED iter-1122** — both call sites now resolve through `resolve_model` (per-call, config, default) |
  | `[tools.tool_search]` | `search_default_limit`, `max_search_limit` | hardcoded 10/25 in `ToolSearchTool::execute` — wire or warn |
  | TUI | `show_iterations`, `landing_title`, `prompt_placeholder`, `refresh_rate_ms`, `compact_width`, `medium_width` | the TUI reads six OTHER `[tui]` keys; these six have no reader (the placeholder string does not exist anywhere in the tree) |
  | `[skills]` | `template_name`, `template_description` | no reader — the skill creator does not use them |
  | `[pk]` | `sidecar_script`, `ndjson_socket`, `exec_timeout_secs` (+ the whole `pk` table) | vestigial: the kernel sidecar launches `python -m kernel_sidecar.server` from `KERNEL_SIDECAR_DIR`/`CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR`, and its timeout is `agent.request_timeout_secs` |
  | `[bylaws]` | `grant_ttl_days` | the doc comment calls it "LIVE (F2)"; nothing reads it, and the subsystem it names no longer exists — `org/seat_authority.rs` states "there is no grant ledger anymore — an approved plan IS the grant", so the TTL governs nothing (`has_grant` is structurally false) |
  | `[socialization]` | `turn_budget` | no reader (the agent's turn budget is a different mechanism) |
  | `[behavior]` | `max_consecutive_tool_only` | no reader — the guard it names does not exist |
  | `[tools]` | `event_channel_size`, `browser_binary_path` | no reader (channels are built with fixed capacities; the browser resolves its binary inside sourcehound) |
  | `[memory]` | `agentmemory_url`, `agentmemory_secret` | legacy shims; `agentmemory_auto_spawn` already warns |
  | `[tools.web]` | `search_url`, `exa_api_key`, `searxng_base_url` | ALREADY honest — `WebToolSettings::warn_legacy_keys` prints one line per key set (plan 11C1); they only look "unread" because the warning is the only reader |

  Also: the audit script is promoted to `scripts/audit-config-surface.py` (iter-1123) so the claim can be re-checked instead of re-derived.

  **Closed iter-1123** — resolution per group, chosen against the operator's OWN config: `~/.operant/operant.toml` already carries every one of these keys (it was copied from the template), and the settings structs are `deny_unknown_fields`, so DELETION would hard-fail the next run. Every key is therefore accepted and reported:
  - `[tools.stt]` — wired (iter-1122), not warned.
  - `[tools.tool_search]` limits, the six `[tui]` keys, `[skills]` template pair, `[pk]`, `[bylaws] grant_ttl_days`, `[socialization]`, `[agent] max_consecutive_tool_only`, `[tools] event_channel_size`/`browser_binary_path`, `[memory] agentmemory_*` — warned by `AppConfig::unused_setting_warnings` (one line per key whose value differs from the default; a clean start is silent), and removed from `operant.example.toml` so a new config never learns them.
  - `[tools.tool_search]` was a wiring candidate (hardcoded 10/25 in `ToolSearchTool::execute`). Left warned, not wired: the limits are model-facing micro-tuning, and a non-vacuous test would have to drive `register_builtin_tools` with a full registry to prove the production path reads them. Recorded as the trade rather than half-wired.

  Completeness check: `scripts/audit-config-surface.py` reports **24** candidate fields across 41 settings structs; all 24 are accounted for — 20 by `AppConfig::unused_setting_warnings`, 3 (`[tools.web] search_url`/`exa_api_key`/`searxng_base_url`) by the pre-existing plan-11C1 migration warning, and the `pk` field itself by the `[pk]` table line. Re-run it after any settings change; `--check` exits non-zero when a NEW candidate appears.

  Residuals: (a) the warnings reach stderr/`operant config show` runs, not the TUI's config pane — the TUI log shows them once at boot; (b) the keys can only be DELETED in a major release (or with a `--migrate-config` sweep), since every existing config that carries one would otherwise stop loading; (c) `scripts/audit-config-surface.py` is a heuristic (identifier-occurrence, not type-aware) — a field read only via a macro or reflection would be missed, which is why the script prints candidates for review rather than a verdict.


- [x] **RT-137 (M, BEHAVIOUR) — `operant tools disable <name>` changed nothing.** The CLI wrote `tool_state.json` next to the database and NOTHING read it: the runtime's disabled set came only from the TOML `[tools] disabled_tools`. An operator who disabled a tool — a noisy one, a dangerous one — kept getting it, and the CLI reported success. Same class as RT-136 on a different surface: a command that says it did something and did not.

  **Fixed iter-1124** — the state file moved into core (`operant-core/src/tools/tool_state.rs`: path, load, save, the union `disabled_tool_names`, and `apply_disabled_tools`, which the registry build calls with the AFT-replaced names). The CLI and the runtime now read the SAME file, so `operant tools disable` removes the tool from the toolset the agent gets. `operant tools list` now shows the FULL registry with a `[disabled]` marker and an "N registered, M enabled" count (before, the listing filtered disabled tools OUT and then printed a marker for a tool it never printed); `operant tools enable` says when the TOML still disables the tool.

  Tests: `tools::tool_state::tests::a_cli_disabled_tool_is_removed_from_the_registry` drives the production function into a real registry and asserts the tool is gone from the model-visible set and still present in the full listing — mutation-checked (dropping the state-file union from `apply_disabled_tools` makes it fail). E2E against a debug binary: `operant tools disable browser` writes the file and the listing reports "83 registered, 82 enabled" with the marker on `browser`.

  Residual: `operant tools enable` only clears the CLI file — a tool the TOML disables stays disabled on purpose (the CLI never rewrites the operator's TOML), and the command now says so instead of claiming success.

- [x] **RT-138 (M, FEATURE GAP) — the whole `operant hooks` family was a façade, and the hook registry only existed with the harness on.** Three defects in one surface. (1) `operant hooks list|test|revoke|doctor` read `~/.operant/data/hooks/*.hook` — a store NOTHING wrote and NOTHING read: the family could never do anything, and `hooks doctor` claimed "All hooks are registered and ready. Hooks execute during agent sessions when matching events fire." (2) The runtime's `HookRegistry` was created only when `[harness].enabled = true` (`main.rs`), so in a normal install NO hook could fire — and of the nine `HookEvent` variants only `AgentStart`/`AgentEnd`/`TurnStart`/`TurnEnd` are ever emitted (`GatewayStartup`, `SessionStart`, `SessionEnd`, `Command` and the `Hooks` catch-all slot are never emitted outside tests — the agent loop emits exactly those four, in `agent/run.rs`). (3) The README claimed "hermes-agent hook parity (before/after tool, turn, memory hooks)": there are no before/after-tool hooks anywhere in the tree, and the "memory hooks" are provider wiring, not hooks.

  **Fixed iter-1125** — shell hooks are real now: `operant-core/src/shell_hooks.rs` owns the store (`<name>.hook`, JSON `{"event","command"}`), the loader, the writer, the runner, and `install_shell_hooks`, which the agent build calls. The registry is created unconditionally; the harness bridge is registered on it when the harness is on. The CLI gained `operant hooks add --event <e> -- <cmd>`, `test` now RUNS the command and reports its exit code (it used to print the file), and `doctor` reports broken files instead of a blanket "ready". A hook naming an event the agent never emits is refused at write time and skipped at load time — a hook that can never fire is the one promise this surface must not make.

  Tests: `shell_hooks::tests::install_shell_hooks_loads_the_hooks_directory` drives the production path end to end (hooks directory → load → register → `emit(AgentEnd)` → the command really ran, observed through a marker file) — mutation-checked (making `install_shell_hooks` register nothing makes it fail). `a_registered_hook_runs_when_its_event_fires` covers the same path without the directory; `a_broken_file_does_not_silence_the_good_ones` covers a corrupt file and a file naming an unemitted event.

  Residual: the README's hook line now names the four real events and the shell-hook command; **tool-level and memory-level hooks remain unimplemented** — the registry shape supports them (a `HookEvent` variant plus a call site in the tool/memory path), but that is a feature, and it is logged here rather than claimed in the README.

- [x] **RT-139 (M, FEATURE GAP) — the SOP playbooks were operator-only: the agent could not read them.** `operant sop list|validate|show` read `~/.operant/sops/*.toml` with a private loader inside the CLI. NOTHING else in the tree read that directory, and the agent had no path to it at all — so a playbook the harness could have followed sat unused, and the loader existed in exactly one place (the CLI), which is why no reader was ever added.

  **Fixed iter-1126** — the store moved into core (`operant-core/src/sops.rs`: `Sop`, `sops_dir`, `discover_sops`), the CLI uses it, and the agent got a `sop` tool (list with no name, read one by name) registered with the builtins. The directory is now reachable from the agent loop, and the CLI and the agent read the SAME files — the operator's list and the agent's view cannot drift.

  Tests: `sops::tests::sops_are_discovered_sorted_with_their_description` (discovery, sorting, description, non-TOML files ignored); `tools::sop_tool::tests::listing_and_reading_an_sop_works` (list, read, unknown name); and the registration assertion in `tools::builtin::tests::test_register_builtin_tools_with_sub_agent` — mutation-checked: dropping `registry.register(SopTool)` from the builtins makes that test fail, so the tool cannot silently stop reaching the agent. E2E against a debug binary: `operant sop list` and `operant sop show` read a hand-written playbook through the shared loader.

  Residual: there is still no WRITER — the operator hand-writes the TOML files (no `operant sop add`), and the `sop` tool is read-on-demand rather than an injection into the system prompt, so a playbook is consulted only when the agent thinks to look for one.

- [x] **RT-140 (M, FEATURE GAP) — `operant webhook` records subscriptions nothing dispatches to.** The whole family was inert: `subscribe` wrote `~/.operant/data/webhook_subscriptions.json` and NOTHING in the tree read it, and `test` printed "This would send a POST request with an HMAC-SHA256 signature to the webhook URL using the configured secret" — the words "would send" were doing all the work; no request was made. The subscription's fields (`prompt`, `events`, `skills`, `deliver`, `deliver_chat_id`, `deliver_only`) promise a per-route event pipeline that does not exist.

  **Partly fixed iter-1127** — the store moved into core (`operant-core/src/gateway/webhook_subscriptions.rs`: the subscription type, the loader/writer, `sign_body` in the format the INBOUND adapter already accepts, and `post_json`), and `operant webhook test` now really POSTs the ping — signed with the subscription's secret when it has one — and reports the status and body. `operant webhook subscribe` now says plainly that the destination is recorded only.

  **Fixed iter-1132 — the DISPATCH, in the R1 (inbound) model.** The owner delegated the model choice ("choose recommended best decisions on your own"), and the fields decide it: `deliver`, `deliver_chat_id` and `deliver_only` only mean anything when WE receive the event and answer it somewhere, and `prompt`/`skills` shape how the AGENT handles the event — so the subscription is an INBOUND route the external service posts to, and the answer is delivered to the target. The reasoning, and why R2 (outbound) was rejected, are in **AD-042**.

  What landed:
  - **The route.** The subscription claims a path segment under `/webhook/`. The route is the LAST PATH SEGMENT of the subscribed URL (`https://example.com/webhook/orders` claims `orders`), with `operant webhook subscribe --route <name>` to name one explicitly. A segment that is not a plain path token claims nothing.
  - **The event.** The event name is the payload's `event` (or `type`) field; a payload that names none is a "message" event, which is the CLI's default. A subscription that does not list the event does not apply to it.
  - **The answer's destination.** The inbound channel cannot carry a reply (the webhook adapter is inbound-only), so the agent's answer was dropped on the floor — the event was handled and the operator never learned the outcome. `deliver` (`platform:chat_id`, with `deliver_chat_id` overriding the chat) now names where it goes, and the delivery ledger records the delivery there.
  - **The admin gate.** The gate is a USER gate, and a signature-verified webhook event has no user — but every webhook event was refused with "You are not authorized to use this bot." in any deployment that configures `admins`, which is every real deployment. An AUTHENTICATED event now bypasses the gate; an unauthenticated one is still refused.
  - **Per-route secrets.** A subscription with its own secret is verified against THAT secret, so two routes can use different ones; without one, `[gateway] webhooks_secret` applies.
  - **`deliver_only`** delivers the EVENT body without running the agent, and without the agent's prompt wrapped around it.

  Tests: `webhook::tests::a_subscription_route_dispatches_the_event` (a real HTTP POST through the adapter against the real store — the production path, not a helper), `gateway::tests::an_authenticated_event_is_not_refused_by_the_admin_gate`, `gateway::tests::a_delivery_target_moves_the_answer_to_the_named_platform`, and four unit tests in `webhook_subscriptions::tests` (the route derivation, the delivery target, the prompt composition, the event filter).

  Residuals, both recorded rather than hidden: the Slack- and WhatsApp-shaped payloads take their own forwarding branches in the adapter and do NOT pick up a subscription (only the generic JSON/text path dispatches); and a subscription's `skills` are NAMED to the agent in the event — the agent reads them with the skills tool — rather than being a hard filter on the loaded skill set, because the gateway builds its agent once with a fixed skill manager.

- [x] **RT-141 (L, FRAGMENTATION) — the binary carried a second, much larger config surface that was never loaded. It is retired.** `operant_config::schema::Config` (the aggregate in `crates/operant-config/src/schema/`) has no production loader: `Config::load_or_init` is called only from its own tests, the one production-typed consumer (`operant-providers::provider_runtime_options_from_config`) is called only from its own tests, and `operant-core/src/doctor.rs` already records the same gap from the other side. The live config is `operant_core::config::AppConfig`. 123 of the schema's 145 structs (645 fields) have no production reader outside the crate — `python3 scripts/audit-parallel-config.py` re-derives the counts.

  **Not an operator-facing break** — `operant.example.toml` advertises only tables the live loader accepts, and the settings structs use `deny_unknown_fields`, so a schema-shaped table in the operator's file is REJECTED rather than silently ignored. The risk is reader confusion and future divergence: a setting added to the schema surface looks live to the next reader and is unreachable, while a setting the operator needs goes into the live `AppConfig` and the schema surface keeps claiming it.

  **Shipped iter-1128 (label + guard), retirement planned** — the schema module now says, in its first lines, that it is a spec and not the live config; a static guard test fails if any non-test file starts loading the aggregate, so the surface cannot start being loaded by accident; the audit (`docs/audit/2026-10-21-PARALLEL-CONFIG-SURFACE.md`) and the plan (`docs/plans/plan-2026-10-21-config-schema-retirement.md`) carry the evidence, the options (retire the surface / adopt it as the spine / keep both) and the recommendation.

  This repeats a finding the repo already had but never tracked: `docs/R40-12-13-WIRE-OR-RETIRE-REVIEW.md` reached the same conclusion ("the 1,043-field schema is dead") and recommended "pick a winner"; that recommendation was never executed and was never recorded in this ledger.

  **The owner's decision (delegated) was Option 1 — retire. Step 1 shipped in iter-1138.** The AGGREGATE is gone: `schema::Config` (~30 sections, in `schema/proxy.rs`), its loader and its whole impl (`schema/config_impl.rs`, 1.9K lines), the v0→v1 migration of that shape (`migration.rs`'s `V1Compat`) and its 7.1K-line test file (`schema/core/core_tests.rs`) — about 9.7K lines, and with them the SECOND loader, the second `config.toml` resolution and the second migration. The two production-typed consumers of the aggregate (`operant-providers::provider_runtime_options_from_config` and `operant-providers::auth::{from_config, state_dir_from_config}`) were called only from their own tests and went with it. The guard is now stronger: it fails if `schema::Config` is USED anywhere in production code, and a second test fails if the type is DEFINED again.

  **What the live surface actually is, measured rather than assumed** (the aggregate's retirement is only safe if the types the live config composes are known): the production code outside `operant-config` names exactly 12 schema types and 5 schema functions — `ProxyConfig`, `ProxyScope`, `ObservabilityConfig`, `ModelProviderConfig`, `FallbackProviderConfig`, `ModelRouteConfig`, `EmbeddingRouteConfig`, `ReliabilityConfig`, `MultimodalConfig`, `ModelPricing`, `MemoryPolicyConfig`, `SearchMode`, plus `build_runtime_proxy_client_with_timeouts`, `apply_runtime_proxy_to_builder`, `build_runtime_proxy_client`, `client_config` and `set_runtime_proxy_config`. Everything else in `schema/` is the dormant surface the audit counted (123 structs, 645 fields).

  **Step 2 shipped in iter-1140 — the dormant SECTION structs are gone too.** The 17 wholly-dormant schema files are deleted (`agent_cfg`, `backup`, `channels_cfg`, `delegate`, `gateway`, `hardware`, `mcp`, `ops_cfg`, `runners`, `scheduler`, `security_cfg`, `skills`, `sop`, `stt`, `tts`, `tunnels`, `web_tools`), the six files that hold a live type are trimmed to it, and the crate's own dead modules go with them (`scattered_types`, `comment_writer`, `domain_matcher`, `pairing`, `typed_value`, `api_error`, `validation_warnings`, `workspace`, `cost`, `platform`, `migration`). The compiler was the oracle: every item it reported as unreachable after the deletions was removed, and the items the live types still reference (the `default_*` helpers, the `HasPropKind` impls, the `Configurable` derive and its support traits) were kept. `operant-config` is 7.1K lines, down from ~31K — the crate is now the live types and the derive machinery, not a second config system. The audit script re-derives the residue: 10 schema structs, 1 of them flagged (a false positive — `EmbeddingRouteConfig` is carried by the live `ProvidersConfig`).

  **Step 3 shipped in iter-1141 — the derive machinery is GONE and the row is CLOSED.** The `Configurable` derive existed for ONE reason: the retired aggregate's property machinery (`secret_fields`, `prop_fields`, `integration_descriptor`, `nested_option_entries`). The live types derived it only for the `#[secret]` and `#[prefix]` attributes, and nothing called what it generated. The derive is dropped from the six live types, the `#[secret]`/`#[prefix]` attributes go with it, and the `operant-macros` crate (1.3K lines) is deleted from the workspace along with the crate-level `helpers.rs` it needed and the `config`/`security` shim modules the macro's generated paths resolved through. `traits.rs` keeps only the two items that outlived it (`PropKind` and `HasPropKind`).

  **The whole parallel config surface is retired.** `operant-config` is now ~5K lines and holds the live types, the provider config, the security policy and the encrypted secret store — and one config system, not two.

  The residue that the retirement exposed is recorded as its own row: **RT-154** — the `#[secret]` marker was the only thing that ever claimed the config's provider keys were encrypted, and nothing in the live path honoured it. The field's doc now says what is true.

- [x] **RT-156 (M, BROKEN COPY-PASTE) — the README's config example did not load, and the README advertised a retired key.** The README carries the operator's first copy-paste config block (`[client]`, `[agent]`, `[memory]`, `[browser]`, `[skills]`, `[bylaws]`). Two of its keys were wrong: `fallbacks = [ { model = "gpt-4o-mini" } ]` — the live field is `fallback_models`, a list of STRINGS — and `grant_ttl_days = 7`, retired with the grant ledger (RT-136; the field is still ACCEPTED, so nothing warned). The first one is fatal: the settings structs are `deny_unknown_fields`, so the loader REJECTS THE WHOLE FILE — every operator who copied the README's example got a config that would not load, with a 40-field error message naming the keys it did not recognise. Both are mutation-checked: restoring `fallbacks` and `grant_ttl_days` makes them fail. This is the check the doc-claims script could not make — it reads PROSE, and this defect lived in a code block.

  Side effect: the README's example block was the only TOML block in the file, and it is now the only one that works; the second block (`[tui] terminal_scroll_mode`) was already valid.

- [x] **RT-157 (M, SURFACE) — the config TEMPLATE advertised three examples the loader REJECTS, so following one breaks the operator's whole config.** RT-156 fixed the README's copy-paste block. `operant.example.toml` — the file a new operator copies — has the same class of defect, and nothing checked it: `config.rs`'s `example_toml_parses` reads the LIVE (uncommented) template only, so every COMMENTED example — the operator's actual menu — was unchecked.

It was also a DUPLICATE: the template already documents the real `fallback_models` 40 lines earlier.

The template still described the whole feature: "the full output is offloaded to a durable artifact under `<workspace>/.operant/artifacts/tool-results/` … a one-line TOC stub … `0` disables. Default: 50000." The live guard is `agent.max_tool_result_share` (a SHARE of the context window, iter-357), and the withheld part is not stored anywhere.
  3. **`[channels.telegram]` / `[channels.discord]`** — the table of the `operant-channels` crate, DELETED in iter-892 (the workspace has 12 crates and none is `channels`). The block's comment still described the rich `Channel` trait stack ("approvals, draft streaming, ack reactions"). The live surfaces are the thin `[gateway]` adapters (and `operant channel start` wires to the gateway runner).

  **Fixed** — the three are gone from the template. The `max_tool_result_share` block now also says the fact the old paragraph implied and the code does not offer: the withheld part is NOT stored anywhere the agent can read it back, and the marker tells the model to re-run the tool with a narrower slice.

  **A fourth claim in the same file was false, and no loader could catch it.** The `[bylaws]` block ended with "(The run consults the grant ledger every tick regardless.)" — the grant ledger, the durable escalation queue, the employee registry, the hierarchy edges and the cast retired to Holon under the owner order of 2026-10-15 (`gateway_runner.rs`: "The approver is resolution-only now — an interactive approval or denial resolves the in-flight ask; there is no grant to mint and no durable row to record"). The line now says that.

  **The gate** — `crates/operant-core/tests/example_template_loads.rs` uncomments EVERY commented statement in the template — alone, and together with the commented table header that encloses it (a key inside a commented `[table]` belongs to that table when the operator uncomments the block) — and hands each candidate to the REAL loader (`parse_config_str`). A statement is accepted when at least one placement loads without an unknown-field verdict; a statement that fails in BOTH is a defect. Mutation-checked: injecting the retired `fallbacks` key and the deleted `[channels.telegram]` table back into a copy of the template makes it report both.

  The audit that found it is `scripts/audit-config-surface.py`'s sibling for EXAMPLES rather than fields; the derivation is in this row, and the tool that keeps it true is the test.

  Residual: the gate judges the unknown-field verdict only. A commented example whose VALUE is wrong for the field's type (a string where a number belongs) is a different verdict and is not caught here — the loader's type error is not what this row is about.

- [x] **RT-160 (M, DRIFT + DEAD GATE) — the README advertised three retired org mechanisms and the crate count was wrong in two documents; the gate that catches exactly this was not in the gate.** Two defects and one cause.

  **The claims.** The README's Features table row for *Org governance* read: "seat policies (`yolo`/`standard`/`scoped`/`lockdown`) with deny/allow globs, **a TTL'd grant ledger**, **durable escalation queue** (unattended cron runs ask-and-deny; **a senior's `/approve` mints a grant**), reporting lines, and **one attributable worklog row per scheduled run**". Every bolded clause names something operant no longer does. The grant ledger, the escalation queue, the employee registry, the hierarchy edges and the cast retired to Holon under the owner order of 2026-10-15 — `gateway_runner.rs` says it in the code comment: "The approver is resolution-only now — an interactive approval or denial resolves the in-flight ask; there is no grant to mint and no durable row to record." `SeatApprover::mint` does not exist. The worklog STORE retired too (`org/mod.rs`: "the worklog store itself retired to Holon's"), so operant writes no worklog row at all — `TurnObservation::into_entry`, the only way a `WorklogEntry` comes into existence, has no production caller (only worklog.rs's own tests). What operant still does is the enforcement surface: the seat policies, the seat budgets, and the interactive approval that resolves an in-flight ask.

  **The count.** `AGENTS.md` said "Workspace members (12 crates, self-contained)" and the README's crate map said "api · infra · macros (12 crates total …)". The workspace has **11** members: `operant-macros` (1.3K lines) was deleted with the retired config derive at iter-1141, and the count was not updated in either document. `scripts/check-doc-claims.sh` — the repo's OWN gate for this — reports it: "DRIFT workspace members: doc says 12, code has 11". It has been reporting that since iter-1141 (2026-10-21).

  **Why nobody saw it: the gate was not in the gate.** `scripts/self-test.sh` runs the build, the tests, clippy, the supply-chain check, fmt, the TUI corpus and the CLI smokes — and NOT `check-doc-claims.sh`. A check that runs only when a human remembers to run it is not a gate.

  **Fixed** — the README row now says what operant does (seat policies, per-seat budget overrides, an interactive approval that resolves an in-flight ask, and the pointer to Holon for the rest); the crate count is 11 in both documents and `macros` is gone from the crate map; and `bash scripts/check-doc-claims.sh` is now a step in `scripts/self-test.sh`, so the next drift fails the local gate the same day it lands.

  The rule this row repeats: a count is not a fact until it is derived from the code, and a check nobody runs is a comment (the same sentence RT-10 used for the supply-chain policy).

- [x] **RT-162 (M, DEAD GATE TAIL) — the local gate's last seven steps have been failing since iter-997, and the failure was invisible because the corpus's PASS line is louder.** `scripts/self-test.sh` runs each step with `eval "$test_command"` — in the CURRENT shell. The TUI-corpus step's command begins with `cd '$REPO_ROOT/crates/operant-cli/tests/tui_scenarios' && …`, so that `cd` moved the script's working directory into the scenarios directory and left it there. Every step after it resolves `./target/release/operant` against THAT directory: "No such file or directory". The seven CLI smoke steps (version, `architecture validate`, `--help`, `chat --help`, `run --help`, `dashboard --help`, and the quick run) have failed on every run since the corpus step was added at iter-997 (2026-10-19) — five days.

  **Why it was invisible.** The corpus's own verdict ("verify PASSED: 0 drift", 64 gated scenarios) prints immediately above the failures, and the summary line reads "Passed: 7 / Failed: 8". The STATE snapshot's claim that "the TUI visual corpus is GREEN and it is in the local gate" is true about the corpus and silent about the seven steps after it. A gate that is red in its tail is a gate that trains the reader to ignore red — and the reader who reads only the corpus's line reads green.

  **Fixed** — the corpus step runs in a SUBSHELL (`(cd … && …)`), so the script's working directory stays at the repository root, and the seven CLI steps name the binary absolutely (`$REPO_ROOT/target/release/operant`) so they do not depend on the caller's cwd at all.

  The doc-claims check (RT-160) is a step of the same gate in the same iteration — a gate that is red in its tail is no place to add one more check.

- [x] **RT-161 (M, DOC-VS-CODE) — `cmd_org.rs`'s module documentation described a command family that was deleted a week earlier, with intra-doc links to types that no longer exist.** The file's own doc comment still carried the pre-G+1 org surface command by command: `org employee create|update|retire`, `org notice post|ack|pin|unpin`, `org worklog append|list`, `org grant give|revoke`, `org sync`, `org check`, `org list`, `org import` — plus a table of where each one's `--reason` is persisted, and a "Storage" section naming `EmployeeDb`/`NoticeBoard`/`WorklogDb` as stores operant owns.

  **None of it exists.** `OrgSubcommand` has two variants: `Budget` and `Holon`. The three store types are gone (`EmployeeDb`, `NoticeBoard`, `WorklogDb` appear nowhere in the workspace outside this doc comment), and the intra-doc links `[`operator_entry`]`, `[`cmd_import`]` and `[`OrgEmployeeAction::Update`]` point at items that are not in the file — broken rustdoc links in a file whose sibling test (`crates/operant-cli/tests/org_reason.rs`) states the truth in its own header: "The `org employee/notice/worklog/sync/import` surface this file used to drive was deleted in iter-838 (owner order 2026-10-15); only the kanban half of the mandate survives."

  **Fixed** — the module doc now describes the surface that exists (`org budget set|list|clear`, and the `org holon` read-API views, the supervision loop, the tick, the archive and the notify seam), says plainly what retired to Holon and why, keeps the `--reason` mandate with the table of where the reason actually goes today (the kanban events; the `notify` reason is validated and echoed, not stored), and drops every link to an item that does not exist.

- [x] **RT-155 (L, RECORD-VS-CODE) — the genome doctor's staleness check did not do what the ledger said it did, so the documented close-out pattern could never converge.** RT-125 (iter-1111) recorded: "the doc MAY carry a 40-hex HEAD sha, and it is only stale when a commit since then touched something outside docs/ or the configured role paths — so the close-out pattern is `genome handoff --write` and commit the snapshot together with worklog/ledger edits (a commit carrying only docs never invalidates it)". **The code did none of that.** The vendored tool compared the snapshot's sha to HEAD literally (`if heads and heads[0] != head`), so the commit that CARRIES the snapshot always moved HEAD past the sha the snapshot names — the check was red after every close-out, and no amount of regenerating could make it green. (The ledger's claim that the fix was "mirrored into the skill copy" was also not true: the skill's `src/10_doctor.py` and its built `scripts/genome` both carried the literal comparison.)

  **Fixed (iter-1143)** — the check now asks the question the ledger says it asks: the snapshot is stale only when a commit SINCE the sha it names touched something outside `docs/` or a configured role path. A code commit invalidates it; a docs-only close-out does not. A snapshot whose named commit is not in the history (a rebase, a shallow clone) is reported as "cannot be compared" rather than silently treated as current.

  The change was made in the SOURCE (`~/.agents/skills/project-genome/src/10_doctor.py`), the skill's built artifact was regenerated with the VERSION bumped (`2026.10.21-2`, the release checksum guard requires it), and this repo's vendored copy was re-synced from the built artifact — so `genome upgrade --apply` is a no-op rather than a silent revert.

  **Verified both directions.** The close-out commit (`docs/handoff/STATE.md` + the worklog) leaves the doctor HEALTHY ("state snapshot a6d6ca79 is current: only documentation changed since it"), and a snapshot pointed at a code-era commit is reported as stale with the count of the non-documentation paths that changed ("61 non-documentation path(s) changed since"). The skill's own suite is green (34/34).

- [ ] **RT-154 (M, FALSE PROMISE) — the config's provider keys were marked `#[secret]`, and the only code that honoured the marker was code that never ran.** Found while retiring the derive (RT-141's last step): `ModelProviderConfig::api_key` carried `#[secret]` and a doc comment promising "Stored via the OS keyring when possible; never commit it to config.toml directly", and `api_keys` carried the same marker with a comment saying "each element is encrypted on save exactly like `api_key`".

  **Neither was true in production.** The `#[secret]` marker is read by exactly one thing: the `Configurable` derive's `secret_fields()`, whose only caller was the config AGGREGATE's `save()` — the surface that was never loaded in production (RT-141). The live write path is `operant_core::config::persist_config_key`, which writes the value as given; the live loader never decrypts one (the `SecretStore` decrypt path is called only from the provider auth-profile file). So `operant config set providers.models.openai.api_key sk-...` wrote the key into `operant.toml` in PLAINTEXT, and the field's own documentation said it would not.

  **What is true, stated now.** The config file's secrets are plaintext in a 0600 file. The ENCRYPTED store is the provider auth-profile file (`operant-providers`' `auth-profiles.json`, written through `SecretStore`) — that is where a long-lived OAuth token belongs. The field's doc comment now says exactly this, and the `#[secret]` marker is gone with the derive that read it.

  Not fixed here on purpose: making the config file's secrets encrypted at rest is a FEATURE with a migration (the loader would have to decrypt `enc2:` values, the writer would have to encrypt, and every existing plaintext value would need a migration path). It is recorded with that shape so the next agent does not have to re-derive it. The `SecretStore` machinery is already there and already proven by the auth-profile store.

  Residuals: (a) `operant connect` and the TUI's `/connect` write keys to `~/.operant/auth.json` and `~/.operant/.env` in plaintext too — the same class, one layer over; (b) `operant.example.toml` does not advertise an encrypted-config format, so nothing in the template promises one.

- [x] **RT-142 (M, FALSE ALARM) — `operant doctor` told the operator to run a command that cannot fix the warning it raised.** The live run reported "Skills Hub directory not initialized — (run: operant skills list)" and pushed "Initialize skills hub with 'operant skills list'" into the ACTIONABLE list ("Found 4 issue(s) to address"), on a machine with 103 working skills. Two facts make that a false alarm: (1) `operant skills list` does NOT create `~/.operant/skills/.hub` (verified live — the command ran and the directory was still absent), and (2) NOTHING in the tree creates it: the marketplace installs skills FLAT (`skills_dir.join(name)` in `skill_marketplace.rs`), and the only other mentions of `.hub` are readers (the skill walkers skip it; `skills_tool.rs` treats a skill under it as "hub-installed" and protects it from the background review). The check could never be satisfied, and the advice pointed at a command that had nothing to do with it.

  **Fixed iter-1129** — the check is informational when the directory is absent ("not in use in this build — skills install flat") and never becomes an actionable issue. The advice is now RETURNED by the check rather than pushed, so the invariant is testable.

  Test: `cmd_doctor::checks_tools::operant_binary_tests::the_skills_hub_check_never_asks_for_an_unfollowable_fix` — mutation-checked (restoring the old advice makes it fail).

  Residual (recorded, not fixed): the hub-install path itself is missing — `skills_tool.rs` protects "hub-installed" skills from the background review, but no install path puts a skill under `.hub/`, so that protection never applies to anything. Making the marketplace install into `.hub/<name>/` would give it teeth, but it changes WHERE `operant skills install` puts skills (the operator's flat layout today), so it is a behaviour decision, not a patch.

- [x] **RT-143 (M, FALSE ALARM) — the doctor reported "2 copies of operant on PATH" without saying which one was stale.** On this box the live run flagged `/home/ishanp/.local/bin/operant` and `/usr/local/bin/operant` and told the operator to "Remove or replace the stale operant copy so one binary is on PATH" — naming neither as the stale one. The wrong choice is a silent DOWNGRADE: the shell resolves `~/.local/bin` first, so removing THAT copy makes the older `/usr/local/bin/operant` the one that runs. Two copies of the same build were reported the same way, although they are nothing to fix.

  **Fixed iter-1130** — the check now classifies the copies: one → OK; several with identical build times and sizes → OK ("N identical copies on PATH"); several that differ → the copy the shell will launch (first on PATH) is named, and the older one is named as the stale one to replace. The classification is a pure function (`classify_binaries`), so it is tested without touching the process environment.

  Tests: `two_copies_with_different_builds_name_the_stale_one` (the copies are handed over in mtime order — stale first — so the classifier must find the newer one itself) and `two_identical_copies_are_not_an_issue` — both mutation-checked (dropping the build comparison or the sort makes them fail).

  Residual: the operator still cannot fix this from the agent side — `/usr/local/bin/operant` needs the owner's password to replace. The doctor's job here is to name the right file; the deploy constraint is recorded in memory and in AGENTS.md.

- [x] **RT-144 (M, FALSE CLAIM) — `operant gateway status` said "not running" while the gateway was running.** The live box runs the gateway under systemd (`operant gateway run` via `operant-gateway.service`), and the status command's running check read ONE source: `~/.operant/gateway.pid`. The unit was `active` with a live main pid, yet the command printed "Runtime: not running" — and then, because the pid file was absent, it DELETED it (`if !running && pid_path.exists()`), so the next run had even less to go on. The pid file is written at the end of `start_gateway`, so it is also briefly absent during a restart, and it is removed by any status run whose recorded pid had gone stale.

  **Fixed iter-1131** — the running check consults BOTH sources: the pid file (when that process is alive) and the systemd unit (`is-active` + `MainPID`). The status line names the pid and where it came from ("Runtime: running (PID 4242, systemd)"). The stale pid file is now only removed when NEITHER source says the gateway is up. The classification is a pure function (`classify_runtime`), so it is tested without touching the process.

  Tests: `cmd_gateway::tests::a_systemd_gateway_counts_as_running_without_a_pid_file` (the case that was broken), `the_pid_file_wins_when_both_sources_agree`, `nothing_running_reports_not_running` — mutation-checked (ignoring the systemd pid makes the first fail).

  Residual: none in the stop path. I first wrote that `operant gateway stop` stops only the pid-file process — that was WRONG, and the correction matters because it is the operator's way to stop the gateway: `cmd_stop` runs `systemctl --user stop operant-gateway` FIRST, then kills the pid-file process, and only falls back to the in-process stop when neither worked. Start and stop are symmetric. (The row was published with the wrong residual; it is corrected here rather than left standing.)

- [x] **RT-145 (M, ORDER-DEPENDENT TEST) — the test suite reached GitHub, and a background update check broke a proxy guard that has nothing to do with GitHub.** `client::tests::env_proxy_is_ignored_by_the_provider_client` (RT-131's guard: provider traffic must never follow `HTTP(S)_PROXY`/`ALL_PROXY`) FAILED in the full `operant-core` suite and PASSED in isolation — reproducibly, on a clean `origin/main` (verified by stashing the working tree: 2442 passed, 1 failed). The capture listener in that test counts ANY connection, and the connection was not provider traffic: it was `CONNECT api.github.com:443`, a request made by the BACKGROUND auto-update check. `binary_updater::kick_session_update` fires on the first use of the aft/sourcehound integration, spawns `gh api`, and `gh` resolves through the process's proxy variables — so the kick landed inside the proxy guard's window, through the guard's own capture listener, and the guard counted it as a violation. The update check is a fire-and-forget background task, so it lands in whatever test is running at the time: a test suite that makes a real network call, on a timing nobody controls.

  **Fixed iter-1132** — the BACKGROUND check does not run in a test build (`cfg!(test)`), and `OPERANT_NO_AUTO_UPDATE=1` turns it off in any binary (the operator's escape hatch, and what `scripts/self-test.sh` exports for the suite). The EXPLICIT paths (`operant doctor`'s integration check, `operant update --integrations`) are untouched: they are operator-initiated, and the fake-`gh` test drives exactly that path. The rule lives in one function, `auto_update_allowed_in(is_test_build)`, so it is testable without a test build.

  Tests: `binary_updater::tests::the_background_update_check_is_off_in_a_test_build`, `the_operator_can_turn_the_background_check_off`; and the gate itself — `cargo test -p operant-core --lib` is green again (2452 passed, 0 failed) and the proxy guard passes IN THE FULL SUITE, which is where it failed.

  Residual: the doctor's integration check still shells out to `gh` when a test drives it (a slow, network-dependent path, not a failure). The `gh` subprocess honours the process's proxy variables by design — that is GitHub's client, not ours.

- [x] **RT-146 (M, DEAD SURFACE) — `operant tui keybindings` wrote a keybindings file NOTHING reads, and told the operator to edit it.** The command created `~/.operant/keybindings.json` (content: `{"//": "User keybindings. See docs for the schema."}`) and opened it in `$EDITOR`. The file had no reader: the loaders (`load_keybindings_from_config` / `save_keybindings_to_config`) were deleted in the 2026-10-14 batch (plan 10) because they were no-op stubs with zero callers, and the TUI's `/keybindings` command was removed for the same reason — but the CLI subcommand survived with the writer intact. So the operator was told to customise their shortcuts in a file whose only pointer was to a schema that does not exist, and editing it changed nothing. It is the "operator writes a file, the runtime reads it" class (RT-137 to RT-140) with the writer still on the wrong side of the fix.

  **Fixed iter-1133** — the command prints the bindings that are IN EFFECT (the same catalogue `/keys` and `/hotkeys` read) and says plainly that custom overrides are not implemented, because the registry is the catalogue and not the dispatcher: `tui/app/key_handling.rs` still runs the if-chain, so an override table would be a promise the runtime cannot keep. It creates no file, and it names an old `keybindings.json` if one is still on disk.

  Test: `cmd_tui_debug::tests::the_keybindings_command_creates_no_file` (with the OLD code, the file is created and the test fails).

  The override feature itself stays open: `[keybindings]` in `operant.toml` is still read-and-ignored with a warning, and the TODO.md row stays — closing it needs the dispatch to read the registry, which the keybindings module itself calls "a separate, riskier change".

- [x] **RT-147 (M, OPERATOR STATE) — the CLI test suite OVERWROTE the operator's real `~/.operant/gateway.pid`, and then deleted it.** `test_gateway_start_stop_with_disabled_platforms` isolated its home with `operant_core::profile::set_operant_home_override(tmp)` — a THREAD-LOCAL override. The stores under the home read `platform::operant_home()`, which reads the ENVIRONMENT variable and never consulted the thread-local, so the "isolated" test called `start_gateway` — which writes the pid file at `operant_home()/gateway.pid` — with the operator's REAL `~/.operant` as the target, and then `stop_gateway()` removed the file. The damage: after any `cargo test -p operant-cli` (the repo's own `scripts/self-test.sh` runs it) a gateway the operator had running has lost its pid file. `operant gateway status` still finds a systemd gateway (it consults the unit too, since iter-1131), but a foreground gateway is reported as not running, and `operant gateway stop` — which kills the pid-file process — can no longer find it.

  **Fixed iter-1134** — the test isolates through the ENVIRONMENT, which is process-global and therefore seen by every thread (a thread-local override is the wrong shape for a server: its work happens elsewhere), and it PROVES the isolation before writing anything — `assert_eq!(operant_core::platform::operant_home(), tmp.path())`, the RT-99 pattern: a test that cannot prove it is writing to its own sandbox must not write at all. It also asserts the pid file is the test's own and is gone after the stop.

  **The broken seam is gone, not just unused.** `profile::set_operant_home_override`, `reset_operant_home_override`, `OperantHomeToken` and the thread-local are deleted, and `profile::get_operant_home()` is now `platform::operant_home()` — ONE resolver for the home. The seam looked like it isolated the home and did not; leaving it in place would invite the next agent to use it and to believe the stores were redirected.

  Tests: `gateway_runner::tests::test_gateway_start_stop_with_disabled_platforms` (the isolation proof and the pid-file assertions) and `profile::tests::test_get_operant_home_reads_the_environment`.

- [x] **RT-148 (L, FRAGMENTATION) — the TUI's `settings.json` and the auth store ignore `HERMES_HOME`, so a profile is not isolated from the default one.** `Settings::config_dir()` was `dirs::home_dir()/.operant` (`crates/operant-cli/src/tui/adapter_types/config.rs:144`), while every other store resolves through `operant_core::platform::operant_home()` (the `HERMES_HOME` variable, else `~/.operant`). So `operant profile use work` — which points `HERMES_HOME` at `~/.operant/profiles/work/` — gives the profile its own config, memory, sessions, skills and logs, but the TUI's visual settings and the CREDENTIAL STORE stayed in the shared `~/.operant`. Two profiles therefore shared one credential set and one set of visual preferences, and the profile doc's "fully independent" claim was not true of them.

  **Fixed iter-1137** — the credential question is answered by the profile design itself: `operant profile create --clone` copies the source profile's `.env` (the file that holds the API keys) into the profile, so a profile is MEANT to have its own credentials. `Settings::config_dir()` now returns `operant_core::platform::operant_home()`, which carries the credential store (`auth.json`), the visual settings (`settings.json`) and the user-level `AGENTS.md` selector with it. The OTHER auth store — the OAuth refresher's `auth.json` and the provider account index — moved in the same iteration, so there is one credential store per profile rather than two.

  Tests: `platform::tests::the_sticky_active_profile_selects_the_home` (the profile's home IS the effective home) and `config::tests::the_database_path_follows_the_operant_home`; the whole surface is pinned by the new guard `crates/operant-core/tests/one_home_resolver.rs` — mutation-checked (a file that builds an operant-home path from the OS home fails it, and the failure names the file and line).

  Residual: the profile's own `operant.toml` is optional — a profile created without `--clone` has no config file and therefore runs on the built-in defaults, not on the default profile's settings. That is the isolation contract (a profile is independent, not an overlay), and `operant profile show` now says so.

- [x] **RT-151 (M, FALSE PROMISE) — `operant profile use <name>` never took effect, and the operant home had NINE different resolvers.** The root cause of RT-148, and much larger than it. `operant profile use work` writes a sticky `<root>/active_profile` file and NOTHING READ IT: a whole-workspace search for `active_profile` finds only `profile.rs` (the writer), `cmd_profile.rs` (its own `list`/`show` display) and an unrelated provider-profile store — no runtime path, no config loader, no store. The live probe (a sandboxed `HOME`, a profile with a distinctive `model` in its `operant.toml`) showed the next run still reporting `model: gpt-4` and the DEFAULT profile's `database.db`. So the command reported "Switched to profile 'work'" and the switch did not exist.

  Underneath that, the home itself was resolved NINE ways, and only one of them honoured `HERMES_HOME`: the database (`AppConfig::default()` → `dirs::home_dir()/.operant/database.db`), the TUI's `settings.json` + `auth.json`, the `.env` that `/connect` writes its endpoint pins into (the runtime read a DIFFERENT file back), the OAuth refresher's `auth.json`, the provider account index, the MCP OAuth token directory, the update throttle (a THIRD spelling of the home: `OPERANT_HOME`), the TUI's log directory (a THIRD spelling again), the approval allowlist, the agents directory, the skill-registry cache, the models.dev cache, the checkpoints store, the transcripts, the stats, the history, the history pins, the slash-usage counter and the changelog marker. A profile therefore shared the default profile's DATABASE — sessions, cron jobs, budgets, tool state — even when `HERMES_HOME` pointed elsewhere.

  **Fixed iter-1137** — one resolver, and it resolves the profile too:
  - `operant_core::platform::operant_home()` is THE resolver: `HERMES_HOME` (explicit, wins) → the sticky active profile → `~/.operant`. `platform::default_operant_root()` is the root that holds `profiles/` and the sticky file, so switching profiles does not move the switch.
  - The sticky file is read on every resolution, and its name is validated with the profile-id rule before it is allowed to steer a path — a tampered `active_profile` is ignored, not obeyed.
  - Every store listed above now resolves through the one function (the `operant-providers` fallbacks are the exception: that crate cannot depend on `operant-core`, and its production path passes the loaded config's parent).
  - The DATABASE follows the home, so a profile has its own sessions, cron jobs, budgets and tool state.
  - The guard `crates/operant-core/tests/one_home_resolver.rs` scans every non-test file in the workspace and fails when one builds an operant-home path from the OS home, so the second spelling cannot come back.

  Tests: four `platform::tests` cases (the sticky profile selects the home; an explicit `HERMES_HOME` wins; a tampered name is ignored; a name that does not exist is ignored), `config::tests::the_database_path_follows_the_operant_home`, `profile::tests::the_profile_id_check_matches_the_documented_pattern` (the hand-written check and the pattern it replaces agree), and the guard itself — mutation-checked.

  **A security property the migration had to keep.** The credential guard (`credential_files::protected_path_reason`) protects `~/.operant/.env` and the other canonical stores RELATIVE TO the operant data dir. Pointing that dir at the effective home alone would have NARROWED it: with `HERMES_HOME` set, the operator's default `~/.operant/.env` would no longer have been a protected path — a credential file the agent could read. The guard therefore protects BOTH the effective home and the OS default home (`platform::os_default_home()`), plus `OPERANT_CONFIG_DIR` when it is set. The suite caught the narrowing as two failures (`aft_tools::tests::guard_blocks_operant_env`, `apply_patch_guard_extracts_file_headers`) before it could ship.

  Residuals: (a) the profile's own `operant.toml` is optional, so a profile created without `--clone` runs on the built-in defaults (RT-148's residual); (b) the gateway picks the active profile up on its next start, so switching profiles while a gateway is running leaves the OLD profile serving until it restarts — the switch is a start-up fact, and `operant profile use` says so; (c) `HERMES_HOME` still wins over the sticky profile, so a deployment that exports it has one home and no profiles.

- [x] **RT-152 (L, ORDER-DEPENDENT TEST) — the `/model` picker tests inherited the OPERATOR's credential state, so the result depended on the machine.** `model_command_opens_the_inline_picker_on_the_configured_rows`, `inline_picker_enter_applies_and_esc_closes` and `test_dialog_open_close_scenarios` all open the picker through the `/model` command, which is GATED on live credentials (`credentials_live()`: the boot snapshot, the auth store, or the shell's API key). The tests established that precondition by ACCIDENT — from the operator's real `~/.operant/auth.json` and the shell's exported key. Nothing in the test said so.

  **Found by the migration, not by the suite.** After the auth store's path became env-derived (RT-151), a parallel test that moved `HERMES_HOME` — or the cleared-auth-env window `with_cleared_auth_env` opens — could leave the store empty and the env keyless, and the picker would open the CONNECT dialog instead of the picker. The three tests then failed in the full suite and passed alone. A clean `origin/main` worktree run passed (1638/1638), and the patched tree failed the same three (1635/1638), so the regression was the migration's, not a pre-existing flake.

  **Fixed in the same iteration** — each of the three sets `app.has_credentials = true` explicitly: the test owns the precondition it needs instead of inheriting the operator's key file and the shell's environment. The neighbouring auth tests already worked this way ("this test controls credential presence explicitly"); the picker tests now match. Suite after the fix: 1638/1638 green, same as clean `origin/main`.

- [ ] **RT-153 (M, FRAGMENTATION) — the `operant-providers` crate is a SECOND provider surface that production never calls.** Found while retiring the config aggregate (RT-141): the crate holds 40+ provider adapters (Anthropic, OpenAI, Gemini, Groq, OpenRouter, Bedrock, Copilot, Ollama, Qwen, DeepSeek, Mistral, …), a provider factory, a resilient fallback chain, an OAuth auth service and a multimodal pipeline — and outside its own crate the whole workspace names exactly TWO of its items: `models_dev` (in `operant-api`) and `create_provider` (in the doctor, for its "can this provider be built?" probe). The live path never touches the factory: `operant-core/src/client.rs` (the `OpenAIClient`/`AnthropicClient` pair) talks to the provider, and `AppConfig.client` / `AppConfig.agent` carry the endpoint, the key and the wire format.

  **What that costs, concretely.** `ProviderRuntimeOptions` — the struct that carries `native_tools`, `merge_system_into_user`, `reasoning_enabled`, `extra_headers`, `api_path`, `provider_max_tokens`, `wire_api`, `think`, `ollama_num_ctx` and the rest — is only ever CONSTRUCTED inside `operant-providers`, and the one production-typed constructor was the dead `provider_runtime_options_from_config` (deleted in iter-1138). So those provider-profile keys are read by nothing in production. `create_resilient_provider` — the cross-provider fallback chain, with the `[providers] fallback_chain` / `fallback_providers` config — has no caller outside the crate's own tests either.

  **Not fixed here.** This is the same class as RT-141 and a much bigger surface: it needs the owner to choose between (a) retiring the crate (the adapters become an archive, the live client stays), (b) adopting the factory as the live provider path (a large change to the agent's client), or (c) keeping it as a spec, which is not defensible without a date and an owner. The evidence, the measurement and the options are the same shape as `docs/audit/2026-10-21-PARALLEL-CONFIG-SURFACE.md`; a plan is needed before anything is deleted. Recorded here so the next agent does not have to re-derive it.

  Residuals: (a) the doctor's provider probe still builds providers through the factory, so the crate is not dead code in the strict sense — it is dead WEIGHT, compiled and shipped for one probe; (b) the `[providers]` section of `operant.toml` — provider profiles, the fallback chain, `model_routes` — is read by the live config (`ProvidersConfig`) but the per-provider OPTIONS those profiles carry are not applied, so a `native_tools = false` in a provider profile has no effect on the live path. That is the RT-136 class (a key the template advertises and the binary ignores) at the provider level, and it is the sharpest edge of this row.

- [x] **RT-149 (M, FLAKE) — `tui::render::tests::accent_role_reaches_the_frame` failed in the full CLI suite and passed alone.** The known P6.11 flake (plan `docs/plans/2026-10-18-runtime-model-catalog-and-remaining-gaps.md:264`, open since iter-927). The assertion reads the ROLE palette after the paint and compares it with the frame's cells.

  **The cause, found by tracing the writes (iter-1136).** `App::new` calls `set_active_theme_enum`, which writes the PROCESS-GLOBAL role palette. The render module's own `make_app` (tui/render/mod.rs) — unlike its two twins in `app/tests.rs` and `app/commands.rs` — did not take the palette lock, and one of its callers, `the_ported_layout_republishes_the_hit_test_cells`, did not either. So that test's `App::new` reset the palette CONCURRENTLY with the theme-asserting test: between its `set_active_theme("light")` and the read of the role palette, the other thread's `App::new` (whose `Settings::default()` theme is "default") wrote the DEFAULT theme's accent — `Rgb(255,191,0)`, the amber the assertion then compared the frame against, while the frame had been painted under the light theme. The failure message named the wrong colour, which is why it read as mysterious.

  **The evidence** (a temporary `set_palette` trace, since removed): the failing runs show the writer's backtrace — `set_palette` <- `sync_role_palette` <- `set_active_theme` <- `set_active_theme_enum` <- `app/init.rs:43 (App::new)` <- `render/mod.rs:192 (make_app)` <- `render/mod.rs:152 (the_ported_layout_republishes_the_hit_test_cells)` — landing between the theme-asserting test's own write and its read. Reproduced in 4 of 8 traced runs; the fix holds over 12 consecutive untraced runs.

  **The fix** is the pattern the two twin helpers already use: the test takes the palette lock for its body, exactly like the theme-asserting tests do. No production code changed — the palette is written once at startup in production, and the lock is `#[cfg(test)]` only.

  What the trace also settled, for the next agent: the palette has TWO globals — `theme_colors::ACTIVE` (18 fields, QUANTIZED to the terminal depth) and the vendored role table (UNQUANTIZED) — and the frame's cells come from the operant palette while the assertion compares against the role table. They agree only when the terminal is 24-bit; in a test process `TERM` is usually unset, so the depth is 16. A future fix that compares them must compare like with like.

  **Measured (iter-1134), so the next agent does not start from zero:** 1 failure in 3 full `cargo test -p operant-cli --bin operant` runs, and 6 consecutive green runs in the same tree minutes later — the rate is load-dependent, not a fixed property. (The cause was found later, in iter-1136 — see above.)
- [x] **RT-150 (S, RENDERING) — the `operant tui keybindings` action column was ragged.** The action was printed with a Debug format spec applied straight to the enum, and a spec on a TUPLE variant pads the field, not the variant: `KeyAction::Custom(1)` rendered as `Custom(` + a 34-wide number + `)`, while a unit variant ignored the width altogether — so the column was ragged in two different ways. **Fixed iter-1135** — the action is rendered to a string first and padded after. Caught by the live probe of the deployed binary (`operant tui keybindings`), not by the tests: the unit test now pins the rendered shape, and the probe is what found it.

- [] **RT-135 (L, FEATURE GAP) — `operant cron run <id>` marks a job "triggered" but never runs it.** The command finds the job, marks it run, then prints "Manual execution via CLI is not yet implemented" (`cmd_cron.rs::cmd_run`): a manual trigger is a bookkeeping write, not a run, so an operator testing a job sees nothing happen until the scheduler's next tick. Not a regression — AGENTS.md documents it as the one accepted stub. Closing it needs a public `run_job_now(id)` on `CronScheduler` (today only `tick()` fires everything due) plus the CLI building the same agent the scheduler gets; that is a feature, not a one-line wire. Companion: `dashboard server --stop` (the other accepted stub) was closed in iter-1121 — pid file + SIGTERM + bounded wait, `--status` reports it, stale files are removed, and pid 0 is refused because `kill(0, …)` targets the caller's process group.

## OPEN — Crash on TTS init and the ONNX Runtime pin (2026-10-19)

Filed from the user's crash log: `thread 'tokio-rt-worker' panicked at .../ort-2.0.0-rc.12/src/lib.rs:211:45: Failed to initialize ORT API`, then `terminated by signal SIGABRT`, plus the repeated `[memory]` warnings in the same log. Each row lists its evidence. None is fixed. H = process abort; M = user-visible; L = hygiene.

- [x] **CR-1 (H) — the process aborts when the TTS engine starts.** `ort` 2.0.0-rc.12 calls `GetApi(ORT_API_VERSION)`, and its `default` features include `api-24` (`ort` Cargo.toml `default` list), so `ORT_API_VERSION` is 24 (`ort-sys` `src/version.rs`: `17` plus the `api-18`..`api-24` bits). `kokoro-tiny` 0.1.0 depends on `ort` without disabling defaults, so `cargo tree --offline -e features -i ort-sys` shows `api-24` enabled through `kokoro-tiny` → `operant-core`. The installed `~/.local/lib/operant/libonnxruntime.so.1` is 1.20.1. Probing it with `OrtGetApiBase` returned a non-NULL API for `GetApi(17)` and `GetApi(20)`, and NULL for `GetApi(21)`, `GetApi(23)`, and `GetApi(24)`. `ort`'s `expect("Failed to initialize ORT API")` panics, and `[profile.release] panic = "abort"` (`Cargo.toml:115`) turns that panic into SIGABRT. The only route into `ort` is `TtsEngine::new` (`crates/operant-core/src/tools/tts_tool.rs:913-915`). The default TTS provider is `kokoro` (`tts_tool.rs:63`), so a `text_to_speech` call without an explicit provider reaches it. The trigger in the user's session is not known. Fix: CR-2 (a runtime at 1.24 or newer). The `api-24` default cannot be removed from operant's own manifest, because Cargo unions features and `kokoro-tiny` requests `ort`'s defaults; that needs a `kokoro-tiny` release or a `[patch]`. Partly fixed iter-970: the engine starts on its own task, so an ORT panic is a tool error, and the release profile unwinds (AD-016; test `a_panicking_engine_start_is_a_tool_error`, mutation fails it). Open until the deploy installs ONNX Runtime 1.24.4 on this machine. Closed iter-975: the deploy installed ONNX Runtime 1.24.4 under `~/.local/lib/operant`. The row's probe now returns non-NULL for `GetApi(24)` on the installed library, so the `ort` panic cannot occur. The live TTS path was not called in this session; the probe is the verification.

- [x] **CR-2 (H) — the build and install scripts pin ONNX Runtime 1.20.1 for an `ort` that needs API 24, and nothing checks the match.** Pins: `scripts/install.sh:97` (copies the 1.20.1 lib into `~/.local/lib/operant`), `scripts/dev-env.sh:24`, `scripts/check.sh:21`, `scripts/setup-build-env.sh:81-83` (1.20.1 and 1.21.0), and `AGENTS.md:528-532` (`ORT_LIB_LOCATION`, `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`). The release build linked and installed with exit 0, so the mismatch appears only at runtime. Runtimes that already serve API 24 are on this machine: `libonnxruntime.so.1.25.1` (`~/Documents/knowledge-base/HoloOS/.mempalace-venv/lib/python3.14/site-packages/onnxruntime/capi/`) and `libonnxruntime.so.1.30.0` (`~/.venvs/hindsight/lib/python3.12/site-packages/onnxruntime/capi/`); both returned non-NULL for `GetApi(24)`. Fix: vendor a 1.24+ release under `local/`, point the four scripts and AGENTS.md at it, and have `install.sh` probe `GetApi(24)` before it copies the lib. Partly fixed iter-970: the pins are 1.24.4 in every script, AGENTS.md and the skill; `install.sh` refuses a library without API 24 (AD-017). Open until the deploy copies the 1.24.4 library into `~/.local/lib/operant`. Closed iter-975: the deploy copies the 1.24.4 library into `~/.local/lib/operant` (the closure condition). The new binary resolves `libonnxruntime.so.1` to 1.24.4 (`ldd`), the gateway started on it, and the installed binary's md5 matches the running executable.

- [x] **CR-3 (H) — `panic = "abort"` makes any panic in an optional subsystem kill the whole gateway or TUI.** `Cargo.toml:115`. The TTS init panic comes from inside `ort`, so `TtsEngine::new` cannot turn it into a tool error while panics abort. Related: RT-21 (panics on fallible paths). Options: set `panic = "unwind"` and run the kokoro init in a spawned task, so a panic becomes a `JoinError`; or run kokoro in a child process. Fixed iter-970: `panic = "unwind"` in `[profile.release]` (AD-016).

- [x] **CR-4 (M) — `operant doctor` reports `tts (available)` where TTS aborts.** Reproduced: the doctor output on this machine has `✓ tts (available)`. Cause: `crates/operant-cli/src/cmd_doctor/checks_tools.rs:452` passes `("tts", vec![], config.tts.enabled)`, and `checks_tools.rs:457-458` computes `envs_ok` with `|| required_envs.is_empty()`, so any row with an empty env list is available whatever its config (`vision` on line 451 has the same shape). The doctor never probes ORT. Fix: an empty env list should not make a row available by itself, and the tts row needs a real init probe. Fixed iter-970: a row with no env list is available only through its configuration; TTS reports `checked on first use` instead of available (AD-018). The doctor's output on this machine now shows `tts (not configured)`.

- [x] **CR-5 (M) — every `[memory]` warning prints twice per process.** `main.rs:2817` calls `apply_startup_proxy` (`operant-core/src/config.rs:2322`), which calls `load_app_config`. `main.rs:2833` calls `load_app_config` again inside `run()`. Each load runs `warn_legacy_keys` (`config.rs:2098-2102`). Reproduced: `operant doctor` printed two copies of the `provider = "builtin"` line and two of the `agentmemory_auto_spawn` line. A second emitter with identical text is at `crates/operant-core/src/memory_provider.rs:371`; which path runs it was not traced. Remedy on the owner's side: delete `provider` and `agentmemory_auto_spawn` from `[memory]` in `operant.toml` (`docs/plans/plan-2026-09-29-telegram-loop.md:122` places it at `~/.operant/operant.toml`). The repo's `operant.example.toml` has neither key. Fix: load the config once and pass the `LoadedConfig` into `run()`. Fixed iter-970: `main` loads the config once and passes it to `run`; `apply_startup_proxy` takes the loaded config. The dev `doctor` prints each `[memory]` warning once (AD-018).

- [x] **CR-6 (L) — two `operant` binaries on the machine, from different builds.** `/usr/local/bin/operant` (root-owned, 2026-10-17 18:05) and `~/.local/bin/operant` (2026-10-19 04:01). Both resolve `libonnxruntime.so.1` to `~/.local/lib/operant`, so both carry CR-1. The deploy notes put `~/.local/bin` first on PATH, but which binary the user launched is not confirmed. Fix: the deploy step should replace or remove the stale copy; `docs/RUNBOOK.md` says the root copy needs the owner's sudo. Fixed iter-970 in part: `doctor` lists every distinct `operant` on PATH and reports more than one as a manual issue (AD-018; test `two_copies_are_found_and_a_repeated_directory_is_counted_once`, mutation fails it). Removing the root-owned `/usr/local/bin/operant` needs the owner's sudo, so that deletion is the owner's step. Still open iter-975: `~/.local/bin/operant` is the current release. `/usr/local/bin/operant` is the stale, root-owned binary; removing it needs sudo, so it is the owner's action. CLOSED as a REPORTED, OWNER-ACTIONABLE issue (iter-987): the agent side is complete — `doctor` names every `operant` on PATH and flags more than one, so the operator is told which copy is which on every run, and the deploy notes put `~/.local/bin` first. The one step left is the owner's `sudo rm /usr/local/bin/operant`, which the agent cannot perform (no sudo password on this box, per the deploy constraint). The row is no longer an unreported defect; it is a diagnosed one with the remedy in the operator's hands. **A THIRD copy found iter-988: `~/.cargo/bin/operant` (2026-10-14).** It is worse than stale: it does not RUN. It needs `libonnxruntime.so.1` version `VERS_1.20.1` and the runtime has 1.24.4, so every invocation fails with a loader error: `version 'VERS_1.20.1' not found (required by /home/ishanp/.cargo/bin/operant)`. It is not on the deploy path (`~/.local/bin` is first), and the agent can move it — it is the user's own file, not root-owned — so it is the agent's to clear, unlike the `/usr/local/bin` copy. **Fixed iter-988:** the copy is moved to `~/.cargo/bin/operant.stale-2026-10-14` (moved, not deleted, so it is reversible) and `doctor` now reports 2 copies on PATH instead of 3. The `/usr/local/bin` copy still needs the owner's sudo.

## OPEN — Terminal env overrides replace the configured endpoint and model (2026-10-19)


Reported: test runs send every message to a `127.0.0.1:2019x` endpoint and ignore the configured model. Reproduced with `target/release/operant` (built 2026-10-19 04:01) under `env -i`, with `OPERANT_CONFIG_DIR` pointing at a copy of `~/.operant/operant.toml`. That file sets `client.base_url = "https://aroute.ishanparihar.com/v1"` and `agent.model = "large-stack"`. The user's own terminal was not inspected, so the source of the stale value is unconfirmed until the check under Workaround runs there.

| Added to the clean env | Observed |
|---|---|
| nothing | `operant config show`: `base_url: https://aroute.ishanparihar.com/v1`, `model: large-stack` |
| `OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://127.0.0.1:20198/v1 HERMES_MODEL=gpt-4o` | `operant config show`: `base_url: http://127.0.0.1:20198/v1`, `model: gpt-4o` |
| `OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://127.0.0.1:20198/v1` | `operant run -q ping` → `POST http://127.0.0.1:20198/v1/chat/completions` → `Connection refused (os error 111)` |
| `HTTPS_PROXY=http://127.0.0.1:20198` (base URL unchanged) | `operant run -q ping` → `https://aroute.ishanparihar.com/v1/chat/completions` → `tunnel error … Connection refused` |

Causes:

- **ENV-1 — env beats the TOML, silently (confirmed).** `AppConfig::apply_env_overrides` (`crates/operant-core/src/config.rs:2389-2390`) writes `OPENAI_BASE_URL` into `client.base_url` and `HERMES_MODEL` into `agent.model` on every load, with no provenance or warning. The global `--base-url` flag is bound to the same variable (`crates/operant-cli/src/main.rs:154`). The TUI loads through the same call (`main.rs:2869`); the TUI itself was not run. Config writes from `/model` and `/connect` go to the same file, so a stale variable silently overrides those too.
- **ENV-2 — env proxy variables redirect provider traffic (confirmed).** reqwest honors `HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, and `ALL_PROXY` by default, and no client builder calls `no_proxy()` (checked across non-test code). A stale proxy value sends every provider request to that port. This shell carries `HTTPS_PROXY=http://127.0.0.1:43117`, which is llmtrim's port. llmtrim's port is not fixed, so a value inherited from an earlier llmtrim session would point at a dead proxy. That is a hypothesis for the reported port, not verified in the user's terminal.
- **ENV-3 — two config schemas; only `operant.toml` is live.** `operant-config`'s `config.toml` loader (`Config::load_or_init`) has no non-test caller, so its `apply_env_overrides` never runs. The live loader is `default_config_paths` (`crates/operant-core/src/config.rs:2332-2352`), which reads `operant.toml`. `~/.operant/config.toml` still holds `[providers.omniroute] base_url = "http://localhost:20129/v1"` and `default_model = "small-stack"`. That is the only 2019x literal in `~/.operant/config.toml` and `operant.toml`, and runtime does not read it. The live loader also prints warnings on every load for stale `operant.toml` keys (`[memory] provider`, `agentmemory_auto_spawn`).
- **ENV-4 — the gateway re-applies `.env` before every turn, from a different file (code-verified, not reproduced).** `crates/operant-cli/src/gateway_runner.rs:4137` calls `env_passthrough::reload_dotenv()`.
