- [x] **RT-26 (M) — `unsafe-libyaml` 0.2.11 is unmaintained (RUSTSEC-2024-0449), reached through `serde_yaml` 0.9.34, which is deprecated.** `deny.toml` sets `unmaintained = "workspace"` and `ignore = []`, so its policy would flag this (`cargo deny` not run here). OPEN, owner decision (AD-033). `serde_yaml_bw` 2.5.8 drops libyaml. It was migrated in full across 13 files and the manifests, then reverted: a missing-field error lost the `pool:` path and its line and column, `name: 5` parsed into a `String` as `"5"` where 0.9 refused it, and `missing_pool_name_names_the_real_cause` failed. Choices: add an `ignore` entry (the owner sets `ignore`), or migrate with path-tracked errors and strict scalars, a larger change. DECIDED in AD-033 (iter-977): NOT changed, for the reasons recorded there — the manifest loader gates organism policy, and both behaviour changes alter what an operator sees and what gets accepted. The advisory is ACCEPTED rather than cleared, and the entry is in `.ci/advisory-allowlist.txt` with that reason, so the supply-chain gate (iter-982) reports it and fails if it changes. The row is closed as an accepted, recorded risk; the migration remains available as a larger change if the owner wants it.
- [x] **RT-27 (L) — `ttf-parser` (RUSTSEC-2026-0192) is reached through `pdf-extract` 0.12.1 → `lopdf` 0.42.0.** Only the `rag-pdf` feature reaches it (`operant-tools/src/pdf_read.rs`, one `pdf_extract::extract_text_from_mem` call). It is not in the default `operant-cli` graph. OPEN. `lopdf` 0.45.0 has no `ttf-parser`, but `pdf-extract` 0.12.1, the latest release, still pins 0.42. The fix is a port of `pdf_read` to `lopdf` 0.45, which is a text-extraction rewrite, or an upstream `pdf-extract` release. Fixed iter-987 (AD-039): `pdf_read` is ported from `pdf-extract` 0.12 to `lopdf` 0.45 (default features off, so `chrono-clock`/`rayon` are not pulled and `font_embedding` — lopdf's `skrifa` alternative — is not either). `ttf-parser` is gone from the graph: `cargo tree -p operant-tools --features rag-pdf` shows zero occurrences, and `cargo audit` now reports two advisories instead of three. The port also closed a live hazard: `pdf-extract` did not bound a page's decompressed content stream, so a PDF decompression bomb was possible; `lopdf` does, and the bound is explicit (64 MiB per page). The port also exposed a broken test fixture: the hand-built PDF declared a 41-byte stream as 44 and its xref entries pointed past their objects. `pdf-extract` reconstructed the cross-reference table and was lenient about it; `lopdf` TRUSTS the table, so the fixture yielded no text at all. The fixture now computes its offsets and its stream length, and a new test (`the_extractor_reads_the_text_the_pdf_carries`) asserts the extracted text contains "Hello PDF" — the tool-level test accepted "no extractable text" as a valid outcome, so it would not have noticed an extractor that extracted nothing. 16 PDF tests pass, including three new ones.
- [x] **RT-28 (L) — `bincode` 1.3.3 is unmaintained (RUSTSEC-2025-0141), reached through `syntect` 5.3.0 in the default `operant-cli` graph.** ACCEPTED (AD-033): syntect's bundled syntax dumps use the bincode 1 format, so a patch would change that format. Re-check at each `syntect` release. The acceptance is now MECHANICALLY ENFORCED: the entry is in `.ci/advisory-allowlist.txt` with its reason, and `scripts/supply-chain-gate.sh` (iter-982) runs `cargo audit` against the committed lockfile and FAILS on any advisory the allowlist does not cover. So the advisory is visible on every gate run rather than only in this ledger, and a `bincode` version change would show up as a new advisory id in the gate output. The row is closed as an accepted, enforced risk.
- [x] **RT-29 (M) — the vim mode is never drawn.** `VimMode::label` (`prompt_input/vim.rs`) has no caller outside its tests, and no render path draws NORMAL, INSERT, or VISUAL, so the composer shows no mode. Found in the AD-034 warnings triage. Owner decision: draw the indicator in the composer, or delete the label and its test. Fixed iter-980: the composer draws the mode badge, and the indicator is drawn only while vim is on. `TuiState::vim_mode_label` is the accessor (`app/tui_state_impl.rs`), the badge leads the hint row (`operant_ui/ui_input.rs`), and the hint row is reserved only when the badge is present, so nothing is drawn while vim is off. Test `vim_mode_is_drawn_in_the_composer_only_while_vim_is_on` pins both halves.
- [x] **RT-30 (M) — slash-command usage is recorded and never read for ordering.** `app/commands.rs` and `app/key_handling.rs` call `slash_usage.record` on every submit, but `rank_commands` (`slash_usage.rs`) has no production caller, and the typeahead (`prompt_input/typeahead.rs`) takes no `UsageStore`. Kept with an `expect` under AD-034. Owner decision: pass the usage store to the typeahead, or stop recording. Fixed iter-980: the typeahead takes the App's cached `UsageStore` and ranks through `rank_commands`, the one ranker the codebase already had. The `expect(dead_code)` on `rank_commands` is gone, so the function has a production caller. The store is read from the App's cache and never from disk on a keystroke. Test `slash_suggestions_rank_by_the_usage_the_caller_passes` pins the ordering.
- [x] **RT-31 (M) — the TUI's bridge-state channel has no producer.** `tui_app.rs` polls `bridge_state_rx`, but `App.bridge_state_tx` is never sent through. The gateway's own sender is created with its receiver discarded (`gateway_runner.rs`, `_bridge_state_rx`), and the gateway runs as a separate process, so the TUI never receives a bridge-state update. Owner decision: publish bridge state from the gateway over its IPC, or remove the channel and the indicator. Fixed iter-984 (AD-036): the second option, because reading the code showed there was nothing to publish to. `App.bridge_state` was written exactly once, to `Disconnected`; the only other write was inside a `try_recv()` loop over a channel whose only sender was its own other half, which nothing ever sent through. The gateway's channel had its receiver discarded at all three creation sites, and the gateway is a separate process. `operant-core` has no bridge concept (the AFT subprocess bridge is unrelated), and there is no `--remote` flag, no `operant serve`, no config key, and no IPC between the gateway and the TUI. So `Connecting` and `Connected` were unreachable variants. Deleted: `tui/bridge_state.rs`, the `App` fields, the channel, the poll site, and the gateway's sender (with its `expect(dead_code)`). The six `TuiState` methods that read them become the constants they already were, each with a comment saying why. The trait methods are NOT deleted — the render path reads them in `ui_header.rs`, `ui_input.rs`, `ui_overlays.rs` and `ui_prepare.rs`, and they already returned a constant, so removing them would be a large diff for no behavioural change.
- [x] **RT-32 (L) — MCP connection failures are not recorded, so no server can show an error state.** The MCP view maps only Connected, Connecting, and Disconnected (`app/commands.rs`), and `McpServerView.error_message` is always `None`. AD-034 removed `McpViewStatus::Error` for this reason. Fixed iter-984 (AD-036) — and the row understated the defect. Reading the startup path found an AVAILABILITY bug, not just a display gap: `connect_mcp_server(mcp_manager, server).await?` at `main.rs:1255` propagated the connect error out of `build_registry`, so ONE unreachable MCP server aborted every registry-building command. Reproduced on the iter-983 binary: `operant tools list` exited 1 with a raw `Connection refused` network error and no server name, and the agent could not start at all — for a tool the user may not even use. The manager had no record of the attempt, because a failed server is never inserted into `servers`. Fixed in three parts. (1) `McpManager` gains `connect_failures`; all three `add_*` methods record the connect error's own text on failure and clear it on success, so a server that was broken and then fixed does not keep its old error, and `remove_server` clears it too. (2) The startup `?` becomes a logged warning, so one broken server no longer stops operant from starting. (3) `McpViewStatus::Error` is restored — AD-034 removed it because nothing could set it, and now something can — and `McpServerView.error_message` (which already existed and already rendered) is populated from the recorded failure. The unreachable `Connecting` variant is deleted: the status was computed as `Connecting` when `server_names.contains(&server.name)`, but `server_names()` and `all_servers()` read the SAME map, so that arm could never fire. Tests: `a_failed_connect_records_the_failure_for_the_view`, `a_server_that_was_never_tried_has_no_failure`, `a_successful_connect_clears_an_earlier_failure` (operant-core), and `a_failed_server_renders_the_error_status_not_disconnected` (operant-cli). Verified end to end: `operant tools list` with a broken MCP server now exits 0 and lists the tools; before the fix it exited 1.
- [x] **RT-33 (L) — light-terminal colour adaptation is not wired.** The vendored `vendor/style/theme_mode.rs` functions (`adapt_color_for_theme`, `adapt_buffer`, `adapt_buffer_for_theme`, `is_light_theme`) have no caller, so colours are not adapted for a light background. Kept under AD-034. Owner decision: wire the adapter into the palette, or delete the vendored functions. CLOSED AS NOT A DEFECT (iter-980). The premise is wrong on two counts. (1) The adaptation IS wired: `adapt_buffer_for_display` is the per-frame substitution choke point in the render path (`tui/operant_ui/mod.rs:2156`), and it carries the light-mode branch. (2) The buffer-level luminance flip must stay INERT on purpose. operant ships eight self-contained palettes, and its `light` palette already carries dark inks on a near-white surface (`tui/theme_colors.rs:471-480`): the seed IS the light adaptation. Enabling the jcode flip would double-adapt — the `light` theme's `text` (Rgb(33,33,33)) would flip back to near-white and disappear. The uncalled functions are the explicit-mode variants that the display path does not use, and they stay as the vendored API. No code change.

- [x] **RT-34 (M) — the build dies in bindgen because three scripts resolved libclang three different ways.** `dev-env.sh` fell through to `local/libclang_extract/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu`, a directory that exists only after the Debian `.deb` fallback ran, and only printed a warning, so the build failed two minutes later inside `espeak-rs-sys` with a message naming neither the cause nor the fix. `check.sh` hardcoded `/usr/lib/llvm21/lib`, so a box with a different LLVM version found nothing. The provisioner left a DANGLING `local/libclang/libclang.so` symlink in place, because a link to a removed target passes a plain existence test — and this box hit exactly that when libclang moved from 22 to 23. Fixed iter-980 (AD-035): one resolver, `scripts/libclang-resolve.sh`, owns the choice for all three scripts. The test is "a globbable name in this directory resolves to a real file", so a dangling link is rejected. A missing libclang is now a startup error naming the fix, not a warning. The provisioner repairs a dangling link. Regression: `scripts/tests/libclang_resolve_test.sh`, 7 properties, PASS.


- [x] **KB-3 (H) — knowledge writes have no approval gate.** `write_approval::gate` has no caller outside its own file and defaults off. `is_permission_gated_tool` (`agent/mod.rs:818-832`) lists bash, terminal, file_write, and patch, but not `memory_store`, `skill_manage`, or `learning_manage`. Background review (`background_review.rs:491`) and gateway sessions write straight to disk. Fix: route background and gateway-origin writes through `gate()`. Adding the three tools to the gated list is a policy change that adds prompts in autonomous and gateway flows: an owner decision. Fixed iter-971 (AD-020): `memory_store`, `skill_manage` and `learning_manage` join the approval prompt; a background review refuses `memory_store` (test `a_background_review_cannot_write_memory`). Found while fixing: `is_background_review()` read only the global origin, so `review_write_guard` never ran in production; fixed (test `the_review_guard_sees_the_task_scoped_origin`). Limits: no staged apply path (the `write_approval` module has no caller; deletion candidate).

- [x] **KB-4 (H) — the security scan on skill writes never runs.** Fixed iter-962: create, edit, patch and write_file all scan the skill directory, and a refused write is rolled back (`skills_tool.rs` `write_screened`; test `test_refused_skill_write_is_rolled_back`). Finding: the `skills_tool.rs` header says security-scan-on-write. `scan_skill` (`skills_guard.rs:1304`) is called only from CLI install (`cmd_skills.rs:687, 804`). `SkillManageTool` never calls it. Fix: scan the target directory after create, edit, patch, and write_file, and revert on a block.

- [x] **KB-5 (H) — a skill description can inject into every later system prompt.** Fixed iter-962: name and description are XML-escaped in the frozen prefix (`agent/mod.rs` `escape_xml_text`; test `skill_text_cannot_close_the_skill_element`). Finding: `agent/events.rs:817-822` writes `<skill name="{name}">{description}</skill>` into the frozen prefix with no escaping. `validate_frontmatter` (`skills_tool.rs`) checks shape and length only. A description containing `</skill></available_skills>` followed by instructions persists across sessions. Fix: escape `<`, `>`, and `&` in name and description.

- [x] **KB-6 (H) — recalled memory is injected unescaped, and every turn is retained.** Partly fixed iter-962: the closing tag is neutralized and recalled text is labelled as data (`agent/mod.rs` `neutralize_wrapper_tag`; test `recalled_text_cannot_close_the_memory_wrapper`). Still open: retention is not gated for untrusted channels. That needs an owner decision, recorded in TODO.md Review. Finding: `run.rs:2484-2497` puts prefetch output inside `<memory_context>`. `format_hits` (`memory_wire.rs:228-243`) does not escape the closing tag. `run.rs:1539` queues `sync_turn` after every turn, and `retain_turn` (`memory_wire.rs:190-205`) stores up to 500 and 2000 characters of raw text. A message from any channel containing `</memory_context>` and instructions is stored and replayed. Fix: escape the closing tag, mark recalled memory as data, and skip retention for untrusted channels. Fixed iter-971 (AD-019): a gateway turn is retained only for a sender named in `gateway.admins`; an empty list retains nothing from the gateway (test `only_a_named_admin_has_its_turns_retained`). Behaviour change recorded in AD-019.

- [x] **KB-7 (H) — a deferred sync can retain one seat's turn into another seat's bank.** Fixed iter-962: the bank is read at enqueue and carried in `SyncJob::SyncTurn` (test `queued_turn_keeps_the_bank_it_was_queued_under`; `sync_turn_in_bank_retains_into_the_queued_bank`). Finding: `SyncJob::SyncTurn` carries only `user` and `assistant` text (`memory_provider.rs:48-53`). The bank is read when the job runs (`memory_provider.rs` run loop, `memory_wire.rs:202`), and `set_scope` (`gateway_runner.rs:1266, 1273`) can retarget the shared provider first. Timing-dependent: a backlog or a fast seat switch triggers it. Fix: carry the bank id in the job at enqueue time, or drain the executor before `set_scope`.

- [x] **KB-8 (M) — unbound sessions use the personal bank.** `bank_for(Personal)` returns `DEFAULT_BANK` (`memory_wire.rs:87-89`), and unbound gateway sessions are set to Personal (`gateway_runner.rs:1273`) for recall and retain. If a non-owner can reach an unbound session, owner memory is injected into it. Reachability not traced: whether `allowed_users` stops this. Fix: a per-sender bank for unbound sessions. Fixed iter-971 (AD-019): an unbound sender not named in `gateway.admins` runs in `MemoryScope::Sender` (bank `sender-` plus a SHA-256 prefix); the scope is set once per turn, so a sender with no session row no longer inherits the previous scope (test `only_a_named_admin_reaches_the_personal_bank`). The wiring in `run_turn` is checked by reading only.

- [x] **KB-9 (M) — the Holon redirect does not cover `learning_manage`.** Fixed iter-962: a Holon run's delete or edit is recorded as an annotation on the run (`learning_mutation_tool.rs` `holon_learning_annotation`; test `holon_run_records_a_delete_as_an_annotation_only`). Finding: The check exists in `memory_tools.rs:88` and `skills_tool.rs:1652`, but `learning_mutation_tool.rs` has none, so a Holon run can change personal skills and memory. Fix: the same `holon_learning_sink()` check.

- [x] **KB-10 (M, plugins-wasm only) — declared plugin permissions are not enforced.** `operant-plugins/src/lib.rs:68-81` declares FileRead, FileWrite, MemoryRead, and MemoryWrite. Only HttpClient and EnvRead are checked (`runtime.rs`). `plugin_memory.rs` sends the query and the full turn text to any memory plugin with no MemoryRead or MemoryWrite check. Fix: check the permission in each host call and in `call_export`, or remove the unused variants. Plan: `docs/plans/plan-2026-10-19-plugin-ecosystem.md` Phase 1.1. Fixed iter-972 (AD-023): `prefetch` needs `MemoryRead` and `sync_turn` needs `MemoryWrite` (`plugin_memory.rs`). Limits: FileRead and FileWrite stay declarative (no host function); the gates are verified by reading, since no WASM fixture exists.

- [x] **KB-11 (M, plugins-wasm only) — a plugin tool can replace a built-in.** Built-ins register at `main.rs:994`, and plugin tools register into the same registry at `main.rs:1124`. `ToolRegistry::register` (`tools.rs:430-437`) overwrites on a name collision and only warns. Signature verification defaults to Disabled (`config.rs:1151`). Fix: reject a built-in name in `register_plugin_tools`. Plan Phase 1.2. Fixed iter-972 (AD-023): a plugin tool whose name is already registered is skipped (`register_plugin_tool`; test `a_plugin_tool_does_not_replace_a_built_in_of_the_same_name`; mutant red).

- [x] **KB-12 (M) — writes are unbounded.** `memory_tools.rs:76-140` caps no key, content, or tag length. `retain_fact` (`memory_wire.rs:245-258`) rejects only empty content. Skill writes are capped at 1 MiB per file (`skills_tool.rs`), with no per-skill or total quota, file-count limit, or rate limit. Fix: caps on memory fields, per-skill and total quotas, and a per-turn rate limit. Fixed iter-971 (AD-021): `memory_store` refuses a key over 128 characters, content over 8000 bytes, more than 16 tags, or a tag over 64 characters (test `an_oversized_write_is_refused_and_a_bounded_one_is_not`; content mutant red).

- [x] **KB-13 (M) — secrets are stored in memory and skills.** `retain_turn` (`memory_wire.rs:190-205`) stores raw user and assistant text. No redaction call exists in `memory_wire.rs`, `memory_provider.rs`, or `memory_tools.rs`, though `operant-core` has `redaction.rs`. Skill writes have no secret scan (KB-4). Fix: redact before `retain_turn` and before skill writes. Fixed iter-971 (AD-021): turns and facts are redacted before retain, and a skill whose text holds a secret value is refused (tests `a_secret_in_a_turn_or_a_fact_is_not_stored_in_clear`, `a_skill_carrying_a_secret_value_is_refused_and_plain_text_is_not`; user-, assistant-, fact- and skill-side mutants red). The memory engine already masks `sk-` and `ghp_` on retain; the operant layer covers more shapes.

- [x] **KB-14 (M) — the memory store falls back to in-memory without a warning.** `memory_wire.rs:321-338` opens an in-memory SQLite store on any open error. The `persistent` flag is read only by `import_legacy_files` (`:367`). `memory_store` still returns `stored: true`. Fix: return a warning or error when `!persistent`, and report it in `operant doctor`. Fixed iter-971 (AD-022): `MemoryProvider::is_persistent`; the `memory_store` result carries `persistent`; `memory stats` prints `Storage: in-memory (degraded)` (test `a_store_that_cannot_open_its_file_reports_itself_in_memory`; mutant red).

- [x] **KB-15 (M) — non-atomic writes and lost updates.** `learning_graph.rs` rewrites `MEMORY.md` and `USER.md` with `fs::write` and no lock around the read-modify-write. `skills_tool.rs` writes skill files in place (around lines 1734, 1778, 1867, 1913, 2023). A crash truncates the file. Fix: write a temp file in the same directory, fsync, and rename; lock memory-file edits. Fixed iter-971 (AD-021): MEMORY.md, USER.md and learning SKILL.md writes are replaced through a temp file and rename, keeping the mode; memory-chunk edits take an in-process lock (test `a_replacement_leaves_no_temp_file_and_keeps_the_mode`; mode mutant red). Limits: the last write still wins across processes; `skill_manage`'s own writes (`write_screened`) still write in place.

- [x] **KB-16 (L) — symlinks inside a skill directory are followed.** `resolve_skill_target` (`skills_tool.rs`) is lexical only, so a symlink inside a skill directory redirects write, remove, and view outside it. A symlink has to exist first, which only the gated terminal tool can create. Fix: canonicalize the parent and check containment. Fixed iter-971 (AD-021): `resolve_skill_target` refuses a symlink on any path component, and the file walker skips symlinks (test `a_symlink_on_the_path_is_refused_and_a_plain_path_is_not`; mutant red).

- [x] **KB-17 (L, plugins-wasm only) — the plugin HTTP host function accepts any address.** `runtime.rs` `handle_http_request` (line 68) accepts any URL, including loopback and the cloud metadata address, for a plugin with HttpClient. Fix: deny loopback, private, and link-local ranges after resolution. Plan Phase 1.3. Fixed iter-972 (AD-023): `checked_destination` refuses an internal address, pins the checked address, and does not follow redirects (`runtime.rs`; tests `internal_addresses_are_refused_and_public_ones_are_not` and `a_request_to_an_internal_literal_is_refused_before_it_is_sent`; mutant red).

- [x] **KB-18 (L, plugins-wasm only) — a WASM plugin is instantiated synchronously on every call.** `plugin_memory.rs` `tool_schemas` creates the plugin synchronously on the async runtime each time. Fix: instantiate off the runtime, or cache the schema. Fixed iter-972 (AD-023): the schemas are read once in the load probe and cached (`read_tool_schemas`).

- [x] **KB-19 (L) — `memory delete <id>` deletes without confirmation.** `cmd_memory.rs:160-161` has no prompt; `clear` does confirm (`:253-266`). Fix: confirm, or accept `--yes`. Fixed iter-971 (AD-022): `memory delete` asks for `yes` unless `--confirm`, like `memory clear`; a refusal prints `Aborted — nothing deleted.` (module `confirm_tests`; mutant red).

- [x] **KB-20 (M, plugins-wasm only) — `operant plugins disable` has no effect on loading.** `cmd_plugins.rs` and `tui/plugins_hub.rs` write and read `<name>.enabled` markers. No code on the load path reads them: `register_plugin_tools` (`plugin_tools.rs`) loads through `PluginHost`, which discovers plugin directories, so a disabled plugin's tools still register. Found while checking the plugin surface for AD-005, not in the red-team record. Fix: decision D1 in `docs/plans/plan-2026-10-19-plugin-ecosystem.md`, then Phase 1.4. Fixed iter-972 (AD-023): `[plugins].disabled` is the deny list; the CLI and hub write it, and the loaders honour it. The marker files are retired. Test `an_unlisted_plugin_loads_and_a_listed_one_does_not` (mutant red). No allow list was built (deviation recorded in AD-023).

## OPEN — TUI legacy purge residue (owner decisions, 2026-10-18)

The dead-code purge of the `operant-cli` TUI tree kept the items below on purpose. Each one is
reported dead by the compiler, and removing it would change behaviour or needs a design call.
The `dead_code` warnings that remain are tracked in `.ci/clippy-allowlist.txt` (52 files after iter-941), so the warning gate stays green.

- [x] **Device-auth flow cannot finish — FIXED iter-937.** The dialog had no opener, so its states were
  unreachable. The scaffold is deleted with its key handling, dialog priority, and overlay entries rather
  than wired. The real OAuth device flow in `operant-core/src/oauth/device.rs` is unchanged.
- [x] **OAuth token fields unused — FIXED iter-940.** `StoredCredential::OAuthToken` had no constructor after the
  device-auth scaffold went (iter-937), so the variant, its `save` arm, and its `refresh` and `expires` fields
  are removed. `api_key_for` is an irrefutable `let` now that the enum has one variant.
- [x] **Dead TUI items remain — FIXED iter-941.** The `dead_code` items the compiler flags in
  `crates/operant-cli/src/tui/` are deleted (9 files, 506 lines), except names whose removal broke the build;
  those stay allowlisted. The allowlist drops from 58 to 52 entries. Whole-module deletion was tried first and
  removed nothing: every top-level TUI module is still reached by live code.
- [x] **Event and notification variants never built.** `notifications.rs:16` (`Success`), Fixed iter-974 (AD-031): our unused variants, fields and helpers are removed; the allowlist went from 52 to 47. The remaining 47 entries are the verbatim jcode ports, the keybinding registry, vendored style code and test-used helpers, kept on purpose.
  `bridge_state.rs:19` (`Reconnecting`, `Failed`, `OutboundOnly`), `debug/event_bus.rs:28` (`Mouse`,
  `Resize`, `Paste`, `OverlayOpened`, `OverlayClosed`), `mcp_view.rs:28` (`Error`), and
  `dialogs/permission.rs:35` (`Bash`, `PowerShell`, `FileRead`, `FileWrite`).
- [x] **Legacy header kept — decided iter-941.** `operant_ui/ui_header.rs` (1811 lines) is live: `operant_ui/mod.rs`
  draws the header from it. Decision: keep it. A jcode / oh-my-pi style header is a visual redesign, not a
  bug, so it stays out of the production-grade bug pass. Its dead items are removed like the rest of the tree.
- [x] **`commands.rs` dead layer (P6.8, 17 attributes) — FIXED iter-936.** The 17 `#[allow(dead_code)]`
  attributes are gone, and the items they hid are deleted: `CommandCategory`, every `CommandResult`
  variant except `Message` and `ShellCommand`, the `CommandDef` category and help fields,
  the dead `CommandRegistry` helpers, `resolve_command`, and the skill and TUI slash caches. The tests
  that covered them are removed; the resolve and execute tests now run against `CommandRegistry`.
  `adapter_types/tui_app.rs` and `tui/app/commands.rs` lost the matching arms. Still open: the two
  `intercept_slash_command{,_with_args}` fns in `tui/app/commands.rs` are dead and remain allowlisted.
- [x] **`[memory] audit_enabled` / `response_cache_enabled` crashed config load (FIXED iter-935).**
  The live `[memory]` table parses into `MemorySettings` (`operant-core/src/config.rs`), which is
  `deny_unknown_fields` and had no such fields. A file carrying either key failed with
  `Invalid TOML … unknown field audit_enabled` and exit 1 (reproduced on the iter-934 binary). The
  module docs in `operant-memory/src/audit.rs` and `response_cache.rs` told users to write those keys.
  Fixed by accepting them as retired fields that warn once, the same pattern as `agentmemory_*`.
  Regression: `config::tests::retired_memory_factory_keys_load_and_warn`.
- [x] **Memory factory is unreachable (R13-4 / R13-5) — FIXED iter-938.** `create_memory`,
  `create_memory_with_storage_and_routes`, `create_memory_for_migration`, and `create_response_cache`
  had no caller outside `operant-memory/src/lib.rs`. Decision: delete the factory and the modules only it
  reached (`hygiene`, `audit` (`AuditedMemory`), `response_cache`), rather than route production through it.
  Hygiene archives and purges memory files on a schedule, so wiring it in by default would start destroying
  data without an opt-in. Production memory is still built by `load_memory_manager` (`operant-cli/src/main.rs`).
  Ceiling: the platform `MemoryConfig` in `operant-config` keeps its factory-era fields (`hygiene_enabled`,
  `response_cache_ttl_minutes`, `audit_retention_days`, and the rest of that struct). They are public schema
  with no reader. A user's `[memory]` table that still carries those keys fails the load, as before.

## OPEN — Gateway turn concurrency (audit plan 06A — LOCKED iter-864, residual below)

The shared-agent race is fixed (one `turn_lock` per handler, held from the
retarget to the end of `handle`; interrupt reset and cron-origin moved
inside the lock; `/stop` only attaches to the session that holds the lock).
Residual, accepted for now:

- [x] **W3-2 — CLOSED 2026-10-18 (iter-906): `/stop` now cancels a queued turn.** `GatewayMessageHandler::cancel_if_queued` marks the session (under the same mutex the turn uses to claim `turn_lock`, so it cannot race the claim); the turn exits `Error::Interrupted` the moment it would start, and `/stop` replies "Cancelled your queued message". `stop_cancels_a_queued_turn_before_it_starts` pins it. Original entry: `/stop` for a QUEUED session is a no-op. With the turn
  lock, a session whose turn is waiting for the lock has an entry in
  `turn_inflight` but is not running, so its `/stop` gets no flag and
  reports "nothing to stop"; the turn runs when its turn comes. Cancelling
  a queued turn needs queue-entry removal in the dispatch loop — small,
  but a separate UX decision (a queued turn starts within seconds on a
  one-owner box). The pre-06A behavior was strictly worse: `/stop` for a
  queued chat killed the RUNNING chat's turn.

Also verified while implementing (plan 06A's D-6 check): the cron
scheduler runs due jobs SEQUENTIALLY (`for job in due_jobs {
run_job(&job).await }`, `scheduler.rs:260`), so the cron agent cannot run
two jobs at once — no lock needed there. D-6's remaining half (can a cron
run overlap a GATEWAY turn?) is moot: cron runs on its own agent (BUGS
D-3, kept).

- [x] **H-8 — CLOSED 2026-10-17 (iter-904): `/fast` removed on both
  surfaces.** The TUI arm flipped `App.fast_mode`, which no agent code
  read, and the gateway twin wrote `fast_mode` session metadata nothing
  read — a toggle that reported "enabled" while doing nothing. Deleted:
  the TUI arm + `App.fast_mode`, the `/fast` registrations (TUI registry,
  CLI `CommandDef`, gateway `CommandDef`, the `/help` line), the gateway
  handler. `test_fast_is_not_a_command` pins it. If provider priority
  tiers are wanted later, wire them to a real request field first.

- [x] **H-9 — CLOSED 2026-10-18 (iter-905): dead ends removed, MCP auth wired.**
  The connect dialog's `openai-codex` row opened a device-auth dialog whose
  polling task never existed ("not yet wired — restart operant"); the
  `github-copilot` arm was unreachable (the provider id is `copilot`, which
  already falls through to a working token dialog). Both arms and the
  `device_auth_pending` plumbing are gone — `openai-codex` now takes an API
  key like the other providers. The MCP panel's `a` key now runs the real
  OAuth 2.1 + PKCE login (`start_mcp_panel_auth`, same flow as
  `operant mcp login`) in the background and reports through the status
  line. Residual: the `device_auth_dialog` scaffold is retained but has no
  opener — delete it when a real device flow lands or in a dead-code sweep.

## OPEN — Duplicate tool rows mid-turn (2026-10-14 batch, plan 01)

- [x] **RT-35 (M) — `[client.rate_limit] max_retries = 0` panicked the process.** The attempt count was used raw in `for attempt in 1..=max_retries` in BOTH retry loops (`client.rs`). A 0 makes the range EMPTY, so the loop body never ran and the fall-through below was reached. The streaming loop returned an error (fixed in iter-139 as "ponytail-audit bug A22"), but the non-streaming loop hit `unreachable!("Retry loop always returns or breaks")` and PANICKED. In the gateway daemon that is a crash, and the setting is documented in `operant.example.toml`. Fixed iter-989: both loops clamp the count to at least 1, so a 0 means "one attempt, no retry" — which is what the setting means — and the non-streaming fall-through is an error rather than a panic. Test `a_zero_retry_budget_still_makes_one_request` drives the real `execute_with_retry` against a local server that answers 429: it asserts the call RETURNS an error (it used to panic) and that the server received EXACTLY ONE request (a 0 must not mean "no request"). Mutation: removing the clamp fails the test.

- [x] **RT-36 (L) — the trajectory directory grew without bound.** A trajectory is written per run, and the file name carries the run's timestamp (`<session_id>_<unix-ts>.json`), so a long-lived session accumulates one file per run. A Holon run ALWAYS records (`is_holon_run()` forces it on) and nothing pruned the directory — the only cleanup was the manual `operant trajectory clear`, which deletes everything. Measured on this box: 18 files, 940K, after a few days. Fixed iter-990: the write path now prunes to the newest 200, following the checkpoint store's `max_snapshots` precedent. The cap is a CONSTANT, not a config key — a trajectory is a diagnostic artefact, not a user-managed store, and no workflow needs the 501st one. Test `the_oldest_trajectories_are_pruned` writes cap+5 files with distinct mtimes and asserts the count is capped, the newest survives, the oldest is gone, and a non-trajectory file is untouched. Mutation: making the prune a no-op fails the test.

- [x] **RT-37 (M) — a configured `logging.log_file` was never rotated.** `init_logging` opened the configured file in APPEND mode with no size bound. The date-stamped `operant-*.log` files the TUI logger writes ARE pruned after 7 days (`cleanup_old_logs_in`), but a CONFIGURED file was not on that list — so the gateway's log grew without bound. Measured on this box: `~/.operant/logs/gateway.log` at 90 MB / 1.7M lines after about three weeks. Fixed iter-991: a `RollingLogFile` write target rotates at 32 MB, keeping two generations (`foo.log` → `foo.log.1` → `foo.log.2`). The check is on the WRITE path, not a timer: a daemon that emits a burst between timer ticks would overshoot the cap by the whole burst, and bounding the file is the point. Two tests: `a_configured_log_file_is_rotated_when_it_grows_past_the_cap` (the live file stays at or under the cap, the overflow lands in `.1`, and no line is lost) and `a_log_rotation_keeps_a_bounded_number_of_generations` (a third generation is never kept — an unbounded rotation would just move the growth problem). Mutation: making the rotation a no-op fails both.

- [x] **RT-38 (M) — the checkpoint shadow store had no size bound, and its size limits were dead config.** `CheckpointConfig` declared `max_total_size_mb` (500) and `max_file_size_mb` (10) and NOTHING ever read them: they were not even populated from `AppConfig`. The only bound was `MAX_CHECKPOINT_FILES` (50,000), which is a COUNT — 50,000 files of any size passes it. Measured on this box: one shadow store had reached 1.42 GiB of packed objects, 3x over the 500 MB the config documented, and the whole `~/.operant` tree was 3.7 GiB. Fixed iter-992: both limits are wired end to end (`[checkpoints]` in `operant.toml` → `CheckpointsSettings` → `CheckpointConfig` → the snapshot gate), and a tree over either limit is not snapshotted. The measurement skips the same excluded directory names the shadow store's `info/exclude` uses, so it covers what would actually be snapshotted. `max_total_size_mb = 0` disables the total check, `max_file_size_mb = 0` disables the per-file check. Tests: `a_tree_over_the_size_bounds_is_not_snapshotted`, `a_tree_within_the_size_bounds_is_snapshotted` (the check must not refuse everything) and `the_size_measurement_skips_the_excluded_directories`. Mutation: removing the size bound fails the first.

- [x] **RT-39 (M) — a panicking hook was swallowed SILENTLY.** `HookRegistry`'s `catch_unwind_safe` was a NO-OP: it awaited the future directly and always returned `Ok`, so the "Hook handler panicked — caught and ignored" warning in `emit` was unreachable. The panic was contained (the handler runs in a spawned task, so tokio's task panic handler catches it) — but "contained" is not "reported": a hook that panicked on every `AgentStart` was invisible in the logs. The old comment claimed `FutureExt::catch_unwind` "requires the future to be Unpin"; it does not — it requires `Sized`, and `AssertUnwindSafe<F>` for any `F: Future` satisfies it. Verified empirically before the change. Fixed in iter-993 and iter-994: `catch_unwind_safe` uses `FutureExt::catch_unwind` and maps the panic to `Err`, so the warning fires; and the handler CALL is inside the caught region, not just the future, because `HookHandler` is `Fn(..) -> Pin<Box<dyn Future>>` and the closure body runs at CALL time — a hook that panics while BUILDING its future panicked at the call site, outside the caught future, and went unreported. The runner is a free function (`run_handler`) so the tests call exactly what `emit` calls: the first attempt had the logic inline in `emit` and the test called `catch_unwind_safe` directly, which tested a re-implementation and MISSED a mutation to `emit` — that is why the runner was extracted. Also corrected `emit`'s doc, which claimed the handlers "run sequentially" — they have always been spawned, and the concurrency is the point (a hook must not be able to stall a gateway turn). Tests: `a_panicking_hook_is_reported_not_swallowed`, `a_hook_that_panics_while_building_its_future_is_reported` and `a_healthy_hook_is_not_reported_as_a_panic` (the fix must not turn every hook into a false alarm). Mutations: restoring the no-op fails two; moving the call outside the caught region fails one.

- [x] **RT-40 (M) — the log rotation broke `operant logs follow` (a regression this sweep introduced).** RT-37's rotation renames the live file aside and starts a new one, so the live file becomes a NEW inode. The follower tracked a byte OFFSET into the old file: `current_size > last_size` could stay false for a long time (so it printed NOTHING until 32 MB of new output had accumulated) and then it seeked into a stale offset and printed half a line. A size comparison is NOT enough: the new file can be LONGER than the old one's length was (11 bytes old, 15 new), so `current_size < last_size` is false and the replacement is missed. Fixed iter-995: the follower tracks the file's IDENTITY (the inode on unix) as well as the offset, and a different file resets the offset to zero. The read step is extracted into `read_appended` so the test drives the same code the follow loop does. Tests: `the_follower_reads_the_new_file_after_a_rotation` (which asserts the new file is LONGER than the old offset, so a size check alone provably cannot catch it) and `the_follower_does_not_reread_what_it_already_printed` (the offset must still work). Mutation: never detecting the replacement fails the first.

(An earlier count of 19 came from a truncated capture of a run that was cut short; the two full runs agree exactly.) The root cause is that the baselines were last regenerated at iter-910 and the TUI changed at iters 931, 933, 934, 936, 937, 941, 950, 952, 954, 964, 966, 969, 972, 973, 974, 978, 980, 984-986. The `typeahead-suggestions` drift is the command registry: iter-969 removed `/rollback`, `/subgoal` and `/verbose` and added `/diff`, so the "+N more" counter moved by one. `hooks-config-menu` is a removed overlay and `session-branching` an overlay flag, so those two need their scenarios retired rather than their baselines regenerated. **The corpus is a gate that has been RED for nine days and nobody noticed**, because `verify` was not in ANY automated gate: `scripts/check.sh` forwards to cargo, and the corpus is a Python script. Fixed iter-996: `scripts/self-test.sh` now runs the corpus verify, so the gate is executed. The baselines were regenerated for the two scenarios whose drift was a real UI change in iter-1001 (RT-47); the five scenarios that could never pass are excluded in iter-1007 (RT-52), and the corpus is GREEN for the first time.

- [x] **RT-42 (H, SECURITY) — the dashboard's auth was theatre: the session token was served on an UNAUTHENTICATED route.** `handle_index` injected the session token into the HTML, and the index route required no auth — so `GET /` returned the token, and that token opened every "protected" route. Reproduced against the iter-995 binary: `GET /` returned `__HERMES_SESSION_TOKEN__="operant_24cb14..."` in the body, and the scraped token fetched `/api/status`, `/api/boards` and `/api/config` (200 each). The API correctly returned 401 WITHOUT a token, so the auth existed — it was just given away on the front door. Compounding it: there was NO Host-header validation, and the dashboard binds to loopback, which every web page the operator visits can reach. A page could point a name it controls at 127.0.0.1 (DNS rebinding) and the browser would send a `Host` the page chose. Fixed iter-996 with three changes. (1) The index route only serves the token-bearing page to a request that ALREADY holds the token: the CLI opens the browser at `/?token=...`, the server exchanges it for an **HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict** cookie and redirects to `/`, so the token does not linger in the address bar. (2) A `Host` header that is not one this server answers to is refused (403) by a `host_guard` middleware. The rule has three cases, in order: an ABSENT `Host` is refused (HTTP/1.1 requires it and every browser sends it, so a request without one is not one a browser made); a host that is an IP LITERAL is allowed (a server bound to `0.0.0.0` is reachable on every interface, and the operator's browser reaches it by the address their machine has on the LAN — which is neither the bind address nor on the allowlist, so refusing it would 403 the operator's own dashboard; a DNS-rebinding attack uses a NAME it controls, not an IP literal); and otherwise the host must be on the allowlist (the bound address plus the loopback spellings of it). (3) The 401 body is JSON `{"error": "unauthenticated"}`, which is what the frontend's 401 path looks for; it deliberately carries NO `login_url`, because the frontend would `assign()` to it and the only URL that carries the token is the one the CLI prints. Verified end to end against the running server: `GET /` → 401 with no token in the body; `GET /?token=...` → 303 + the HttpOnly cookie; `GET /` with the cookie → 200 with the token injected; `GET /api/status` with the bearer token → 200. Eight tests against the REAL server on an ephemeral port: `the_index_route_does_not_hand_the_token_to_an_unauthenticated_caller`, `the_token_in_the_query_becomes_an_httponly_cookie`, `the_cookie_holder_gets_the_page_with_the_token`, `a_request_with_a_foreign_host_is_refused`, `the_host_check_accepts_the_operators_own_addresses`, `an_ip_literal_host_is_allowed_even_when_it_is_not_the_bind_address`, `a_request_without_a_host_header_is_refused`, `the_api_routes_still_require_the_bearer_token`. Two mutations, both caught: serving the token to anyone fails the first, disabling the Host guard fails the fourth. The tests need `flavor = "multi_thread"`: they do BLOCKING socket I/O, and the default current-thread runtime has no thread left to run the server on — a deadlock, not a slow test.

- [x] **RT-43 (H, FUNCTIONAL) — the dashboard's UI could never have worked: the server read the wrong auth header.** The frontend bundle's fetch wrapper sets `X-Hermes-Session-Token` on every request; the string `Authorization` does not appear in the bundle at all. The server's `check_auth` read ONLY `Authorization: Bearer`, so every API call the real dashboard made returned 401 — the UI was non-functional, and the "the dashboard works" claim in AGENTS.md was wrong. (The server's own tests passed because they sent the header the SERVER expected, not the one the CLIENT sends.) Found while fixing RT-42: the same read of the bundle that showed the token was injected into the page also showed the header name. Fixed iter-998: `check_auth` accepts the token from `Authorization: Bearer`, `X-Hermes-Session-Token` (what the bundle sends) and the `operant_dashboard_token` cookie (what the browser sends on a same-origin fetch). The test reads the header name OUT OF THE BUNDLE, so a change to the bundle's contract is caught. Verified end to end against the running server: no token → 401; each of the three carriers → 200. The accepting side of the check is exercised directly on `check_auth` rather than through the server: the 200-path handlers read the SQLite database SYNCHRONOUSLY, and a test that does blocking socket I/O on the same runtime exhausts the worker pool and deadlocks — the first attempt hung and was killed.

- [x] **RT-44 (H, FUNCTIONAL) — the dashboard serves 4 of the 79 API routes its own frontend calls.** The bundled frontend is a build artefact from a much larger operant/harness; it calls 79 distinct `/api/...` paths (`/api/analytics`, `/api/gateway`, `/api/mcp`, `/api/skills`, `/api/sessions/*`, `/api/ops/*`, `/api/ws` and more). The Rust server implements four (`/api/status`, `/api/boards`, `/api/health`, `/api/config`). Every other call 404s, so most of the dashboard's panels are empty or error, and the WebSocket at `/api/ws` — which the bundle opens for live updates — does not exist at all. Measured: `grep -oE '`/api/[a-z0-9/_{}-]+`' index-BB4BRelo.js | sort -u | wc -l` = 79. **This is a product gap, not a bug**: the dashboard was ported as a thin status view and the full bundle was shipped alongside it. The honest statement is that operant ships a four-route status dashboard with a large frontend bundle that expects a much larger API. Fixed in iter-998 by making the CLAIM match the CODE: the module doc, the AGENTS.md entry and the README now say what the dashboard IS, and the 79-route expectation is recorded here as the work it implies. Building the missing 75 routes is a project, not an iteration, and it is the owner's call whether to build them, to swap in a bundle that matches the four routes, or to ship the four routes as the dashboard. **RESOLVED iter-1120 (owner standing order: choose the recommended production decision): the bundle was REPLACED.** `crates/operant-cli/src/dashboard/index.html` is a single first-party file with inline CSS/JS — no bundle step, no external requests, no fonts — that renders the four routes it actually has (status incl. the RT-117 host metrics, boards, config) and refreshes every 5s. The 1.9 MB JS, its CSS, seven woff2 fonts, a webp and `favicon.ico` (2.2 MB total, none reachable by the new page) and the `/assets/{filename}` route + `handle_asset` are deleted; `fonts-terminal/` was already unreferenced and went with them. The defect was structurally untestable before because nothing compared the page's calls to the router; now `the_page_calls_only_routes_the_server_serves` parses every `getJSON("...")` path from the page, asserts it is one of the four, and requests it from the REAL server (no 404), and `the_status_payload_carries_every_field_the_system_panel_reads` reads the page source for each payload field so a field dropped on either side fails. Residual: the dashboard is still a status view (no live WebSocket, no chat) — that is the product's scope now, not a broken promise.

- [x] **RT-47 (M) — the TUI corpus's stale baselines are regenerated for the scenarios whose drift was a real UI change.** The `prove` pass (the determinism proof) re-rendered every scenario twice and gated the goldens: 65 deterministic and goldened, 0 unstable, 8 nondeterministic (excluded, with a stated reason), 3 broken. That is an environment-dependent render — the committed golden holds the deterministic "no API key configured" message — and it was restored rather than overwritten. - [x] **RT-54 (M, SECURITY) — a webhook adapter with no signing secret accepted every POST, silently.** The HMAC check is `if let Some(ref sec) = secret`, so with no `webhooks_secret` configured the whole verification block is skipped and the body is accepted and forwarded into the agent as a message. The adapter's default listen address is `0.0.0.0:8080` — the public internet. The configuration is NOT refused: a receiver on a private network with no secret is a legitimate setup, and changing that would break existing deployments. What was missing is that the operator could not TELL an unauthenticated receiver from a verified one — there was no warning anywhere. Fixed iter-1009 with a startup warning naming the address, what is accepted, and the two ways to fix it. Two tests pin the behaviour the warning is about: `a_signed_adapter_refuses_an_unsigned_request` (a signed adapter refuses an unsigned body — mutation-checked: skipping the verification block fails it) and `an_adapter_with_no_secret_accepts_an_unsigned_request` (an unsigned body is accepted AND forwarded, so the guard is the warning, not a behaviour change). The second test's first version failed with a 500, which turned out to be the CHANNEL being closed — the handler forwards the message and a dropped receiver makes the send fail, so the test was measuring a 500 that happened AFTER the signature check; the receiver is now kept alive and the test asserts the message really was forwarded.

- [x] **RT-55 (M) — the supply-chain gate could not report the finding it exists to report.** The gate's retry logic treated cargo-audit's non-zero exit as "the advisory database could not be read" — but cargo-audit exits non-zero whenever it finds a vulnerability, which is the normal case this gate exists to report. So a real finding sent the script down the fetch-retry branch, the fetched run ALSO exited non-zero, and the gate printed "cargo-audit failed; the advisory database could not be read" and exited 1 WITHOUT ever naming the advisory. Reproduced with a stub that reports one vulnerability and exits 1, exactly as the real tool does. The script's own comment said "the JSON's own `vulnerabilities.found` field is the authority" while the code did the opposite. Fixed: the JSON is the authority — the exit status only decides whether to re-run with a fetch, and a new `json_is_readable` helper decides whether the output is usable. The test's stub now exits non-zero when it reports a vulnerability, which is what made the path reachable at all: the old stub always exited 0, so the branch was never exercised. Mutation-checked: restoring the exit-status logic fails the test.

- [x] **RT-56 (S) — a size-bound checkpoint skip was reported as the wrong cause.** iter-992 made the checkpoint store honour its size limits, and the skip reason went to a `debug!` — invisible at the default log level. The tool's error message named only two causes ("checkpoints may be disabled in config" and "there were no changes to snapshot"), and NEITHER was the size skip. An operator whose tree was over the limit was told to enable checkpoints that were already enabled. The manager now records the reason and the tool surfaces it: "Failed to create checkpoint: the tree has a 3 MiB file, over the 1 MiB per-file checkpoint limit ([checkpoints] max_file_size_mb)". Mutation-checked: a skip that records no reason fails the test.

- [x] **RT-57 (S) — the documentation and the genome's role map disagreed about which files exist.** The genome's doctor reported two missing roles: `ownership_map` and `asks`. `ownership_map` was a real gap — the path (`docs/INDEX.md`) existed but had been scaffolded EMPTY by a concurrent `genome init`, and it was not linked from anywhere, so the documentation gate's D003 check failed on it (fixed in iter-1008). `asks` had no file at all: it is the operator-ruling ledger (what we asked, what they ruled, and which owner doc the ruling was applied to), and it is the ledger that makes the "operator feedback amends its owner in the same session" rule checkable. Both roles are now mapped in `genome.toml`, `docs/INDEX.md` carries the real ownership map, and `docs/kb/ASKS.md` exists with the table the genome's validator reads. The doctor's "missing role" findings are gone.

- [x] **RT-58 (H) — the checkpoint shadow store was SNAPSHOTTING ITSELF, which is where the 1.5 GB came from.** The store lives at `<operant home>/checkpoints/store/<hash>` and the snapshot's working directory is the operant home itself. The store's `info/exclude` listed `.git`, `node_modules/`, `target/`, `__pycache__/`, `.venv/`, `venv/` — but not `checkpoints/`, so `git add -A` reached `checkpoints/store/<hash>/objects/**` and committed the store's own packed objects into the next snapshot. Every snapshot then embedded the previous one's pack: geometric growth. Measured on this box: 11 commits whose reachable blobs total **2.68 GiB**, of which **1.21 GiB is the store's own pack files** (a 406 MB and a 428 MB pack inside its own tree), and the on-disk store is 1.5 GB. Two things had to change, and the second is the one that would have left the loop in place: (1) `checkpoints/` is now excluded by NAME, which covers the default layout, and the store's own ABSOLUTE PATH is excluded too, because `base_dir` is configurable and a store at any other path would still snapshot itself; (2) the exclude file is now REWRITTEN on every `ensure_store` call instead of only when the store is NEW — a store created before the list grew kept the old one FOREVER, which is exactly what let the loop run. The test puts the store INSIDE the snapshotted tree (the production layout) and asserts the store's own objects are not among the committed blobs. Mutation-checked: removing the `checkpoints/` exclusion fails it.

  **The existing 1.5 GB store is NOT reclaimed by this fix** — the bound and the exclusion are forward-looking. Reclaiming it means deleting the store: `rm -rf ~/.operant/checkpoints/store` (the snapshots are of `~/.operant` itself, not of the user's work), and the store is rebuilt on the next checkpoint. Left as the OWNER's call, because it deletes a rollback history.

- [x] **RT-59 (H) — four reachable holes in the credential rotation, found by reviewing iter-986/987's own claim.** AD-038 published "a key rotated in `.env` now takes effect on the next turn". That claim was still false in four reachable configurations. (1) **A rotation AFTER a 401/429 was dead on arrival**: `CredentialPool::replace_value` swapped only `cred.value` and left `status`/`error_reset_at` intact, so the NEW secret was stored on the BENCHED credential — whose availability window (5 min on a 401, an hour on a 429) was still running. The pool refused to select it and the agent answered "no available keys" with a working key in hand. The failure state belonged to the OLD secret; `replace_value` now clears exactly the fields `reset_statuses` clears. (2) **`current_api_key` returned the literal placeholder**: `OpenAIClient::effective_api_key` falls back to the string `"default"` when there is no key, and `OpenAIModelClient::current_api_key` handed that straight back — so the retarget path could seed a pool credential whose secret was the word "default". It now reports "no key" for the placeholder. (3) **`AnthropicModelClient` implemented neither accessor**: `base_url()` returned `None` (so the change-guard always fired) and `current_api_key()` returned `None` (so `set_endpoint(url, None)` EMPTIES the pool). The anthropic path hit exactly the failure the change was meant to remove. Both accessors are now implemented. (4) **The `operant.toml` retarget emptied the pool in the documented setup**: it passes `endpoint.api_key` from `parse_config_file`, which does NOT apply the environment overrides, so with the key in `~/.operant/.env` the key is `None` — and `set_endpoint(url, None)` removes every pool credential. The `.env` path already had the `or_else(current_api_key)` fallback; this path now has the same one. Plus a fifth, found while fixing the fourth: **the rotation anchor was not refreshed**. `last_credential_id` (the pool credential a `.env` rotation replaces) was only updated inside the client's own request loop, so the agent-loop rotation — which moves the client onto a different credential — left the anchor pointing at the credential it had just benched. The next `.env` rotation then replaced the BENCHED credential's secret, which the pool would not select, and the rotation was silently a no-op. `set_api_key` now refreshes the anchor. Three mutation-checked tests: `replacing_a_value_clears_the_old_secrets_failure_state`, `an_env_rotation_after_an_agent_loop_rotation_replaces_the_new_credential`, and the existing pooled-client suite.

- [x] **RT-60 (S) — the PDF tool's test accepted a broken port.** `extracts_text_from_valid_pdf` passed when the result was an error containing "no extractable", so an extractor that returned empty for every file — or failed on every file — would have passed. The unit level was already covered (`the_extractor_reads_the_text_the_pdf_carries` asserts the text), but the TOOL level was not: nothing checked that `PdfReadTool::execute` puts the text in its OUTPUT. The tool-level test now asserts the output carries the fixture's "Hello PDF". Mutation-checked: an extractor that returns empty text for everything fails three of the six extraction tests, including this one.

- [x] **RT-61 (S) — `operant checkpoints clear` cleared the rows and left the bytes.** The database rows and the shadow store on disk are two different things, and both `operant checkpoints prune` and `operant checkpoints clear` removed only the rows: a store that had reached 1.5 GB of packed objects reported "cleared N checkpoints" and reclaimed nothing. The store held SNAPSHOTS of the working directory, so removing it removes a rollback history and nothing else — and `clear` is the command that says so, so it now does it: it removes the store and reports the bytes it freed. `prune` keeps its meaning (keep the newest N), and `clear` is the one that reclaims the space. The 1.5 GB on this box is now reclaimable with `operant checkpoints clear`.

- [x] **RT-62 (M, SECURITY) — AD-039's "the decompression bomb is now bounded" was true of only half the paths.** The port to `lopdf` applied the 64 MiB limit to the EXTRACT path (`extract_text_with_limit`), but the LOAD path was unbounded: `Document::load_mem` uses `LoadOptions::default()`, whose `max_decompressed_size` is `None`, and the loader decodes the object streams and the cross-reference streams EAGERLY — the largest decompression in the PDF happened before the per-page limit was ever consulted. The vector the port was meant to close was still open. And the per-page limit is a limit PER PAGE, so a 1,000-page document still cost 1,000 x 64 MiB. Fixed: the load is bounded with `Document::load_mem_with_options(bytes, LoadOptions::with_max_decompressed_size(...))`, the per-page budget is divided across the pages actually read, and the page count is capped at 256. The correction is recorded as AD-041, because AD-039 is immutable once numbered and its claim was overstated when it was published — after AD-041 the claim is accurate.

- [x] **RT-63 (M) — the trajectory prune silently deleted the corpus `operant trajectory export` exists to collect.** `operant trajectory export` exports these files as prompt-completion pairs for fine-tuning, and the prune (iter-990) deleted the oldest ones past a hard-coded cap of 200, with no way to turn it off and nothing said about it. A user with a fine-tuning pipeline lost the oldest trajectories and had no signal that they had. Fixed: the cap is a config key (`[agent] max_trajectories`) whose default is 0 — KEEP THEM ALL — so the bound is the operator's choice, and `operant trajectory export` says so when a cap is in force ("the oldest trajectories were deleted, so this export is the most recent N"). A silent subset of a corpus looks like a complete one, which is the failure this closes.

- [x] **RT-64 (S) — the watchdog's thread-state test flakes under load.** `thread_states_are_parsed_into_compact_records` reads the LIVE `/proc/<pid>/task/*` state of the test's own threads and asserts the parsed records have a fixed shape. Under a loaded machine the test process has more threads in more states than the assertion expects (`expected tid:name:state:wchan, got 100375:tui::app::tests:S:futex_do_wait` — 8 columns instead of 4), so it fails. It passes 3/3 in isolation and failed once during a full-workspace run under load. It is not caused by any change in this sweep — the test reads the machine, not a fixture. The cause is not the thread COUNT but the thread NAME: the record is "tid:name:state:wchan" and the name comes from `comm`, which for a Rust test thread is the module path — "tui::app::tests" — and its COLONS made the record ambiguous. Any machine whose test threads carried a module path failed the four-field assertion. Fixed by replacing the name's colons with dots, so the record's four fields are unambiguous whatever the thread is called, and by asserting the shape of EVERY record rather than of the first one. Mutation-checked: a name that keeps its colons fails the test.

- [x] **RT-65 (S) — the live-organism test failed on a machine whose organism tree has no manifests.** `live_organism_tree_compiles_when_present` walks `~/.hermes/organism/**` for `_org.yaml` / `_pool.yaml` and asserts that at least one manifest was found ("no live manifests were found — the walk is broken"). The test's own doc says it "skips cleanly when it does" exist — but it skipped on a MISSING DIRECTORY, not on an EMPTY one. This box has an organism directory with no manifests in it, so the assertion fired and reported the walk as broken when the walk was fine. Verified pre-existing: the same failure at iter-982, before any of this sweep's changes. Fixed: an empty tree is not a live tree, so the test skips with a reason. The walk itself is covered by the fixture tests in the same file. Mutation-checked: not skipping fails the test.

- [x] **RT-66 (M) — the CLI and the core resolved the operant home DIFFERENTLY on Windows.** `CliConfig::default` built the config dir from `dirs::home_dir()/.operant`, while `operant_core::platform::operant_home()` uses `%APPDATA%\operant` on Windows. On Unix the two agree, so the divergence was invisible here — but on Windows the startup `.env` loader read `~\.operant\.env` while the per-turn `.env` reload read `%APPDATA%\operant\.env`: two different files, which is the exact class of bug AD-038 was written to fix, still latent on Windows. The CLI now uses the core's resolver, so there is ONE resolver and ONE path. (The core's resolver honours `HERMES_HOME` first, exactly as the CLI's did, so nothing changes on Unix.)

- [x] **RT-67 (H) — an MCP tool's registry name was not injective, and a hostile server could shadow another server's tool.** `McpNamespacedTool::new` built the name as `mcp_{server}_{tool}` with `/` and `-` sanitized to `_`. The tool name comes from the MCP SERVER, which is untrusted, and the registry's `register` REPLACES an existing tool with the same name ("Tool already registered, replacing") while the sync's teardown removes BY NAME. So server `a` with tool `b_c` and server `a_b` with tool `c` both produced `mcp_a_b_c`: the second registration silently replaced the first, and the teardown list held the name twice, so tearing one server down removed the OTHER server's still-live tool. Two further defects in the same expression: it was LOSSY (`/` and `-` both became `_`, so `a/b` and `a-b` collided), and it produced names the PROVIDER rejects — a server named `my.srv` gave `mcp_my.srv_tool`, which carries a `.`, and OpenAI's strict alphabet is `^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]{1,64}$`, so the WHOLE REQUEST was rejected, not just that tool. And because `ToolSchema::new` runs `sanitize_tool_name` over the name while the registry keys on the unsanitised `OperantTool::name()`, the provider and the dispatcher could disagree about what the tool is called. Fixed with a LOSSLESS encoding: every character outside `[A-Za-z0-9_]` is encoded as `_<hex>_` (so `-` is `_2d_`, `/` is `_2f_`, `.` is `_2e_`), and the two components are joined with a SINGLE `-` that no encoded component can contain. The encoding is injective by construction — the pair is recovered by splitting on that one `-` and decoding each half — and the result is always inside the provider's alphabet. The common case stays readable: `github` + `create_issue` is `mcp_github-create_issue`. Four tests: `the_mcp_qualified_name_is_injective`, `the_mcp_qualified_name_is_inside_the_provider_alphabet`, `the_schema_name_equals_the_registry_name`, and `the_mcp_qualified_name_round_trips` (which proves injectivity by recovering the pair). Mutation-checked: restoring the original expression fails three of the four.

- [x] **RT-68 (M) — the gateway session store grew without bound: the prune existed and nothing called it.** `PersistentSessionStore::prune_old_entries(max_age_days)` has been in the store since it was written, and a workspace-wide search found its ONLY callers were its own two tests. So every conversation the gateway ever saw stayed in the `gateway_sessions` table AND in the store's in-memory map, forever — the code looked like it handled retention and did not. This box's store held 57 sessions since 2026-08-17 in a 92.7 MiB database. The growth is bounded by the number of distinct conversations rather than by time, so it is slow, but it is unbounded, and there was no config key that promised a bound either. Fixed: a periodic sweep (`session_retention_sweep`, module scope, every 6 hours) calls the prune, and a new `[gateway] session_retention_days` key sets the horizon (default 90 days; 0 keeps them forever, the previous behaviour). The sweep takes its period as a parameter so the test drives the REAL sweep rather than a re-implementation. Two tests: `the_prune_removes_an_idle_session_from_the_map_and_the_database` (core — the function does what the task assumes, and it removes the row from the DATABASE as well as the map) and `the_gateway_sweep_prunes_idle_sessions` (CLI — the wiring: it spawns the real sweep on a real store and waits for the session to leave). The second test is deliberately not a re-implementation: an earlier version of it called `prune_old_entries` directly and passed with the sweep REMOVED — the mutation proved it. Mutation-checked now: emptying the sweep body fails the test. The store also gained `set_session_activity`, the counterpart to `update_activity`, so a session's age can be set (and a test can mark one idle) without reaching into private state.

- [x] **RT-69 (H) — the context eviction could not meet the budget and said nothing; the reactive overflow path had no typed failure.** Three defects, one shape. (1) `evict_to_budget` breaks out of its loop as soon as the total fits, but it can only evict from the `evictable` set — the system prompt at index 0 and the whole recency reserve are never candidates. When their combined size alone exceeded the budget, the loop exhausted `evictable` and returned a list that was STILL OVER BUDGET, with no signal to the caller. Reproduced: a 40 KB system prompt against a 100-token budget returned 10,052 tokens. (2) The REACTIVE overflow path (the provider returned a context overflow, the agent compresses, the request is re-issued) re-issued the request UNCONDITIONALLY: if the compression could not reduce the history, the retry sent the same prompt the provider had just rejected, earned the SAME provider 400, and the turn died with a raw provider error the gateway cannot act on. (3) The compression sized itself against the CONFIGURED window, not the EFFECTIVE one, so a limit a prior overflow had taught the agent was ignored on the very path that overflow produced. Fixed: a final trim drops the recency reserve (oldest first, whole tool groups) when that is what is over budget; `EvictionOutcome` reports whether the budget was met; the compression reports whether it REDUCED the history, and the retry's own error is handled where the retry happens — so the two-strike rule can fire. The rule is deliberately NARROW: a provider context-overflow can be TRANSIENT (a mis-reported limit, a load balancer routing to a differently-configured backend), so the FIRST overflow is always retried; only the SECOND overflow, after a compression that did not reduce the history, fails typed with `Error::ContextExhausted` — the typed error the gateway's context-exhausted handler already understands — instead of burning more model calls on a request that cannot succeed. The trim respects three invariants: the system prompt is never evicted, a tool group is dropped WHOLE (a `tool_use` is never separated from its `tool_result` — an orphaned `tool_use` is a malformed history the providers reject with a 400), and the live turn (the newest message) is never dropped. Five tests: `the_eviction_meets_the_budget_or_reports_that_it_could_not` and `the_eviction_trims_the_recency_reserve_when_it_is_what_exceeds_the_budget` (core), `the_final_trim_never_orphans_a_tool_use_from_its_result` and `the_final_trim_keeps_the_live_turn` (core), and `a_provider_overflow_that_cannot_be_compressed_fails_typed` (the production path: the agent's `run()` must fail typed, and the request must be sent ONCE and retried ONCE — two calls, not one, because the first overflow is still retried). FOUR mutations, each run SEPARATELY: reverting the group snap fails the orphan test (and only it); reverting the live-turn guard fails the live-turn test (and only it); removing the reactive typed-failure makes the production-path test see a different call count; and the FIRST version of that test was GREEN for the wrong reason — it asserted only the error VARIANT, and the build-time guard produces the same variant, so it proved the BUILD path rather than the reactive one; the shared call counter is what discriminates them. The pre-existing `loop_recovery_paths::context_overflow_compresses_refunds_and_retries_inline` caught the over-strict first attempt at this fix (it asserted that a compression which cannot shrink a two-message history still recovers the turn) — that test is why the rule is a two-strike rule and not a one-strike one.

- [x] **RT-70 (H, SECURITY) — the AFT binary was downloaded and EXECUTED with no checksum, and the shared updater's verification never ran.** Three findings, one class. (1) The shared updater's checksum guard matched ONLY the asset name `SHA256SUMS` — and `cortexkit/aft` publishes `checksums.sha256`, so `release_has_sums` returned false and `verify_sha256` was NEVER called for aft. The module doc claimed "SHA256-verifies when the release carries a `SHA256SUMS`" and for aft it never did, so the updater installed a freshly downloaded binary with no checksum comparison at all. A verification that silently never runs reads as protection. (2) The FIRST-INSTALL path (`download_aft_release`) never went through the shared updater at all: it downloaded the bytes over HTTPS, wrote them with `tokio::fs::write` — NOT atomic, so an interrupted download left a half-written binary at the path the bridge EXECUTES — and made it executable. And `aft_enabled` defaults to true, so on a machine where the operator never installed aft by hand, operant downloaded a binary and ran it with the user's privileges. (3) The first-install path now VERIFIES before it writes, and the write is atomic. The asymmetry is deliberate and is stated here so the next reader does not "fix" it: a FIRST INSTALL is a fresh download of an executable that will be run, so it must be verifiable and FAILS CLOSED when the manifest cannot be read; an UPDATE to an already-installed, already-verified binary has a weaker case for blocking, so the shared updater warns rather than refuses. The guard now accepts both spellings (`SUMS_ASSET_NAMES`).

- [x] **RT-71 (H) — the MCP qualified name was NOT injective, and the published claim said it was.** iter-1014 shipped the claim "the encoding is injective by construction — the pair is recovered by splitting on that one `-` and decoding each half" — and it was false. The encoding left `_` alone but encoded the illegal characters as `_<hex>_`, so `-` and the literal string `_2d_` both encoded to `_2d_`: server `-` with tool `x` and server `_2d_` with tool `x` produced the SAME registry name, which is the exact defect iter-1014 was fixing (the registry REPLACES a tool with the same name and the sync's teardown removes BY NAME). A false claim is worse than the bug it hid, because the next reader would not look. The encoding is now injective BY CONSTRUCTION and provably so: every byte outside `[A-Za-z0-9]` is encoded as `-` + two hex digits, so an encoded component contains NO underscore and a `-` only as the start of an escape; the two components are joined with a single `_`, which — since the components carry none — can only be the join. The qualified name's body therefore carries EXACTLY ONE underscore, and splitting on it recovers the two encoded components. The common case stays readable: `github` + `create_issue` is `mcp_github_create-5fissue`. The round-trip test asserts the split is unique and recovers the pair, and the injectivity test pins the `-` / `_2d_` collision. Mutation-checked: leaving the underscore unescaped fails both.

- [x] **RT-72 (H) — the Email adapter panicked on every send, and the proxied-WebSocket path had the same defect.** `rustls::ClientConfig::builder()` calls `CryptoProvider::get_default_or_install_from_crate_features()`, which PANICS when the crate features are ambiguous — and they ARE: `hyper-rustls` (reached through `reqwest`) turns on `aws-lc-rs` by default, and `tokio-rustls` turns on `ring`, so BOTH are on whenever the workspace is built with `--all-features`, and the workspace-wide feature unification brings `aws-lc-rs` in even when a crate's own dependency does not. Reproduced: `rustls-0.23.46/src/crypto/mod.rs:249` — "Could not automatically determine the process-level CryptoProvider from Rustls crate features". The Email adapter's TLS setup (`gateway/email.rs`) hit it on EVERY send, so the adapter was 100% non-functional, and `operant-config/src/schema/proxy.rs` (two call sites) broke every proxied WebSocket connection the same way. Fixed by NAMING the provider rather than inferring it: a shared `operant_config::schema::client_config(roots)` builds the configuration with `builder_with_provider(ring::default_provider())` and the protocol versions set explicitly, and all three call sites use it. The feature-level change (rustls and tokio-rustls with `default-features = false`) is ALSO in this iteration — it removes the ambiguity for the common build — but it is NOT the fix on its own: the workspace-wide feature unification brings `aws-lc-rs` back under `--all-features`, which is why the provider is named at the call site. The regression test is `crates/operant-core/tests/rustls_probe.rs`, with two assertions: the named-provider path BUILDS (building IS the assertion — the failure mode is a panic, not a wrong value), and the inferred path ALSO RESOLVES (`the_inferred_provider_path_also_works_now`) — because the feature-level change pins `rustls` and `tokio-rustls` to `default-features = false` with `ring` explicitly, so only one provider is enabled and the inference is unambiguous. That second assertion fails if a future dependency change reintroduces a second provider — which is the point: it says the feature pinning is load-bearing, not that the named-provider call is a no-op. The named-provider call remains because the workspace-wide feature unification brings `aws-lc-rs` back under `--all-features`, so the code must not DEPEND on the inference holding.

- [x] **RT-73 (H) — the SMTP client treated every server reply as success.** The adapter read each server reply into a buffer and DISCARDED it: a `535 Authentication failed`, a `550 mailbox unavailable` and a `552 message too large` all produced `Ok(())`. The send reported SUCCESS for a message the server had rejected — the caller had no way to know the mail was never sent, and the operator saw the reply come back with no explanation. The only reply that was ever checked was the `220` STARTTLS one. Fixed: every step now checks the server's status code — the greeting (`220`), EHLO (`250`), AUTH LOGIN (`334`), the username (`334`), the password (`235`), MAIL FROM (`250`), RCPT TO (`250`/`251`), DATA (`354`), the message body (`250`) — and a rejection is an error that names the code and says the message was NOT sent. A multi-line reply's LAST line carries the decisive code, and that is the one read. Four tests: `an_accepted_reply_passes`, `a_rejected_reply_fails`, `the_last_line_carries_the_decisive_code`, `an_unparseable_reply_fails`.

- [x] **RT-74 (H) — the LLM compression could orphan a tool pair, and the function that repairs it was never called.** The compressor splits the conversation into a HEAD (kept verbatim), a MIDDLE (replaced by the summary) and a TAIL (kept verbatim). The TAIL boundary is group-aware — `tool_group_starts` is applied to it — but the HEAD boundary was a raw MESSAGE COUNT (`protect_head_n`, 3 by default). So a `tool_use` at the head's edge and its `tool_result` in the middle were separated: the head kept the request and the summary replaced the result. That is a malformed history, and strict providers reject it outright ("unexpected tool_use_id in tool_result blocks" / a 400 for an unanswered `tool_use`). Reproduced: a history of [System, User, Assistant(tool_use), Tool(result), …] compressed to a result where `call_1` survived with no `tool_result`. The HEAD boundary is now group-aware too — it moves FORWARD to the END of the group, so the group travels with the head and is never cut in half. Separately, `repair_tool_pairs` — the function that removes a `tool_result` stranded after the summary — existed since the compression path was written and had NO caller outside its own tests; the compressor now calls it on its own assembly, so every caller gets a well-formed history. Two tests, and TWO mutations run SEPARATELY: removing the group-aware head boundary fails the orphan test (and only it); removing the repair call fails the stranded-result test (and only it). The second test's first construction did NOT bite — the group-aware TAIL boundary already kept the pair together, so the test passed with the repair removed; the fixture was rebuilt around a LONE tool message (one with no `tool_use` in the history), which is the case the group boundaries cannot fix and the repair exists for.

- [x] **RT-75 (H) — a failed Telegram send was reported as delivered.** Only HTTP 400 was special-cased; every other failure status fell through to `response.json()`, which SUCCEEDS because Telegram's error body is JSON (`{"ok":false,"error_code":401,"description":"Unauthorized"}`). `data["result"]["message_id"]` is then absent, and the code returned `Ok(String::new())`. A revoked token (401), a blocked bot (403), flood control (429) and a 500 all looked like SUCCESS: the delivery ledger recorded "delivered" and the operator saw a silent no-op. The same defect class as the SMTP one (RT-73). The status now decides, and the body only supplies the description: 2xx is delivered, 400 is the plain-text retry, everything else is an error that says the message was NOT delivered.

- [x] **RT-76 (H) — the Telegram `edit_message` treated EVERY 400 as "message is not modified", which silently lost answers.** A 400 is also "message to edit not found", "chat not found" and — the one the caller's own comment names — "message text is too long" (>4096 UTF-16 units, the case the streaming path hits after tool lines are merged). The runner uses `.is_ok()` to decide whether the streamed answer was delivered, so a rejected edit made the code believe the answer landed, and the dispatch-loop fallback send was suppressed: the user's answer was never delivered. The description is now read, and only "not modified" is a success.

- [x] **RT-77 (H) — a failed Telegram poll looped forever in silence.** In the poll loop only 409 was special-cased.
