A 401, 403, 429 or 5xx took the `Ok(resp)` path, `data["result"]` was absent, so the loop saw an EMPTY update set, slept 2 s and polled again — indefinitely, with nothing logged. A revoked bot token produced a silent permanent 2-second polling loop instead of a loud failure, and a flood-control 429 was re-polled immediately rather than backed off. A non-success status now ends the poll with an error that names the likely cause, and a 429 backs off instead of failing the bot.

- [x] **RT-78 (M) — the LLM compression could orphan a tool pair, and the function that repairs it was never called.** The compressor splits the conversation into a HEAD (kept verbatim), a MIDDLE (replaced by the summary) and a TAIL (kept verbatim). The TAIL boundary is group-aware — `tool_group_starts` is applied to it — but the HEAD boundary was a raw MESSAGE COUNT (`protect_head_n`, 3 by default). So a `tool_use` at the head's edge and its `tool_result` in the middle were separated: the head kept the request and the summary replaced the result. That is a malformed history, and strict providers reject it outright ("unexpected tool_use_id in tool_result blocks" / a 400 for an unanswered `tool_use`). Reproduced: a history of [System, User, Assistant(tool_use), Tool(result), …] compressed to a result where `call_1` survived with no `tool_result`. The HEAD boundary is now group-aware too — it moves FORWARD to the END of the group, so the group travels with the head and is never cut in half. Separately, `repair_tool_pairs` — the function that removes a `tool_result` stranded after the summary — existed since the compression path was written and had NO caller outside its own tests; the compressor now calls it on its own assembly, so every caller gets a well-formed history. Two tests, and TWO mutations run SEPARATELY: removing the group-aware head boundary fails the orphan test (and only it); removing the repair call fails the stranded-result test (and only it). The second test's first construction did NOT bite — the group-aware TAIL boundary already kept the pair together, so the test passed with the repair removed; the fixture was rebuilt around a LONE tool message (one with no `tool_use` in the history), which is the case the group boundaries cannot fix and the repair exists for.

- [x] **RT-79 (M) — a TUI test could poison the process-global reasoning-mode cell for every test that ran after it.** The override cell is process-global; the tests that write it take a `reasoning_override_test_guard()` mutex and reset the cell BY HAND, after their assertions. A test that PANICS before the reset skips it, so the cell keeps the value the failing test set — and the next test that expects a different mode sees it. That is how a suite that passes in isolation fails under load, with a failure that reads like a product bug ("the thinking did not render") and is not one: it was observed in this sweep as a full-suite failure that passed 3/3 in isolation. **The diagnosis took a tracer, and the leak was NOT the one the row first described.** Three separate defects were in play, and the row's first fix only addressed one of them:

1. **A test that PANICS before its manual reset leaks the cell.** The reset is after the assertions, so a failing assertion skips it, and the cell keeps the value the failing test set. Fixed: the guard now restores the cell on DROP, so every exit path — including a panic — leaves the snapshot default.

2. **The fix's OWN test had the defect.** It set the cell to `Off` BEFORE taking the guard, so the write landed while another test held the guard and expected `Full`. The write now happens INSIDE the guard.

3. **The actual flake: a test that dispatches a key event which flips the cell, WITHOUT taking the guard.** `global_bindings_should_fire_their_action_behaviourally` and `a_modified_binding_must_not_fire_on_the_bare_key` iterate over the drifted key bindings, and one of them is Ctrl+T — the thinking toggle, which writes the process-global cell. Neither took the guard. So the write landed in the middle of a parallel test that expected a different mode, and that test failed with "the thinking did not render" — a message that reads like a product bug and is not one. Both now take the guard.

**The method matters and is worth recording.** The first two were found by reading the code. The third was found only by a TRACER: a `eprintln!` in the setter that printed the mode, the thread, and the TEST NAME, and the test harness's own thread names (which libtest sets) identified the writer. Reading the code said every writer was guarded — because a search for "the setter is called" does not say "and the CALLER holds the guard". The tracer is what found it, after the obvious suspects were all cleared.

**Evidence:** 5 failures in 20 runs before the fix, 0 in 20 after. The failure is order-dependent and load-dependent, so it never reproduced in isolation — which is why it survived the earlier sweeps.

**Two tests pin the guard's contract:** `the_guard_restores_the_cell_when_it_is_dropped` (the restore happens on drop) and `the_guard_excludes_another_thread` (a second thread cannot take the guard while it is held — the mutation that removes the restore fails the first, and the second is what proves the serialisation is real rather than assumed).

- [x] **RT-80 (M) — dropping an MCP stdio transport left the server subprocess running as an orphan.** tokio's `kill_on_drop` defaults to FALSE, and the only path that killed the child was `kill_child` — which is called on the RECONNECT path, not on drop. There is no `impl Drop` for the stdio transport. So every transport dropped without an explicit `disconnect()` (a failed connect, a manager teardown, the agent shutting down) left a live MCP server process behind, holding its stdin/stdout pipes. The spawn now sets `kill_on_drop(true)`. The test took four attempts to become non-vacuous, and each failure is worth recording: the shared fake server reads `for line in sys.stdin:`, so it EXITS on stdin EOF — and the transport's pipes close when it is dropped, so the server died of its own accord and the test passed with the fix REMOVED. The fixture needed a server that ignores stdin EOF and sleeps forever; then the handshake turned out to be TWO round trips (`initialize` and `tools/list`), and a server that answered only the first left the client waiting on the second. Only after all three did the mutation fail, with the subprocess still alive: "the MCP server subprocess 1388833 must not outlive the transport".

- [x] **RT-81 (H) — a `transport = "streamable-http"` MCP server could not connect at all.** `McpTransportKind::StreamableHttp` was routed to the plain-HTTP client in all four places that build the registry (`main.rs`, `cmd_mcp.rs`, `mcp_serve.rs`, the TUI's re-add path). The plain-HTTP client speaks the pre-2025-06-18 transport, and it differs from the streamable one in three ways, each of which is fatal on its own: (1) it sends no `Accept` header, and a spec-compliant streamable server answers `406 Not Acceptable` to a request that does not offer `text/event-stream`; (2) it never captures or echoes the `Mcp-Session-Id`, so every request starts a NEW session and the server forgets the initialized state; (3) it cannot read an SSE-framed response. The failure message ("MCP request failed: 406 Not Acceptable") did not name the transport as the cause. The core's `McpClient` now has a streamable mode that sends `Accept: application/json, text/event-stream`, captures the session id and echoes it, and reads the payload out of an SSE stream. The plain-HTTP transport is UNCHANGED — it predates the revision, and some old servers reject an `Accept` they do not understand. A CORRECT Streamable-HTTP implementation also exists in the tree (`crates/operant-tools/src/mcp_transport.rs`, with the `Accept` and `Mcp-Session-Id` constants) and has no production caller — it is a separate, parallel transport stack with its own config type, not wired to the core's manager. Two mutations, run separately: dropping the `Accept` header fails the handshake with "MCP request failed: 501 Not Implemented"; dropping the session echo fails it with "MCP response ID mismatch: expected 2, got 1" (the second request starts a new session, so the server answers the OLD id).

- [x] **RT-82 (M) — every MCP transport advertised `sampling` and `roots`, and two of the three cannot honour them.** The stdio transport reads the server's messages off the same stream as the responses, so it can spot a server-initiated request (one with a `method` field rather than a `result`) and answer it. The HTTP path has no such branch: it parses every body as the response to its own request, so a `sampling/createMessage` would be read as a response with a mismatched id, the call would fail with "MCP response ID mismatch", and the server's request would never be answered — so it would block waiting for a reply. The SSE path logs it and moves on ("server-initiated request (not handled)"). Advertising a capability the transport cannot answer is worse than not advertising it: the server is TOLD it may ask, and then the request hangs. The advertised capabilities now depend on the transport — the stdio one advertises `sampling` and `roots`, the HTTP and SSE ones do not. The mutation (every transport advertises `sampling`) fails the test with "the Http transport cannot answer a sampling request, so it must not advertise one".

- [x] **RT-83 (H) — the token estimate was blind to the tool-call arguments and the reasoning, so the eviction was blind to the largest messages.** `estimate_message_tokens` charged `4 + content/4`. A tool-call turn's `content` is EMPTY BY CONSTRUCTION — `run.rs` suppresses the text so the provider sees a pure tool-call turn — and the payload is in `tool_calls[].function.arguments`. So a `write_file` call with a 100 KB body was estimated at 4 tokens and ~25 000 real tokens, and the reasoning (which IS serialised back to the provider on the next request) was not counted at all. Every budget decision in the system goes through this estimate: the eviction, the `ContextExhausted` guard, the output-token fitter, the learned limit, and the build-time threshold checks. So a turn that accumulated a few large tool arguments was estimated as tiny, nothing was evicted, and the request the provider rejected was over-budget by an amount the estimator never saw. The estimate now covers the tool name, the call id, the arguments, and the reasoning. The mutation (not counting the arguments) fails the test with "a tool turn carrying 40 KB of arguments must not be estimated at 4 tokens — got 4". The fix also RECALIBRATED one test fixture: the semantic-compaction fixture's budget was 60, chosen against the old (blind) counts, and its tool group cost 20 then and 29 now. The budget is a property of the fixture AND the estimator, so it moved to 63 — the window where the recency cut still drops the goal fact and the semantic cut still keeps it. Two pre-existing tests failed until it was recomputed, and the recomputation is derived from the corrected counts, not chosen to make them pass: the cumulative costs are [11, 22, 30, 37, 44, 55, 71, 82, 94, 100], the recency cut needs 55..70 to drop the fact, and the semantic cut needs 63 to hold the system group + the goal group + the opening question + the tool group. A displacement rule ("a more relevant group may evict a cheaper, less relevant one") was also TRIED here and REMOVED: it never fired on the fixture at any budget, so it was speculative complexity with no test able to prove it.

- [x] **RT-84 (H) — a JSON-RPC ERROR from an MCP SSE server was handed back as a successful result.** The SSE reader did `parsed.get("result").or_else(|| parsed.get("error"))` and sent whichever it found, so a server that rejected a `tools/call` returned the ERROR OBJECT as a successful result — and the caller treated the error payload as the tool's output. The failure was reported as a success. The reader now distinguishes them, and the error goes back as an error carrying the server's code and message ("MCP error -32602: tool not found"). The pending-response map carries a `Result` rather than a bare value, so the error cannot be laundered into a result at the type level. The mutation (hand the error back as the result) fails the test with "a JSON-RPC error must not be a successful result: Object {\"code\": Number(-32602), ...}". Fixed in iter-1032.

- [x] **RT-85 (M) — the shared updater picked the aft asset by luck of the release's asset order, and it installed an UNVERIFIED download without saying so.** Two defects in the same function. (1) `pick_asset` takes the FIRST asset whose name contains the marker, and the aft release carries BOTH `aft-linux-x64` (a raw binary) and `ck-aft-linux-x64.zip` (an archive) — both contain the marker `linux-x64` and both pass the shape filter. Which one was picked depended on the ASSET LIST'S ORDER, which upstream happens to keep alphabetical. A reorder upstream would silently switch the install from the raw binary to the archive. The raw binary now wins: it is the unambiguous form, it needs no extraction, and the manifest lists it by the same name the asset carries. (2) When the release carries NO checksum manifest, the verification is skipped and the bytes are installed — SILENTLY. Installing anyway is deliberate (refusing would strand the operator on an old version if upstream stopped publishing checksums), but "a verification that silently never runs reads as protection" is the defect class, so the updater now warns, naming the release and saying the download is not verified. The mutation (first match wins again) fails only the raw-binary test.

- [x] **RT-86 (M, FEATURE GAP) — the Slack adapter is OUTBOUND-ONLY, and nothing said so.** The adapter's own doc claimed "Socket Mode — authenticated by the bot token over the WebSocket", and AGENTS.md said "Slack: fully implemented (Socket Mode + Web API)". Neither was true: `start()` logs "event-based, no polling" and returns, and NOTHING opens a `wss://` connection or reads the Events API. The `handle_update` parser is DEAD CODE — the TRAIT's `handle_update` method has ZERO production callers for ANY adapter (RT-120): Telegram and Discord receive through their own poll loops and the STATIC parsers, and the webhook adapter through its own HTTP handler, so the trait method the Slack adapter implements is never invoked. So a Slack message never reached the agent, while the gateway reported the adapter as "enabled" and the operator had no way to know. Telegram and Discord RECEIVE; Slack, WhatsApp, Email and SMS do not. The fix here is to make the truth visible: the adapter's doc says OUTBOUND-ONLY, `config_json` reports `"inbound": false, "outbound": true"`, AGENTS.md is corrected, and a test pins the report. **And the first fix was incomplete in a way that made the claim false in PRACTICE:** the field lived in the adapters' `config_json()`, and NOTHING read it — `Gateway::status()` and `get_platform_status` had been DELETED for having zero external callers (iter-151), and `operant gateway status` printed "enabled" and nothing more. So the operator still saw "Slack: enabled" and believed they could receive Slack messages. Fixed in iter-1087: the gateway has ONE table of the inbound-capable platforms, the four adapters read it, and `operant gateway status` PRINTS it — "Slack … send-only (no inbound)" against "Telegram … receives messages". **Implementing Socket Mode is a FEATURE, not a patch** — the `apps.connections.open` handshake, the WebSocket, the reconnect and the event routing — and it is left as the owner's call, tracked here. The same honesty applies to WhatsApp, Email and SMS: their `handle_update` parsers are also uncalled, and their AGENTS.md entry already said OUTBOUND-ONLY.

**The class is FOUR adapters, not one.** `PlatformAdapter::handle_update` is called from NOWHERE in the workspace — the Telegram and Discord poll loops use their own STATIC parsers (`TelegramAdapter::parse_update`), and the webhook adapter has its own HTTP handler. So the trait method is dead for all seven adapters, and the four that implement a complete inbound parser — **Slack, WhatsApp, Email, SMS** — never receive anything. All four now carry `"inbound": false` in their status report, and ONE test covers all four (`every_outbound_only_adapter_says_it_cannot_receive`), so a fifth outbound-only adapter added without the field fails it. Mutation-checked: making the email adapter claim it can receive fails the test.

**What the audit got WRONG, recorded so it is not re-litigated:** it reported that the Discord gateway does not handle the server-requested HEARTBEAT (opcode 1) and that the socket is therefore dropped repeatedly. **It does handle it** — `discord.rs` spawns a heartbeat task that sends opcode 1 on the interval the server asked for. The audit misread the receive loop's `_ => unknown opcode` arm.
- [x] **RT-87 (M, REDUNDANCY) — a COMPLETE, SUPERSEDED MCP implementation sits in `operant-tools` with no production caller.** Six modules — `mcp_protocol`, `mcp_transport`, `mcp_client`, `mcp_deferred`, `mcp_tool`, `tool_search` — 3 232 lines, and a workspace-wide search found NO caller outside `operant-tools` itself. The production path is `operant-core/src/mcp.rs` (3 176 lines) plus `operant-core/src/tools/{tool_search,mcp_tool}.rs`; the `tool_search` hits outside `operant-tools` are the CORE's module, a different one. The duplicate is the OLDER implementation: it pins `MCP_PROTOCOL_VERSION = "2024-11-05"` (the core speaks `2025-06-18`) and it does not advertise `sampling`/`roots` at all. So the two stacks have DIVERGED — and that divergence is exactly the defect class: the Streamable-HTTP gap (RT-81) had to be fixed in the core, and the copy in `operant-tools` was never consulted even though it HAD a Streamable-HTTP implementation (the `Accept` header and the `Mcp-Session-Id` handling), in an older form. Two MCP implementations mean a fix to one can silently not apply to the other. The duplicate is to be DELETED — it is superseded, not merged. (Recorded; the deletion is its own iteration.)

- [x] **RT-88 (M, REDUNDANCY) — `operant-tools` is a 30 860-line crate that the product calls ONCE.** The crate is a NON-OPTIONAL dependency of `operant-core` and `operant-cli` (so all of it is compiled into every binary) and declares 55 public modules. A workspace-wide search for `operant_tools::` outside the crate itself found exactly ONE reference: `crates/operant-core/src/doctor.rs` calling `operant_tools::cli_discovery::discover_cli_tools(&[], &[])` — and even that passes EMPTY arguments. The CLI references the crate ZERO times while declaring it as a dependency and enabling `browser-native` and `rag-pdf` from it. The crate's own README says it holds "the concrete tools that agents can call", but the agents call the CORE's tools: `operant-core/src/tools/` carries its own `browser_tool.rs`, `file_tools.rs`, `http_tool.rs`, `web_search_tool.rs` and the rest, and the core's `builtin.rs` registers THOSE. So the crate is a PARALLEL implementation of the same tools, kept in step by hand — which is exactly the divergence that cost the Streamable-HTTP fix (RT-81) its reference implementation. Two further symptoms: the `rag-pdf` feature (in `operant-cli`'s DEFAULT feature list) compiles the `lopdf` dependency and ships a 693-line `pdf_read` module whose tool is registered NOWHERE — so the feature costs build time and buys no tool; and `file_write`/`file_edit`/`browser` are duplicated three ways. The crate is `publish = false`, so it is not a public API and nothing external depends on it. The MCP subset was deleted in iter-1037 (RT-87); the rest is a larger removal and is tracked here.

- [x] **RT-89 (H, FEATURE GAP) — 43 923 lines of provider, memory and session implementations are compiled into every binary and the product calls almost none of it.** The pattern RT-87 and RT-88 found in `operant-tools` repeats across three more crates, and it is the same shape: a parallel implementation library, kept in step by hand, that the product barely reaches. **Measured by an exact module-path search from outside each crate:**

| crate | lines | reachable | what the live call is |
|---|---|---|---|
| `operant-providers` | 30 390 | 19 of 21 modules unreachable | ONE call: `doctor.rs` → `create_provider(name, None)`, to VALIDATE a provider name |
| `operant-memory` | 11 324 | 0 | nothing — the core's `build_memory_provider` IGNORES every provider name but memory-wire, and warns that the key is ignored |
| `operant-infra` | 2 270 | 0 | nothing — the core carries its own `session` module |

**What this means, concretely — and what it does NOT mean.** The core's provider routing (`create_model_client`) sends every non-anthropic provider to the OpenAI-compatible client. That is CORRECT for the OpenAI-compatible family, and it is what `[providers]` profiles are FOR: a profile is a base_url + key, so deepseek, ollama, openrouter and llama.cpp all work. So the provider ROUTING is not broken. What `operant-providers` holds is a separate, unwired capability: implementations against the NATIVE APIs (gemini, bedrock, azure) that no configuration can reach — a `[providers]` profile for gemini is a base_url, and the OpenAI-compatible client would talk to it. There is no configuration that selects the native implementation.

The memory side is HONEST and is not part of this finding: the core's `build_memory_provider` ignores every provider name but memory-wire AND warns, and the config's own doc and `operant.example.toml` say so ("the old provider choices are accepted-with-warning in existing configs and ignored"). A known-but-ignored key with a warning is the right design. `operant-memory` still holds 11 324 lines of backend implementations (postgres, qdrant, lucid, markdown) that no configuration can select.

`operant-infra`'s session backends are likewise replaced by the core's own `session` module.

So the gap is narrower than it first looks, and the honest statement is: **three crates hold implementations for capabilities that no configuration can reach** — native-API providers, non-memory-wire memory backends, and the infra session backends.

**This is a FEATURE GAP wearing a redundancy's clothes.** The implementations are not junk — they are real, and they are what a provider-agnostic operant would use. What is missing is the WIRING: the core's routing does not dispatch to them, so the product's configuration surface promises capabilities it does not deliver. Three honest resolutions, and the choice is the owner's: (a) wire the routing so the configured provider is the provider used — the implementations are already there; (b) delete the unreachable implementations and narrow the configuration surface to what works, so no configuration is silently ignored; (c) split the crates out of the binary's dependency graph so they are available to build on but not compiled in. **(b) is the one that removes the silent-ignore behaviour, and it is the cheapest.** Nothing here is deleted in this iteration — the row records the measurement and the options.

- [x] **RT-90 (S) — a published API's doc claimed three callers it did not have.** `operant_providers::default_provider_config` was documented as "One shared function — gateway onboarding pre-fill, CLI wizard prompt pre-fill, and any future caller all consume this so the surfaces can't drift." It had ZERO callers outside its own crate: a workspace-wide search for the name found only its definition. So the drift the sentence was written to prevent was the one already present — the surfaces do not share it, because none of them uses it. The function is the only place the per-provider defaults (`default_base_url`, `default_temperature`, `default_max_tokens`, `default_timeout_secs`, `default_wire_api`) are read, and the TUI's provider screen exists (`tui/app/providers.rs`) — so the fix is to WIRE it, not to delete it. The doc now says what is true and points at RT-89, which records the wider finding: 43 923 lines of provider, memory and session implementations that no configuration can reach.

- [x] **RT-91 (H, DEPLOYMENT) — the installer's ONNX Runtime copy left a STALE symlink, and the installed binary could not start.** `scripts/install.sh` copies the runtime library with `cp -a`, which PRESERVES symlinks, into a directory that may already hold a `libonnxruntime.so.1` symlink from an EARLIER install pointing at a DIFFERENT version. The copy left the old link in place, the dynamic loader followed it, and every invocation of the installed binary died with `libonnxruntime.so.1: version 'VERS_1.24.4' not found (required by .../operant)`. It was not hypothetical: this machine's `~/.local/lib/operant/libonnxruntime.so.1` pointed at `1.20.1` while the binary needs `1.24.4`, and the binary could not start at all — which is what made the TUI corpus report "0 gated" and 70 drifts, a number that looked like a regression and was not one. The fix removes the destination's ONNX Runtime files and symlinks BEFORE copying, and then recreates `libonnxruntime.so.1` to point at the version that was just copied. The same trap is the reason the corpus's GATED COUNT is the tell: a binary that cannot start is a binary that cannot answer, and the drift list that follows is an artifact of that.

- [x] **RT-92 (M, DEAD WIRING) — the CLI compiled the `postgres` dependency for a crate it never called, through a CI feature that no CI runs.** `operant-cli` declared `operant-memory` as an optional dependency and defined `memory-postgres = ["operant-memory/memory-postgres"]`, which pulls the `postgres` crate. But a workspace-wide search for `operant_memory::` found ZERO uses outside the crate itself — the CLI's source never touches it. The only way to compile it was `ci-all`, a meta-feature referenced by NO workflow and NO script (the GitHub Actions quota is exhausted, so there is no CI to run it). So a database driver was compiled for a crate nothing called, in a feature nobody asked for. The CLI's `operant-memory` dependency, the `memory-postgres` feature and the `ci-all` meta-feature are all removed. The `operant-memory` CRATE is kept — it holds the postgres/qdrant/lucid backends, and RT-89 records that no configuration can select them; whether to wire or to remove that capability is the owner's call, not a dead-wiring cleanup's.

- [x] **RT-93 (M, ORPHAN CRATE + CAPABILITY GAP) — `operant-infra` is an orphan crate, and it holds a gateway capability the gateway does not have.** No crate depends on `operant-infra`: it is 2 270 lines compiled into the workspace and reachable by nothing. Its README says it holds "cross-cutting utilities used by several channel implementations" — and no channel implementation uses them. But the two capabilities it DOES hold are real, and NEITHER is reachable: **`MessageDebouncer`** (accumulate rapid successive messages per sender within a window and emit them as one concatenated message, so a fast typist gets one agent run instead of three) and **`StallWatchdog`** (detect a WebSocket channel transport going idle beyond a threshold, and fire a caller-supplied callback). There is no config key for either, so they are not "off by default" — they are unreachable. A search of `operant-core/src/gateway/` finds no debouncing and no coalescing: `route_message` hands every inbound message straight to the handler, so three quick messages produce three turns and three model calls.

Two honest notes on what wiring them would mean. The debouncer CHANGES a user-visible behaviour (three messages become one turn), so it needs a config key and a default the operator chooses — it is not a drop-in. And the stall watchdog's doc says it is for "WebSocket-based channels" — the transports RT-86 records as not existing (Slack Socket Mode, the Discord gateway) — so it becomes relevant only if one of those is implemented.

The crate is kept, not deleted, because deleting it would remove the only implementation of those two behaviours — the fix is to WIRE them, which is the owner's call (the same call as RT-89's). The crate's own `SessionStore`/`SqliteSessionBackend` ARE superseded by the core's `session` module, so those two modules are the redundant part.

- [x] **RT-94 (H) — the doctor reported a provider as "valid" that the runtime cannot serve.** `operant doctor` validates a provider NAME against `operant-providers`' factory, which knows twelve providers. But the runtime has exactly TWO model clients — `AnthropicModelClient` (Anthropic's Messages API) and `OpenAIModelClient` (chat completions) — and its provider routing sends every non-anthropic provider to the OpenAI-compatible one. **Bedrock speaks AWS's Converse API** (`POST /model/{id}/converse` with `{"modelId", "messages"}` and SigV4 signing), so a Bedrock configuration passed the name check, the doctor printed `provider "bedrock" is valid`, and every request would have been sent as a chat-completions POST to an endpoint that cannot parse one. Bedrock is in the TUI's provider catalogue, so an operator could select it. The root of the misreport was the `Provider` trait's `default_wire_api`, which DEFAULTS to `"chat_completions"` — and bedrock, gemini and anthropic all took that default while speaking something else. Fixed on both sides: the three non-chat-completions providers now declare their real wire API (`converse`, `generate_content`, `messages` — the constants are in `operant-api`), and the doctor compares the declared wire API against the two the runtime speaks and reports an ERROR when they do not match, for the default provider, the fallback chain, and the model routes. The mutation (treat every provider as reachable) fails the test.

- [x] **RT-95 (H) — the TUI told the operator "Connected to AWS Bedrock." while the agent kept talking to the PREVIOUS provider.** `connect_endpoint` returns the provider's catalogue `default_base_url` — or `None` when there is no usable HTTP endpoint. Five providers have no usable one: `bedrock` and `azure-foundry` carry an EMPTY URL, and `azure` carries a `{resource}` template that is not a URL until it is filled in. A `None` endpoint means `retarget_endpoint` is skipped, so `client.base_url` keeps its old value — the agent keeps sending to the previous provider while the model name changes. The operator was told "Connected to AWS Bedrock." and nothing was different. A virtual provider (`acp://`, `cloudcode-pa://`) legitimately has no single endpoint, so the fix distinguishes the two: a provider whose catalogue URL is HTTP-but-unusable NEEDS an endpoint and now says so ("AWS Bedrock, but it has no endpoint URL in the catalogue — the agent is still using the previous provider's endpoint"), while a virtual provider still reports a plain connection. A SECOND defect surfaced while writing the test: the status message was set BEFORE `persist_provider_and_model`, and the persist can call `set_status_notice` — which OVERWRITES it. So a persist failure replaced the message the operator needed with "Could not persist model: ...". The message is now set LAST, so the outcome of the switch is the final word. The mutation (treat every no-endpoint provider as virtual) fails the test.

- [x] **RT-96 (H) — `[[model_routes]]` is a documented feature that does not work.** The config's own doc says: "Route a task hint to a specific provider + model… Usage: pass `hint:reasoning` as the model parameter to route the request." The resolution IS implemented — `operant-providers/src/router.rs` strips the `hint:` prefix and looks the hint up. But the ROUTER IS NEVER CALLED: `ProviderRouter` and `create_routed_provider` have no consumer outside `operant-providers`, and the runtime's `self.model()` returns `config.agent.model` verbatim. So an operator who follows the documented usage writes `model = "hint:reasoning"` in their config, the string is sent to the provider AS THE MODEL NAME, and the provider rejects it. A workspace-wide search for `model_routes` and `embedding_routes` finds only the config's own validation, the doctor's checks, and the doc comments — nothing that routes a request. This is the sharpest instance of the RT-89 pattern: not merely an unreachable implementation, but a documented usage that produces a broken request.

**The fix resolves the hint where the model name becomes a request.** `ProvidersConfig` gained `model_route_for_hint` and `is_model_hint`, and the CLI's client construction resolves the hint BEFORE the provider is inferred from the model name — a `hint:<name>` model now becomes the route's provider and model. An UNRESOLVABLE hint is now an ERROR ("no `[[model_routes]]` entry has that hint") rather than a model name sent to the provider, so a typo produces a clear message instead of an API 404. The mutation (do not resolve the hint) fails the resolution test and leaves the other two green.

**The embedding side is the same defect and is recorded here rather than as a separate row.** `[[embedding_routes]]` is documented the same way (`[memory] embedding_model = "hint:semantic"`), and `memory.embedding_model` has a default and NO consumer outside the doctor — so the documented embedding route does not work either. Unlike the memory PROVIDER (which warns that it is ignored), this key is silently accepted. The two need the same treatment: resolve the hint, or warn that the key is ignored.

- [x] **RT-97 (M) — the DOCUMENTED config examples for the model routes were rejected by the config.** `operant-config`'s doc comments showed `[[model_routes]]` and `[[embedding_routes]]` at the TOP level and `[memory] embedding_model = "hint:semantic"`. The routes live under `[providers]`, and the top-level form is refused by the config's `deny_unknown_fields`: `operant doctor --config` answered `unknown field 'model_routes', expected one of 'version', 'client', 'proxy', ...` — the whole config would not load. `[memory] embedding_model` is refused the same way (`unknown field 'embedding_model'`), because the core's `MemorySettings` has no such field at all. So an operator who copied the documented example got a config that failed to start, with an error naming a field the documentation had just told them to write. The doc examples are corrected to the form that loads, and TWO tests now pin it: `the_documented_providers_model_routes_example_loads` parses the documented TOML through the real deserializer, and `a_top_level_model_routes_table_is_rejected` pins that the old form is still refused. The lesson is the one this whole ledger is about: a documentation example is a CLAIM, and it needs the same evidence as any other — a test that parses it.

- [x] **RT-98 (M) — the test suite's environment locks did not exclude each other, and one poisoned lock took four tests down with it.** The process environment is global, and `get_default_operant_root()` computes the operant home from the CURRENT `HERMES_HOME` and `HOME` — so an interleaved mutation of either changes the root mid-test. Each module had its OWN `env_lock()` (a per-module `OnceLock<Mutex<()>>`), so the modules did NOT exclude each other. The observed symptom: `profile.rs`'s `test_create_and_delete_profile` failed with `Profile 'testprofile' does not exist` — the profile was created under one root and deleted under another — and then THREE more tests in the same module failed with `PoisonError`, because the first failure poisoned that module's lock and every later `env_lock().lock().unwrap()` then panicked. Two defects, both fixed: (1) the environment lock is now CRATE-WIDE (`crate::platform::test_env_lock`), so every test in the crate that mutates the environment excludes every other; (2) it is poison-tolerant (`unwrap_or_else(PoisonError::into_inner)` — the repo's own convention, used in seven other places), so a test that panics while holding it does not take the rest of the environment tests down with it. The per-module locks in `profile.rs`, `config.rs` and `browser_provider.rs` are gone. **The diagnosis took a TRACER and a loop, not a reading** — the failure appeared in roughly one full-suite run in six, and only under the suite's concurrency. The first captured failure was a DIFFERENT test (the checkpoint one), which is the same class: a test that depends on a process-global and can be disturbed by another.

**A THIRD flake, of the same shape, surfaced in the next loop: the webhook tests' ephemeral port.** Both chose their port by binding a probe socket on port 0 and DROPPING it, then binding the adapter to that port — a TOCTOU race, because another test's ephemeral bind can take the port in between. The symptom was `AddrInUse` — "the webhook adapter cannot start" — which reads as a product failure and is a harness race. Both now try a few ports. The retry is on the HARNESS side only; the adapter's behaviour is unchanged.

**A FOURTH flake, the same shape with a different global: the WRITE ORIGIN.** `WRITE_ORIGIN` is a process-global `RwLock<String>`, and `is_background_review()` reads it whenever the caller is not inside a scoped tokio task — which is most tests. The memory-tools test did not take the origin lock, so a test elsewhere that had set the origin to "background_review" made the memory store REFUSE there ("Refused: background review does not write memory; the user saves it in a turn"), and the failure appeared in roughly one full-suite run in ten. The test now pins the origin it expects, under the origin lock, using the `WriteOriginGuard` — **not** a bare `set_write_origin`, because the token has no `Drop` and a bare set would leak "user" into the global, which is the very leak being fixed.

**The CLI had the same shape, and worse: TWO per-module locks (`config.rs`'s and the TUI's `system_glyph_env_lock`), and the TUI's app tests had NO lock at all** while removing `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and `OPENAI_API_KEY` — the two keys the config's provider resolution reads. The CLI now has ONE crate-wide lock (`crate::config::test_env_lock`) and the app tests take it.

**And the locks are ASYNC-AWARE.** A `std::sync::MutexGuard` held across an `.await` is a `!Send` hazard, and the browser tests bracket their await with the mutation — so the env lock is a `tokio::sync::Mutex`, with a `blocking_lock` accessor for the synchronous tests. A `write_origin.rs` test carried an `#[expect(clippy::await_holding_lock)]` for exactly this; it is now unfulfilled and REMOVED, because the hazard is gone. The origin lock got the same treatment.

**And the POISON cascade is the general lesson: one failed test became four.** A mutex poisoned by a panicking holder is a normal thing, and `.lock().unwrap()` turns it into a failure in every LATER test that takes that mutex. The repo's own convention is `unwrap_or_else(PoisonError::into_inner)` — used in seven other places — and the env locks used `.unwrap()`. All the env locks in `operant-core`, `operant-cli`, `operant-memory` and `operant-providers` are now poison-tolerant.

- [x] **RT-99 (H, TEST HYGIENE) — the test suite had been WRITING INTO THE OPERATOR'S REAL `~/.operant/profiles/` since at least August.** The profile tests set `HERMES_HOME` to a temp directory and then create and delete profiles under it. But `get_default_operant_root()` computes the home from the CURRENT `HERMES_HOME` and `HOME` — and the environment is process-global, so a concurrent mutation anywhere in the crate could make it resolve to the real home instead. The evidence is on this machine: `~/.operant/profiles/source/operant.toml` and `~/.operant/profiles/target/operant.toml` contained EXACTLY `test_clone_profile`'s content (`[agent]\nmodel = "gpt-4"\n`), dated **2026-08-12** — months before this sweep. The residue has been removed; the operator's real profiles directory is empty again. Two fixes, and the second is the one that matters: (1) the environment lock is crate-wide and poison-tolerant (RT-98), so the tests that mutate the environment exclude each other; (2) **the profile tests now ASSERT the resolved root before they write anything** — `use_temp_home` sets `HERMES_HOME` and then checks that `get_default_operant_root()` IS the temp directory, so a test that cannot prove it is writing to its own sandbox refuses to write at all. A test that might touch the operator's real state must prove it will not, before it acts. The symptom that exposed it was `test_clone_profile` failing with "Profile 'target' already exists at /home/ishanp/.operant/profiles/target" — the test was colliding with ITS OWN residue from a previous run.

- [x] **RT-100 (H) — the Slack and Discord adapters DISCARDED the API's response, so every failed send was recorded as delivered.** `SlackAdapter::send_message` and `DiscordAdapter::send_message` both ended with `client.post(...).send().await?;` — the `Response` was dropped without being read. A revoked token (401), a bot without access to the channel (403), a rate limit (429) and a message over the limit (400) are all real failures, and the delivery ledger recorded "delivered" while the operator saw a silent no-op. This is the same defect class as the SMTP reply discarding (RT-73) and the Telegram status (RT-71) — and the last two adapters to be checked. **Slack is the subtler one: Slack answers a rejected request with HTTP 200 and `{"ok": false, "error": "not_in_channel"}`**, so a status check alone does NOT catch it — a bot that is not in the channel, or a message that violates a workspace rule, is the COMMON case. Both adapters now check: Slack reads the body and rejects `ok: false` by name; Discord checks the status. **The check is a PURE function over the response** — `slack_send_outcome(status, body)` and `discord_send_outcome(status, body)` — so the tests need no server. That was not the first design: the tests originally drove the adapters against a local HTTP server, and they were UNRUNNABLE together. Three separate races, each found by running the pair:
1. The runtime config is a process-global, so two tests that pointed the API base at different servers raced — each got the OTHER server's answer.
2. A lock that spans the request await did not fix it (a `tokio::sync::Mutex` held across an await in a current-thread runtime, with each test on its own runtime, did not exclude them).
3. With the port race removed, the OS reused the freed ephemeral port, and the process-global `reqwest` client's connection POOL reused the stale keep-alive connection — so the second test's request went to the first test's server.

**The lesson: the transport is standard; the DECISION is the defect. Test the decision.** The same shape as the MCP SSE error (RT-84), where the pure function is `sse_response_payload`. Five tests, mutation-checked with clean attribution: removing Slack's `ok: false` check fails only `a_slack_send_rejected_with_ok_false_is_a_failure`; removing Discord's status check fails only `a_discord_send_the_api_rejects_is_a_failure`; the acceptance tests stay green in both cases.

- [x] **RT-101 (M) — the delivery ledger recorded only REPLIES, so every proactive send was invisible to the operator.** `Gateway::send_to_platform` — the PROACTIVE path: a scheduled message, a cron push, a permission notice, a gateway event, a mirror target — never wrote to the ledger. Only `handle_with_lifecycle`, the REPLY path, did. The ledger's own doc says "Every send is recorded — success or failure, so operators can inspect recent delivery health instead of guessing whether a reply actually left the gateway"; a proactive send is a send. Worse, most proactive call sites DISCARD the result (`let _ = gw.send_to_platform(...).await`), so a failed proactive send was invisible in BOTH the ledger and the log. `send_to_platform` now records every send, and a failure is recorded as `"failed"`. The ledger's own contract now holds: the reply path records the response's content length, and the proactive path records the outcome. Mutation-checked: removing the recording fails `a_proactive_send_is_recorded_in_the_delivery_ledger` ("a proactive send that succeeded must be recorded as delivered: left: 0, right: 1"). This closes the loop on RT-100: the ADAPTERS now report a failed send as a failure, and the LEDGER now records it.

- [x] **RT-102 (M) — five proactive sends DISCARDED their result, so a failed delivery was invisible in the log.** `let _ = gw.send_to_platform(...).await` at five sites in `gateway_runner.rs` — the gateway notice, the error notice, the permission prompt, the question, and the acknowledgement. A permission prompt that could not be delivered leaves the turn blocked forever with no visible cause; an error message that could not be delivered loses the turn's error with it. The delivery ledger now records the outcome (RT-101), but the ledger is not something an operator watches — the LOG is. All five now log the failure with the platform and the error. (The two call sites that PROPAGATE the error — `gw.send_to_platform(...).await?` — were already correct.)

- [x] **RT-103 (S) — the WhatsApp and SMS error messages named a bare status, so the operator could not tell WHY a send failed.** "WhatsApp API error: 400" and "Twilio API error: 400" leave four common causes indistinguishable: an expired token, an unverified phone number, a rejected template, a message outside the 24-hour window, an invalid destination number. Both APIs put the reason in the response body — the Graph API as `{"error": {"message": "...", "code": N}}`, Twilio as `{"code": N, "message": "..."}` — and both bodies were DISCARDED, leaving only the status. The adapters now read the body and name the reason. The body is not echoed raw when it does not parse: it can be large, and it can echo the request. Three tests. (The Email adapter already named the SMTP reply — that was RT-73.)

- [x] **RT-104 (H) — a Telegram PERMISSION PROMPT that failed to send was reported as sent, and the turn waited forever for an approval the user was never shown.** `TelegramAdapter::send_approval_prompt` parsed the response body for `message_id` without checking the status, and returned `Ok(None)` when the id was absent — so a 401 (a revoked token), a 403 (a blocked bot) or a 429 (flood control) all produced "no message id", which the caller reads as "the prompt was never answered". The user saw nothing, the agent waited, and nothing was logged. `send_choice_prompt` was worse: it discarded the response entirely and returned `Ok(())`, so a failed question looked like a delivered one. Both now check the status and fail with a message that says the user was never asked, so the turn cannot proceed. This is the worst instance of the class RT-71/100/103: a permission prompt is the one message the user MUST see, and its failure mode is a silent stall rather than a wrong answer.

- [x] **RT-105 (H) — on Discord and Slack the agent's answer was TRUNCATED to its first chunk, and the rest was never sent.** The streaming path sends the first chunk, remembers the message id, and edits the message for each later chunk. But ONLY Telegram overrides `send_message_return_id` — the default sends the message and returns an EMPTY id, because the platform cannot edit. So on Discord and Slack: the first chunk was delivered, every later chunk's edit was a no-op that reported success, and the delivery marker (recorded with the empty id) told the dispatch loop to SKIP its full send. **The user received the beginning of the answer and never the end.** The fix: an empty id means "this platform cannot edit", so the delivery marker is NOT recorded for it — the dispatch loop then sends the whole answer as a fresh message, and the user gets the complete response. The marker is still recorded for Telegram, whose edit path works, so the duplicate-send suppression it was written for is unchanged.

- [x] **RT-106 (M) — Discord and Slack could not EDIT a message, so the streaming path had no incremental updates.** `PlatformAdapter::edit_message`'s default is a no-op that returns `Ok("")`, and only Telegram overrode it. So the streaming path's edits on Discord and Slack did nothing: the user saw the first chunk of the answer and then the full answer as a second message. RT-105 fixed the TRUNCATION (the delivery marker no longer suppresses the full send for a platform that cannot edit); this iteration gives the two platforms the capability, so the streaming UX is the incremental one they were designed for. Discord now overrides `send_message_return_id` (reading the id from the response body) and `edit_message` (`PATCH /channels/{c}/messages/{m}`); Slack overrides them too (`ts` from the body, and `POST /chat.update`). Both edits check the status — and Slack's `ok: false` body — so a failed edit is not mistaken for a delivered one. Two tests pin the id extraction, which is what the streaming path keys on.

- [x] **RT-107 (M, GAP) — the response-MIRROR feature is entirely inert: there is no way to configure it, and no code to act on it.** `Gateway::mirror_rules` / `with_mirror_rule` / `add_mirror_rule` / `mirror_targets` are a complete `hermes gateway/mirror.py` parity implementation — and **nothing calls them outside their own tests**: `with_mirror_rule` and `add_mirror_rule` have no production caller, `mirror_targets` has none, and there is NO CONFIG KEY for a mirror rule. So the feature cannot be reached at all: an operator cannot turn it on, and if they could, nothing would happen. The doc on `mirror_targets` says "the caller should send the mirrored copies via `send_to_platform`" — and there is no such caller. This is the RT-89 pattern in the gateway: a capability with the mechanism and no wiring. **It is recorded rather than implemented**, because "what should mirroring mean" — which channels, which targets, dedup, rate limits — is a product decision, and a half-wired mirror (a config key with no send path, or a send path with no config key) is worse than none. The owner's call: wire it, or delete it.

- [x] **RT-108 (M) — the delivery ledger was visible NOWHERE.** The ledger is IN-MEMORY in the gateway daemon, and nothing exposes it: `operant gateway status` reads the CONFIG FILE, the daemon writes no status file, and no command reads the ledger. So the counts the ledger was built for — how many sends were delivered, how many failed — were invisible to the operator, which is the entire stated purpose of the ledger ("so operators can inspect recent delivery health instead of guessing"). The gateway now logs a summary — delivered and failed counts — on stop, which is the one moment the operator is guaranteed to see the log. (The full fix would be a status file the daemon writes and `gateway status` reads; that is a feature, and the summary in the log is the part that can be right without one.)

- [x] **RT-109 (H) — a Discord edit over the 2000-character limit TRUNCATED the answer, silently dropping its tail.** iter-1063 added Discord's `edit_message`, and it took `chunk_text(content, 2000).next()` — the FIRST chunk — so an accumulated answer longer than 2000 characters was cut to its first 2000, with no indication that the rest was gone. The streaming path's edit carries the ACCUMULATED text, which grows with every chunk, so this fires on exactly the long answers the streaming path exists for. The API REJECTS an over-limit edit (400) and keeps the old content — so the honest behaviour is to FAIL the edit: the streaming path then drops the delivery marker and the dispatch loop sends the WHOLE answer as a fresh message, and the plain send CHUNKS it correctly. Truncating was the one thing the user must never lose. (RT-105's truncation and RT-106's edit support shipped without a regression test; the three tests for RT-104, RT-105 and this are now in, and each is mutation-checked — reverting its fix fails it.)

- [x] **RT-110 (M) — a Slack message over 40,000 characters was NOT delivered, and a debug print was left in the send path.** Slack's `chat.postMessage` limit is 40,000 characters; over it, Slack answers `{"ok": false, "error": "invalid_text"}` — a 200, so the status check alone does not catch it. The Slack send did not CHUNK the way the Telegram and Discord sends do, so a long answer was silently not delivered. The send now chunks. Separately, the `eprintln!("DEBUG slack api_base = ...")` left in the send path during the RT-100 work was printing the API base on EVERY Slack message — it is removed.

- [x] **RT-111 (M) — a WhatsApp message over 4096 characters was NOT delivered.** WhatsApp's text-message limit is 4096 characters; over it, the Graph API rejects the request and the message never arrives. The WhatsApp send sent the whole body in one request with no chunking, so a long answer was lost — the operator saw an error (RT-103's fix made the error legible) but never the answer. The send now chunks, the way the Telegram, Discord and Slack sends do. (With RT-110, every text-capable adapter now respects its platform's message limit: Telegram 4000, Discord 2000, Slack 40,000, WhatsApp 4096, SMS 1600.) **And the claim needed a correction:** RT-110's own text said 'every text-capable adapter now respects its message limit' while Slack's `send_message_return_id` — the path the STREAMING path uses — still posted the whole body UNCHUNKED, so a long answer on Slack was rejected with `invalid_text` and lost while the plain send delivered it. The published claim was false until iter-1081: `send_message_return_id` now chunks too, and returns the FIRST chunk's `ts` — the message the streaming path edits. The regression test drives the ADAPTER against a counting HTTP server and asserts how many messages the body was sent as, because a test that asserted on the chunking HELPER would pass against the pre-fix code — the helper was called, just not by the path that mattered. (The mock needs a per-instance `api_base` rather than the process-global runtime config, which `runtime_config()` is read from ~20 call sites — mutating it in a test would leak the mock base into every other test in the process.) Mutation-checked: reverting the chunking in `send_message_return_id` fails the test — "an over-limit body must be sent as more than one message, got 1".

- [x] **RT-112 (M) — an SMS over 1600 characters was NOT delivered.** Twilio's message-body limit is 1,600 characters; over it, Twilio answers with a 400 and the message never arrives. The SMS send sent the whole body in one request with no chunking. The send now chunks, the way every other text-capable adapter does. (With RT-110/111, all five text-capable adapters respect their platform's limit: Telegram 4000, Discord 2000, Slack 40,000, WhatsApp 4096, SMS 1600.)

- [x] **RT-113 (M) — the Telegram VOICE send hard-coded the API base, so voice messages went to the real Telegram API while everything else went to the configured one.** `send_voice` built its URL as `https://api.telegram.org/bot{token}/sendVoice` — the only one of the ten Telegram call sites that did not use `self.api_url()`. A deployment that points the Telegram API elsewhere (a test, a proxy, a regional endpoint) had its voice messages delivered to the REAL Telegram API, with the configured base ignored, while every other message went to the configured one. The voice send now uses `api_url()` like the other nine. **And the fix has a trap worth recording:** `api_url()` ALREADY includes the `/bot{token}` segment, so the first attempt — `{api_url()}/bot{token}/sendVoice` — produced `.../bot123/bot123/sendVoice` and would have 404'd for every voice message, which is WORSE than the hard-coded base it replaced (and no test covered `send_voice`, so the suite stayed green either way). The URL is now built by a named `voice_url()` and a test pins the shape: mutation-checked — doubling the bot segment fails it ("left: 2, right: 1").

- [x] **RT-114 (M) — the WhatsApp and Twilio API bases were HARD-CODED, so a deployment behind a proxy could not reach them at all.** Telegram, Discord and Slack each have a configurable API base (`telegram_api_base`, `discord_api_base`, `slack_api_base`); the WhatsApp adapter built its URL from a literal `https://graph.facebook.com/v18.0/...` and the Twilio adapter from a literal `https://api.twilio.com/2010-04-01/...`. A deployment that must route through a proxy, or use a regional endpoint, could configure the other three and not these two. WhatsApp now has `whatsapp_api_base` (in the gateway config, with the example) and Twilio has `TWILIO_API_BASE` (an environment variable, like its other credentials — the adapter reads them all from the environment).

- [x] **RT-115 (M) — the MCP SSE client's TOTAL timeout cut the long-lived stream every 300 seconds.** One `reqwest::Client` served both the SSE stream (a long-lived GET that the server pushes server-initiated requests over, and which has no natural end) and the POSTs (bounded tool calls). The client's 300-second total timeout therefore ended the STREAM every five minutes, so the server had to re-establish it five times an hour — and a server that does not expect that breaks. The MCP SSE spec assumes a long-lived stream. The SSE client now has two clients: the stream client has NO total timeout, and the POST client keeps the 300-second one. **And the first version of this fix was incomplete, in two ways:** it claimed the reader has a 10-second per-event timeout — it does NOT. The 10 seconds bounds the wait for the server's ENDPOINT EVENT during connect, not the events on the stream; a stream that goes quiet is not detected. And because the split REMOVED the client's only timeout, `connect()` became UNBOUNDED: a server that accepted the TCP connection and never answered would hang the connect forever. The stream client now has a 30-second `connect_timeout` — which covers the TCP connect and the response headers — so the CONNECTION is bounded while the STREAM is not. A tool call is still bounded at 300 seconds.

- [x] **RT-116 (M) — the output fitter sized `max_tokens` from a STALE prompt count, so a request could exceed the window by exactly the tool output accumulated since the last request.** `fit_output_tokens` exists to stop a request whose `prompt + max_tokens` exceeds the window. It sized the cap from `estimate_current_tokens`, which PREFERS the provider's last REPORTED prompt count — exact, but exact for the PREVIOUS request. Every tool result appended since was missing from it, and the fitter SKIPS its safety margin for a reported count ("reported is exact"). So `room = window - stale_prompt - headway` was too large by the accumulated tool output, `max_tokens` came out too large, and the request could exceed the window by that much — the 400 the fitter exists to prevent. The fix is in ONE place: `estimate_current_tokens` now returns the LARGER of the current estimate and the reported count, so EVERY caller sees at least the local estimate rather than a stale, possibly-smaller number — the output fit, the compression threshold, and the pre-flight guard. (The output fit additionally always applies the margin, because a reported count is exact for a request that is not the one being sent.) The dead `prefer_reported` helper went with it — it had no other caller. Mutation-checked: restoring the 'prefer the reported count' rule fails `the_token_estimate_is_the_larger_of_the_heuristic_and_the_reported_count`. (The current estimate counts the tool-call arguments and the reasoning since iter-1030, so it does not undercount the tool payload either.) **The residual, stated honestly:** `max()` can still SELECT the reported count when the provider's tokenizer counts more than chars/4 — which it does for code — so the fit is sized against the previous request's number in that case, and the two numbers are not comparable (one is a provider-tokenizer count, the other a char heuristic). The fix makes the fit never SMALLER than the local estimate; it does not make it exactly the current prompt. Calibrating the local estimate by the provider's own ratio, or dropping the reported count from the fit, is the remaining work.

- [x] **RT-117 (M) — the dashboard's SERVED `/api/status` omits the fields the bundled frontend reads, so the system panel renders empty.** FIXED iter-1110 by serving the shape, which is the half of the RT-44 decision that needs no bundle replacement. `/api/status` now carries `cpu_count`, `cpu_percent`, `load_avg`, `memory{used,total,percent}`, `disk{used,total,percent}`, `hostname`, `os_release`, `arch`, `psutil` and `hermes_version` alongside the fields it already served. Three details are load-bearing and were read OFF the bundle, not guessed: `memory.used`/`disk.used` are BYTES (the bundle formats them with its own B/KB/MB/GB helper — a MB value renders "0.0 KB"), `load_avg` is the 1/5/15 ARRAY (a scalar renders nothing at all), and `psutil` is the flag that hides the bundle's "install the psutil extra" note, so it is set from whether the metrics were actually read, not from a constant. `cpu_percent` is measured between two status polls (a since-boot average never moves on a long-running host), so the FIRST poll after startup reports `null`. Reads are `/proc/stat`, `/proc/meminfo`, `getloadavg`, `statvfs("/")` and `gethostname` — no dependency added, no blocking call off-thread (they are file reads, microseconds). `update_available` is deliberately NOT served: the panel reads that from its separate update-check call (which 404s — RT-44's family), not from `/api/status`. Proof: `dashboard_server::tests::the_status_payload_carries_every_field_the_system_panel_reads` reads the BUNDLE and the SERIALIZER and fails if either side drifts (mutation-checked: removing `#[serde(flatten)]` fails it), and `the_host_metrics_are_real_on_linux` asserts the collector returns real bytes on this host. Verified end to end against a live dashboard: `curl /api/status` returned `cpu_count 24, load_avg [17.95,20.42,26.27], memory 52.3%, disk 93.9%, hostname "omarchy"`, and a second poll returned `cpu_percent 63.3`.

- [x] **RT-118 (S) — `operant channel list` omitted SMS.** The gateway's registry has SEVEN adapters (Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp, Email, SMS, Webhooks) and the command's list showed six — so an operator with `sms_twilio_enabled = true` saw no SMS in the channel list, and the "is my channel configured" view disagreed with the gateway's own registry. SMS is now in the list. (The list is a SEPARATE surface from the gateway's status report, which RT-86 fixed to carry the inbound truth for the four outbound-only adapters — so the two surfaces had drifted: the status report knew which channels can receive, and the channel list did not list SMS at all.)

- [x] **RT-119 (S) — `operant channel doctor` reports "healthy" for a channel it never probed.** FIXED iter-1090 (closed here in iter-1109 after re-reading the code): the JSON no longer has a `healthy` field at all. It carries `enabled`, `configured` (the SAME flag, named so the value is not mistaken for a probe result), `health: "not probed"` — a literal that cannot be read as a verdict — and `inbound: c.can_receive()`, the gateway registry's real answer for the four outbound-only adapters (RT-86). The human-readable path prints the same truth: an enabled send-only channel is labelled "send-only (no inbound)" instead of a tick. That is the minimum the row asked for ("it should not report healthy for a channel it did not check"); the residual is the one the row named — the doctor still does not PROBE, so an enabled channel with a bad token still shows a tick, and it says `health: "not probed"` rather than implying otherwise. A real probe stays an open design decision (one network call per channel), not a defect.

- [x] **RT-120 (M) — the `PlatformAdapter::handle_update` trait method is DEAD: nothing calls it for any adapter.** It is a REQUIRED trait method, so every one of the seven adapters implements a complete inbound parser — and no production code path invokes it. The platforms that receive use their own route instead: Telegram's and Discord's poll loops, and the webhook adapter's own HTTP handler. The four that do NOT receive — Slack, WhatsApp, Email, SMS — have an inbound parser that nothing reaches, so a message sent to them is never seen by the agent. The inbound table in the gateway (`INBOUND_CAPABLE_PLATFORMS`) is the single source of that truth, and `operant gateway status` now prints it. **The trait method itself is still dead** — deleting it, or wiring it, is the same decision as RT-89's (implement Socket Mode for Slack, and the provider webhooks for WhatsApp/Email/SMS). Recorded so the next reader does not assume the parsers are live.

- [x] **RT-121 (M) — `operant channel add` did not say the channel it added cannot receive.** The command printed "Added slack channel and persisted the config" and nothing else, so an operator who configured Slack believed the agent would see Slack messages — and it never would (RT-86: Slack's inbound parser is never called). The command now says so at the moment of configuration: "NOTE: slack is SEND-ONLY — operant can post to it, but messages sent to it never reach the agent", and its JSON carries `"inbound": false`. The truth comes from the gateway's single inbound table (iter-1087), so the note, the status report and the adapters' own reports cannot disagree. **And the WRITE path was missing too**: the supported set had no "sms", and it listed the platform key as "webhooks" (plural) where the platform key is "webhook" — the CONFIG FIELD is `webhooks_enabled`, but the KEY the command writes and matches on is the singular one. So `operant channel add sms` was REJECTED, and `operant channel add webhook` — the correct spelling — was rejected as unsupported while `channel add webhooks` wrote a key the config does not have. The plural is now an alias that normalises to the canonical key, and SMS is configurable. Three more false claims in the same file: the doctor reported `"healthy"` for a channel it never probed (now `"configured"` and `"health": "not probed"`), and `channel add` told the operator to "Run `operant channel doctor` to verify connectivity" — a command that does not reach the platform. `operant channel list` and `operant channel doctor` print the same column, so the three surfaces agree. (Both look the table up by the platform KEY — the display label is "Webhooks" and the key is "webhook", so looking it up by the label silently missed the one platform that CAN receive. The CLI row is not a platform, so the column does not apply to it.)

- [x] **RT-122 (M) — the STREAMING send did not record in the delivery ledger, so every streamed answer was invisible in the operator's delivery-health view.** The ledger exists so the operator can see whether replies actually left the gateway, and its own doc says "Every send is recorded — success or failure". The PLAIN send (`send_to_platform`) has always recorded. The STREAMING path (`send_message_return_id`) did not: so the answers the agent streamed to a platform were absent from the counts while the proactive sends around them were present — the two paths disagreed about the same thing, and the summary the gateway prints on stop under-reported. Fixed: the streaming path records, with the status taken from the OUTCOME (a failed send is recorded as failed, not as a success). The regression test drives the streaming path for both outcomes — mutation-checked: removing the record call fails it with "left: 0, right: 1".

- [x] **RT-123 (M) — the TUI's `/mcp` pane never painted a streamable-HTTP server, and counted it as scrolled-off.** The pane groups the servers by transport, and the buckets were "stdio", "sse" and "http". The label is the Debug of the config's `McpTransportKind` lowercased, and that enum has THREE variants — Http, Stdio and StreamableHttp — so a server configured with `transport = "streamable-http"` was in NO group and was never painted at all. Worse: the "N more" count compared the number shown against EVERY server, so the invisible one was counted as scrolled-off — the pane said "1 more" for a server it had no group for. The "sse" bucket was dead in the other direction: the enum has no SSE variant, so it never matched anything. Fixed: the buckets are the enum's three variants, the dead "sse" bucket is gone, and the test is over the enum's variants — mutation-checked: removing the streamable bucket fails it with "the groups carry 2 of 3".

- [x] **RT-124 (S) — `operant mcp add` cannot create a streamable-HTTP server.** FIXED iter-1109: `operant mcp add` takes `--transport <http|streamable-http|stdio>`. Omitted, it keeps the historical inference (`--url` means plain HTTP, `--command` means stdio), so every existing invocation behaves as before; named, it selects the transport the config and the client already support, and an unknown value is REJECTED rather than silently falling back (a typo meant the server was added on the wrong transport and the connect error blamed the server). `--transport stdio` requires `--command`, `--transport http|streamable-http` requires `--url`; the connection-test line and the printed `[[mcp.servers]]` snippet now name the transport actually used instead of always saying `transport = "http"`. Proof: `cmd_mcp::tests::the_streamable_transport_is_selectable` fails if the streamable arm is removed from `parse_transport`, and `operant mcp add --help` lists the flag.

- [x] **RT-125 (S) — `docs/handoff/STATE.md` can never pass the genome doctor's own staleness check.** FIXED iter-1111 in the vendored tool (`scripts/genome`), and mirrored into the skill copy so `genome upgrade` is a no-op rather than a silent revert. The check compared the doc's first 40-hex sha against HEAD; the handoff WRITES HEAD and the commit carrying it moves HEAD, so the doc was stale the instant it landed and no amount of regenerating converged. The rule is now: a snapshot stops describing the tree only when a commit AFTER it changed something other than the documentation roles — every path in the range must live under `docs/` or be a configured role path (state, archive, worklog, ledger, plans, audit, AD/RG), so the routine close-out commit (snapshot + worklog + ledger + archive row, all at once) leaves the check GREEN. Anything narrower would have kept it red for exactly the commit it exists to verify. Two messages replace the one: a genuinely stale doc now says how far it is and where ('names 24939ee5 and 9 commit(s) since changed more than it (latest bed89862, HEAD is bed89862)'), and a sha outside the history (rebase, shallow clone) says so instead of pretending. Proof: at iter-1111 HEAD the pre-patch check was red naming 24939ee5; after the patch it names the 9 real commits; and the doc-only commit that follows this one leaves `genome doctor` healthy — the first time the check has ever converged. Residual: the doc is checked against commits, not against uncommitted work, so a long dirty session is still invisible to it.



- [x] **RT-126 (S) — the message-delete capability was unreachable, and the one implementation that existed reported success it never checked.** FIXED iter-1113, in two parts that belong together. (1) The path: `Gateway::delete_message_on_platform` and `operant channel delete-message --channel-id <p> --recipient <chat> --message-id <id>` (JSON with `--json`, exit code 1 when the deletion did not happen, so a script cannot mistake a refusal for a retraction) now reach the adapter. It rides the same two transports as `channel send`: the live gateway when the service is running, a one-shot gateway otherwise — `deleteMessage` is a stateless API call. (2) The honesty: the trait method was `fn` (not `async`), so Telegram's implementation had to `tokio::spawn` the request and return `Ok(())` before the platform answered — a bot without delete rights, or a message too old to retract, produced a logged line and a successful-looking return. It is `async fn` now, awaited, with the HTTP status checked and the adapter's error surfaced to the caller. The DEFAULT now REFUSES ("<platform> does not support deleting messages") instead of returning `Ok(())`: a platform that cannot delete must not report a deletion it never performed — that default was the same defect in latent form, one caller away. Proof: `the_gateway_reaches_the_adapters_delete_and_reports_its_failure` drives the GATEWAY with a recording adapter and asserts the call arrives with both ids, that an unknown platform errors, and that an adapter failure surfaces (mutation-checked: returning `Ok(())` from the gateway method fails it with `left: []`); `an_adapter_without_delete_support_refuses_instead_of_reporting_success` pins the default. End to end against a fake Bot API: `channel delete-message` sent `POST /bot<token>/deleteMessage {"chat_id":"42","message_id":"7"}` and printed `status: deleted` with exit 0; the same command against a server answering 400 printed `Telegram deleteMessage failed: HTTP 400 Bad Request (the message was NOT deleted)` and exited 1. Residual: only Telegram implements delete, so the other six adapters return the refusal — that is now the honest answer, not a silent success.


- [x] **RT-129 (S) — the platform-base ENV overrides reached the agent's tools but not the gateway adapters, so the two disagreed exactly when the override was set.** FIXED iter-1114: the adapters now read the same owner (`tools/platform_bases::telegram_api_base()` / `discord_` / `slack_` / `whatsapp_`), six call sites in all — Telegram's `api_url()`, its `getUpdates` base and its media base, Discord's `api_url()`, Slack's `api_base()` (a per-instance override still wins, then the env, then the config) and WhatsApp's send URL. The module doc told the truth about the intent and a lie about the state; it now states the state. Proof: `the_adapter_api_base_honours_the_environment_override` builds a real `TelegramAdapter` with `TELEGRAM_API_BASE=http://127.0.0.1:9/tg/` and asserts `api_url()` is `http://127.0.0.1:9/tg/bot123:abc` (mutation-checked: reverting the site to `runtime_config().gateway.telegram_api_base` fails it); the full gateway suite is green (118); and end to end, with the config still pointing at `https://api.telegram.org`, `TELEGRAM_API_BASE=http://127.0.0.1:3998/ operant channel delete-message` reached the fake server (`POST /bot<token>/deleteMessage`) and printed `status: deleted` — the exact command that got a 401 from the real API when the config was the only lever. Residual: the media-download base in `telegram.rs` copies `base` at loop start (unchanged behaviour), so a mid-process env change still needs a restart; and the SMS adapter reads `TWILIO_API_BASE` directly (its own precedent, same semantics). Found while proving RT-126 (iter-1113).





- [x] **RT-128 (M) — the agent's TOOLS hard-coded their API bases, so a proxy deployment could reach a platform through the gateway but not through the agent.** RT-113 and RT-114 made the ADAPTERS' bases configurable, and the rows say so — but the agent's TOOLS had their own literals: `send_message_tool.rs` posted to `https://api.telegram.org`, `https://discord.com/api/v10` and `https://slack.com/api`; `reaction_tool.rs` to the same three; and `discord_tool.rs` carried a `DISCORD_API_BASE` constant. Two more sites had the same defect outside the tools: the gateway's boot-time `setMyCommands` (which registers the command menu) went to the REAL Telegram API while every message went to the configured endpoint, and the doctor's Telegram probe did the same. So a deployment that had to reach a platform through a proxy (the case `telegram_proxy` exists for) could send messages through the gateway and could not send them through the agent's tools, could not react, and could not register its command menu. Fixed: one owner — `operant_core::tools::platform_bases` reads the SAME runtime config the adapters read, so the two cannot disagree, and the bases are trimmed of a trailing `/` because the callers append their own path. Every hard-coded base is gone from `crates/operant-core/src/tools/` and `crates/operant-cli/src/`. Each base also has an environment override that WINS over the config — `TELEGRAM_API_BASE`, `DISCORD_API_BASE`, `SLACK_API_BASE`, `WHATSAPP_API_BASE` (the SMS adapter's Twilio base is `TWILIO_API_BASE`) — so a container can point the gateway AND the agent's tools at a proxy without rewriting the config file; the keys are documented in `operant.example.toml`. Mutation-checked: ignoring the override fails the test with "left: https://api.telegram.org, right: https://proxy.example/telegram". The boot-time `setMyCommands` client now carries the adapter's `telegram_proxy` too, so the menu registration reaches the base AND the transport the adapter uses; an invalid proxy value is logged and ignored rather than failing the boot.

- [x] **S1 (OSC-8 / pinned-image post-draw writes)** — CLEARED by a live terminal check (iter-1139). `osc8::emit_hits` and `pinned_images::emit_strip` run after `terminal.draw` and write cells outside ratatui's diff; a width or position disagreement leaves ghosts until a scroll rewrites them.

  **The method.** A live TUI session in a 200×50 tmux pane (tmux 3.8), the operator's real config and model, with a transcript TALLER than the viewport (a 100-line reply), then PageUp (scroll up) and PageDown (scroll back), with `capture-pane -p` before and after. The post-scroll case — the one iter-978 explicitly did NOT exercise — is the whole point: a ghost is a cell the scroll left behind, so it can only appear in the frame that comes back.

  **The result.** The frame after scrolling back is BYTE-IDENTICAL to the pre-scroll frame in every row except one: the live `⏱ N tok/s` counter (11 tok/s vs 9 tok/s), which is a wall-clock metric the row itself excludes. No fragment of the scrolled-past content, no half-drawn border, no residue of the previous frame. The borders of the `Overview` panel are at the same cells before and after (160 and 199 of a 200-cell pane). S1 is cleared.

  **The limit of the claim.** The check observes the TERMINAL's grid (tmux's), not the app's internal buffer: it proves that what the terminal ends up showing is correct, which is what the operator sees. It does not prove the two agree cell-for-cell in every case — that is the same limit S2 carries below.
- [ ] **S2 (Ambiguous/Wide glyph widths)** — PARTIALLY cleared (iter-1139): the AMBIGUOUS class is cleared by a live wide-terminal check; the WIDE (emoji) class is still unproven, and again not reproduced.
  `○ ⛭ ⚡ ⏳ 🐝 🧠 📣 ⚠(+VS16)` disagree between unicode-width and real terminals;
  shifted cells leave fragments of the previous frame.

  **The AMBIGUOUS class is CLEARED.** The live 200-column session rendered every ambiguous-width glyph the TUI uses — `●` (provider dots), `✗`/`✓` (tool rows), `›` (the prompt), `▸` (thought rows), `⚑` (advisor rows), `⏱` (the rate counter), `⇧` (the shift hint), `…` (the path ellipsis), `·` (the separator) and the whole `╭ ─ │ ╮ ╰ ╯` box set — and every one of them lands in exactly ONE cell with the columns aligned: the two tool rows' labels both start at cell 5 (`   ✗ bash ·` / `   ✓ aft_bash ·`), the provider dots are at cell 1 in both rows, the `Overview` box's right border is at cell 199 of the 200-cell pane in every row, and the footer panel's borders are at 160 and 199. That is the wide-terminal case iter-978 could not exercise.

  **The WIDE class is still open, and still unreproduced.** The emoji DID render in that session (the model's reply `A🐝B✅C⚠️D` is in the captured frame, and the characters after each emoji are present and in order), so the width disagreement the row describes did not produce a visible artifact here. What is missing is a cell-for-cell comparison against the app's OWN buffer: the row asks for the F12 `d` buffer dump, and F12 did not open the dump in this build, so the check is one-sided.
